%% =========================================================
%% Chapter: G-injective PEPS and quantum double models
%% =========================================================

\chapter{G-injective PEPS and quantum double models}%
\label{ch:peps_quantum_double}

A tensor that is invariant under a representation of a finite group $G$ on its
virtual legs cannot be injective; at best it is injective on the invariant
subspace. Such $G$-injective tensors describe the quantum double models of
\cite{Kitaev2003FaultTolerant}, whose simplest instance, for
$G=\mathbb{Z}_2$, is the toric code. This chapter develops them on the torus
PEPS of Section~\ref{sec:peps_torus_site_tensor}: the closure of
$G$-injectivity under contraction of regions, the ground spaces of regular
$G$-injective regions, and the reduced densities and entropies of their
physical cuts. The last two sections compare the physical ranges of
$G$-injective tensors with the invariant virtual spaces that produce them.

\section{The toric code and quantum double models}%
\label{sec:peps_quantum_double}

Let $G$ be a finite group. The quantum double model of $G$
\cite{Kitaev2003FaultTolerant} carries a spin with basis
$\{\ket{g}\}_{g\in G}$ on every edge of a square lattice; for
$G=\mathbb{Z}_2$ it is the toric code. The review gives two PEPS
representations \cite[Appendix~A, ``The Toric Code and quantum double
    models'']{Cirac2021Matrix}. Their symmetries are phrased through the map
$\mathcal{P}(A)=\sum A^i_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\ket{i}\bra{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}$
from the virtual to the physical system
\cite[Section~``MPS and PEPS'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.

\begin{definition}[$G$-injective and $G$-isometric tensors]%
    \label{def:peps_g_injective}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.IsGInjective, TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric,
        TNLean.PEPS.siteMap, TNLean.PEPS.siteMap_apply}
    \leanok
    Let $g\mapsto U_g$ be a representation of a finite group $G$ on the
    virtual system of a tensor $A$, and let $\Pi_U$ be the projector onto the
    $U_g$-invariant subspace. The tensor is $G$-injective if
    $\mathcal{P}(A)U_g=\mathcal{P}(A)$ for every $g$ and $\mathcal{P}(A)$ is
    injective on the invariant subspace
    \cite[Section~``Two dimensions: $G$-injective PEPS'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.
    It is $G$-isometric if moreover
    $\mathcal{P}(A)$ restricted to the invariant subspace is a multiple of an
    isometry, $\langle\mathcal{P}(A)x,\mathcal{P}(A)y\rangle
        =c\,\langle x,y\rangle$ for invariant $x,y$ and one constant $c>0$
    \cite[Section~``Isometric PEPS'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Left-inverse form of $G$-injectivity]%
    \label{thm:peps_g_injective_left_inverse}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.apply_averageMap_of_forall_comp_eq,
        TNLean.PEPS.isGInjective_iff_exists_leftInverse}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_g_injective}
    A tensor is $G$-injective if and only if
    $\mathcal{P}(A)U_g=\mathcal{P}(A)$ for every $g$ and $\mathcal{P}(A)$ has a
    left inverse $L$ with $L\,\mathcal{P}(A)=\Pi_U$
    \cite[Section~``Two dimensions: $G$-injective PEPS'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    An invariant map satisfies $\mathcal{P}(A)\Pi_U=\mathcal{P}(A)$, since
    $\Pi_U$ is the average of the $U_g$. If $\mathcal{P}(A)$ is injective on
    the invariant subspace, choose a linear map $L$ into the invariant
    subspace with $L\,\mathcal{P}(A)x=x$ for invariant $x$; then
    $L\,\mathcal{P}(A)=L\,\mathcal{P}(A)\Pi_U=\Pi_U$. Conversely, if
    $L\,\mathcal{P}(A)=\Pi_U$ and $\mathcal{P}(A)x=0$ for an invariant $x$, then
    $x=\Pi_Ux=L\,\mathcal{P}(A)x=0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[$G$-injectivity for the trivial representation]%
    \label{thm:peps_g_injective_trivial}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.isGInjective_trivial_iff,
        TNLean.PEPS.siteMap_injective_iff}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_g_injective}
    For the trivial representation, $G$-injectivity is injectivity of
    $\mathcal{P}(A)$, that is, linear independence of the physical vectors
    $A^{\bullet}_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}$ indexed by the virtual
    configurations.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Every vector is invariant under the trivial representation, and
    $\mathcal{P}(A)$ is the linear map whose columns are the vectors
    $A^{\bullet}_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}$.
\end{proof}

The group-theoretic input to $G$-injectivity is the averaging over the group
and an operator $\Delta$ that inverts the expansion of an operator in the
$U_g$'s \cite[Section~``MPS: the $G$-injective case'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.
Throughout, $g\mapsto U_g$ is a representation of a finite group $G$ on a
finite-dimensional complex space.

\begin{theorem}[Group averaging]%
    \label{thm:peps_group_averaging}
    \lean{Representation.averageMap_linHom_apply,
        Representation.mem_invariants_linHom_iff,
        Representation.isProj_averageMap_linHom,
        Representation.averageMap_linHom_apply_of_adjoint,
        Representation.trace_adjoint_comp_averageMap_linHom}
    \leanok
    The map $\sigma(X)=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_g U_gXU_g^{-1}$ is a projection onto
    the operators commuting with every $U_g$. If the representation is unitary,
    then $\sigma(X)=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_g U_gXU_g^\dagger$ and
    $\tr[Y^\dagger\sigma(X)]=\tr[\sigma(Y)^\dagger X]$, so $\sigma$ is the
    orthogonal projector onto these operators for the Hilbert--Schmidt inner
    product \cite[Section~``MPS: the $G$-injective case'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    The map $\sigma$ is the average of the conjugation action
    $X\mapsto U_gXU_g^{-1}$, so it is a projection onto the operators fixed
    by every conjugation, and these are the operators commuting with every
    $U_g$. For a unitary representation,
    $\tr[Y^\dagger U_gXU_g^\dagger]=\tr[(U_g^\dagger YU_g)^\dagger X]$, and
    summing over $g$, with $g$ replaced by $g^{-1}$, gives the symmetry of
    $\sigma$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Isotypic projectors and the operator $\Delta$]%
    \label{def:peps_delta_operator}
    \lean{Representation.charProjector,
        Representation.irreducibleCharacters,
        Representation.characterMultiplicity,
        Representation.deltaOperator}
    \leanok
    Let $\chi_U$ be the character of $U$, and let $\chi_1,\dots,\chi_r$ be the
    distinct characters of the irreducible subrepresentations of $U$, with
    $d_i=\chi_i(1)$ and multiplicities
    $m_i=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_g\chi_U(g)\chi_i(g^{-1})$. The isotypic projectors
    are $P_i=\frac{d_i}{|G|}\sum_g\chi_i(g^{-1})U_g$, and
    \begin{align*}
        \Delta=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\frac{d_i}{m_i}P_i
    \end{align*}
    \cite[Section~``MPS: the $G$-injective case'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Isotypic projectors from characters]%
    \label{thm:peps_isotypic_projectors}
    \lean{Representation.sum_character_inv_smul_eq,
        Representation.exists_isInternal_isAtom,
        Representation.charProjector_apply_of_mem,
        Representation.irreducibleCharacters_finite,
        Representation.characterMultiplicity_eq_finrank,
        Representation.sum_charProjector_irreducibleCharacters,
        Representation.trace_comp_charProjector}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_delta_operator}
    For irreducible representations $D^i$ and $D^j$ of $G$,
    $\sum_g\chi_i(g^{-1})D^j(g)$ equals $\frac{|G|}{d_i}\Id$ if $D^i$
    and $D^j$ are equivalent and $0$ otherwise. Consequently $P_i$ acts as
    the identity on every irreducible subrepresentation of $U$ with
    character $\chi_i$ and as zero on every other irreducible
    subrepresentation, the number $m_i$ is the dimension of the space of
    intertwining maps from $D^i$ to $U$, and
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_{i=1}^{r}P_i & =\Id, & \tr[U_kP_i] & =m_i\chi_i(k)
    \end{align*}
    \cite[Section~``MPS: the $G$-injective case'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    The operator $\sum_g\chi_i(g^{-1})D^j(g)$ commutes with every $D^j(h)$,
    because characters are constant on conjugacy classes, so by Schur's lemma
    it is a multiple of the identity; its trace is
    $\sum_g\chi_i(g^{-1})\chi_j(g)$, which orthogonality of characters
    evaluates. By Maschke's theorem $U$ is a direct sum of irreducible
    subrepresentations. On each summand the projectors act as just
    described, which gives $\sum_iP_i=\Id$, and the trace of
    $U_kP_i$ is the sum of $\chi_i(k)$ over the summands with character
    $\chi_i$. Orthogonality of characters counts these summands as $m_i$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Block form of $\Delta$]%
    \label{thm:peps_delta_block_form}
    \lean{Representation.deltaOperator_apply_of_mem}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_delta_operator}
    On every irreducible subrepresentation of $U$ with character $\chi_i$,
    $\Delta$ acts as the scalar $\frac{d_i}{m_i|G|}$. Thus, in a basis in which
    $U_g\cong\bigoplus_iD^i(g)\otimes\Id_{m_i}$,
    \begin{align*}
        \Delta\cong\frac{1}{|G|}\bigoplus_i\frac{d_i}{m_i}\,
        \Id_{d_i}\otimes\Id_{m_i}
    \end{align*}
    \cite[Section~``MPS: the $G$-injective case'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_isotypic_projectors}
    Only the projector of $\chi_i$ acts nontrivially on such a
    subrepresentation, and it acts as the identity.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Expansion in the representation]%
    \label{thm:peps_delta_expansion}
    \lean{Representation.trace_comp_deltaOperator,
        Representation.sum_trace_comp_deltaOperator_smul,
        Representation.sum_trace_inv_comp_deltaOperator_comp_smul}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_delta_operator}
    For every $g\in G$,
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_h\tr[U_{h^{-1}}\Delta U_g]\,U_h
        =\sum_h\tr[U_{gh^{-1}}\Delta]\,U_h=U_g .
    \end{align*}
    For a unitary representation $U_{h^{-1}}=U_h^\dagger$
    \cite[Section~``MPS: the $G$-injective case'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_isotypic_projectors}
    By the trace formula for the projectors,
    $\tr[U_k\Delta]=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_i d_i\chi_i(k)$, so
    $\sum_k\tr[U_{k^{-1}}\Delta]U_k=\sum_iP_i=\Id$. Substituting
    $h=kg$ gives
    $\sum_h\tr[U_{gh^{-1}}\Delta]U_h=\big(\sum_k\tr[U_{k^{-1}}\Delta]U_k\big)U_g
        =U_g$, and the first expression equals the second by cyclicity of
    the trace.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Semi-regular representation]%
    \label{def:peps_semiregular}
    \lean{Representation.IsSemiRegular,
        Representation.isSemiRegular_leftRegular}
    \leanok
    A representation of $G$ is semi-regular if it contains every irreducible
    representation of $G$, that is, if every irreducible representation
    admits a nonzero intertwining map into it
    \cite[Section~``MPS: the $G$-injective case'']{Schuch2010PEPS}. The
    left-regular representation is semi-regular.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Semi-regularity under representation equivalence]%
    \label{thm:peps_semiregular_equivalence}
    \lean{Representation.IsSemiRegular.of_equiv,
        Representation.Equiv.isSemiRegular_iff}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_semiregular}
    Equivalent representations $\rho$ and $\sigma$ are semi-regular
    simultaneously. Thus the occurrence condition of
    \cite[Definition~4.5]{Schuch2010PEPS} is independent of coordinates.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    If $e$ intertwines $\rho$ with $\sigma$ and is invertible, every
    nonzero intertwiner $f$ from an irreducible representation into
    $\rho$ gives a nonzero intertwiner $ef$ into $\sigma$. Apply the
    same argument to $e^{-1}$ for the converse.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Linear independence of a semi-regular representation]%
    \label{thm:peps_semiregular_trace}
    \lean{Representation.character_leftRegular,
        Representation.deltaOperator_leftRegular,
        Representation.sum_irreducibleCharacters_mul_of_isSemiRegular,
        Representation.trace_inv_comp_comp_deltaOperator_of_isSemiRegular}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_delta_operator, def:peps_semiregular}
    If $U$ is semi-regular, then $\tr[U_{g^{-1}}U_h\Delta]=\delta_{g,h}$ for
    all $g,h\in G$, with $U_{g^{-1}}=U_g^\dagger$ for a unitary
    representation. For the left-regular representation
    $\Delta=\frac{1}{|G|}\Id$
    \cite[Section~``MPS: the $G$-injective case'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_delta_expansion, thm:peps_isotypic_projectors}
    The character of the left-regular representation is $|G|\delta_{k,1}$,
    so every character $\chi_i$ has multiplicity $d_i$ in it, and
    $\Delta=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_iP_i=\frac{1}{|G|}\Id$. Every
    irreducible representation occurs in the left-regular representation,
    and, since $U$ is semi-regular, in $U$; hence $U$ and the left-regular
    representation have the same irreducible characters. Comparing the two
    expressions for $\tr[U_k\Delta]$ in the left-regular representation gives
    $\sum_id_i\chi_i(k)=|G|\delta_{k,1}$, and therefore
    $\tr[U_{g^{-1}}U_h\Delta]=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_id_i\chi_i(g^{-1}h)
        =\delta_{g,h}$.
\end{proof}

\input{chapter/ch24_peps_semiregular_group_algebra}

\begin{theorem}[Tensor products of semi-regular representations]%
    \label{thm:peps_semiregular_tensor_product}
    \lean{Representation.isIrreducible_of_finrank_eq_one,
        Representation.IsSemiRegular.exists_mem_invariants_ne_zero,
        Representation.IsSemiRegular.tprod_of_mem_invariants,
        Representation.IsSemiRegular.tprod}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_semiregular}
    If $U$ and $V$ are semi-regular representations of $G$, then so is
    $U\otimes V$
    \cite[Section~``Two dimensions: $G$-injective PEPS'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    A one-dimensional representation is irreducible, so the semi-regular
    representation $V$ contains the trivial representation and has a nonzero
    invariant vector $v$. If $D$ is irreducible and $f$ is a nonzero
    intertwining map from $D$ to $U$, then $x\mapsto f(x)\otimes v$ is a
    nonzero intertwining map from $D$ to $U\otimes V$.
\end{proof}

A matrix product tensor is a family of operators $A^i$ on a bond space
carrying the representation $U$. Its virtual system is the space of
operators $X$ on the bond space, on which $G$ acts by conjugation
$X\mapsto U_gXU_g^{-1}$, and identifying the virtual vector
$\sum x_{\alpha\beta}\ket{\alpha,\beta}$ with the operator
$X=\sum x_{\alpha\beta}\ket{\beta}\bra{\alpha}$ gives
$\mathcal{P}(A)X=\sum_i\tr[A^iX]\ket{i}$
\cite[Section~``MPS and PEPS'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.

\begin{definition}[$G$-injective matrix product tensor]%
    \label{def:peps_mps_g_injective}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.mpsSiteMap, TNLean.PEPS.mpsSiteMap_apply}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_g_injective}
    A matrix product tensor $A$ is $G$-injective if the map
    $\mathcal{P}(A)X=\sum_i\tr[A^iX]\ket{i}$ is $G$-injective for the
    conjugation action of $U$ on the operators on the bond space.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[$G$-injective matrix product tensors]%
    \label{thm:peps_mps_g_injective_source_form}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.mpsSiteMap_comp_linHom,
        TNLean.PEPS.comp_smulRight_dual,
        TNLean.PEPS.eq_of_forall_trace_mul_eq,
        TNLean.PEPS.mpsSiteMap_comp_linHom_eq_iff,
        TNLean.PEPS.isGInjective_mpsSiteMap_iff}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_mps_g_injective}
    A matrix product tensor $A$ is $G$-injective if and only if
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $U_gA^iU_g^{-1}=A^i$ for all $i$ and $g$, and
        \item $\mathcal{P}(A)$ has a left inverse on the commutant
              $\mathcal{S}=\{X\mid [X,U_g]=0\}$: there is a linear map $L$
              with $L\,\mathcal{P}(A)X=X$ for every $X\in\mathcal{S}$.
    \end{enumerate}
    For a unitary representation $U_g^{-1}=U_g^\dagger$, and this is the
    source's definition
    \cite[Section~``MPS: the $G$-injective case'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_group_averaging}
    Since $\tr[A^iU_gXU_g^{-1}]=\tr[U_g^{-1}A^iU_gX]$, invariance of
    $\mathcal{P}(A)$ under conjugation by $U_g$ says that $U_g^{-1}A^iU_g$
    and $A^i$ have the same trace pairing with every operator $X$; pairing
    with the rank-one operators $\ket{v}\bra{f}$ shows that they are equal.
    The operators fixed by conjugation are the commutant, and a linear map
    is injective on a subspace if and only if it has a left inverse there.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Stability under concatenation]%
    \label{thm:peps_mps_g_injective_concatenation}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.concatTensor, TNLean.PEPS.concatTensor_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.concatLeftInverse,
        TNLean.PEPS.apply_mpsSiteMap,
        TNLean.PEPS.averageMap_linHom_apply_mul,
        TNLean.PEPS.sum_leftInverse_mul_mul,
        TNLean.PEPS.concatLeftInverse_comp_mpsSiteMap,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGInjective.mpsSiteMap_concatTensor}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_mps_g_injective, def:peps_delta_operator}
    Let $A$ and $B$ be $G$-injective matrix product tensors for the same
    representation, with left inverses $L_A$ and $L_B$ satisfying
    $L_A\mathcal{P}(A)=L_B\mathcal{P}(B)=\sigma$. Then $C^{ij}=A^iB^j$ is
    $G$-injective, and the map
    \begin{align*}
        L_C\ket{i,j}=|G|\,\big(L_B\ket{j}\big)\,\Delta\,\big(L_A\ket{i}\big)
    \end{align*}
    satisfies $L_C\mathcal{P}(C)=\sigma$
    \cite[Section~``MPS: the $G$-injective case'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_mps_g_injective_source_form,
        thm:peps_g_injective_left_inverse, thm:peps_delta_expansion}
    Invariance of $C$ follows from
    $U_gA^iB^jU_g^{-1}=(U_gA^iU_g^{-1})(U_gB^jU_g^{-1})$. For the left
    inverse, first $\sum_i\tr[A^iY]\,L_A\ket{i}=\sigma(Y)$ for every $Y$.
    Second, for every operator $M$,
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_j\big(L_B\ket{j}\big)\,M\,B^j
        =\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_h\tr[U_{h^{-1}}M]\,U_h ;
    \end{align*}
    writing $M$ in a basis $\{b_k\}$ with coordinate functionals $b^*_k$,
    the left-hand side applied to $w$ is
    $\sum_k\sigma\big(\ket{w}\bra{b^*_k}\big)Mb_k$, which is the right-hand
    side applied to $w$. Taking $Y=B^jX$ in the first identity and
    $M=\Delta U_g$ in the second,
    \begin{align*}
        L_C\mathcal{P}(C)X
        =|G|\sum_j\big(L_B\ket{j}\big)\,\Delta\,\sigma(B^jX)
        =\sum_g\Big(\sum_j\big(L_B\ket{j}\big)\,\Delta U_g\,B^j\Big)XU_g^{-1}
        =\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_gU_gXU_g^{-1},
    \end{align*}
    the last step by the expansion
    $\sum_h\tr[U_{h^{-1}}\Delta U_g]U_h=U_g$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Blocking a $G$-injective tensor]%
    \label{cor:peps_mps_g_injective_blocking}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.IsGInjective.mpsSiteMap_reindex,
        TNLean.PEPS.wordTensor,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGInjective.mpsSiteMap_wordTensor,
        MPSTensor.toLin'_evalWord,
        MPSTensor.isGInjective_mpsSiteMap_blockTensor}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_mps_g_injective, def:blocked_tensor}
    If $A$ is $G$-injective, then the tensor obtained by blocking $L\ge1$
    sites, $(i_1,\dots,i_L)\mapsto A^{i_1}\cdots A^{i_L}$, is $G$-injective.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_mps_g_injective_concatenation}
    Induction on $L$: the blocked tensor on $L+1$ sites is the concatenation
    of $A$ with the blocked tensor on $L$ sites, up to relabelling the
    physical index, and relabelling preserves $G$-injectivity.
\end{proof}

For $G$-injective tensors the parent-Hamiltonian argument runs as in the
injective case, with the left inverses replaced by left inverses up to the
twirl $\sigma$
\cite[Section~``Parent Hamiltonians'']{Schuch2010PEPS}. In the next two
theorems $A$ and $B$ are $G$-injective for the same representation $U$; three-leg
tensors $M$, $N$ carry one physical and two virtual legs, and the operators $X$,
$M$, $N$, $U_g$ sit on a bond.

\begin{lemma}[Twirling the closing operator]%
    \label{lem:peps_mps_g_injective_twirl}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.mpsSiteMap_averageMap,
        TNLean.PEPS.averageMap_mul_of_conj_eq,
        TNLean.PEPS.mul_comm_of_conj_eq,
        TNLean.PEPS.concatTensor_conj_eq}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_mps_g_injective}
    If $U_gC^iU_g^{-1}=C^i$ for all $i$ and $g$, then
    $\tr[C^i\sigma(X)]=\tr[C^iX]$ for every operator $X$: an operator closing
    an invariant tensor may be replaced by its twirl without changing the
    state. Moreover $\sigma(YZ)=\sigma(Y)Z$ whenever $Z$ commutes with every
    $U_g$, and the concatenation of two invariant tensors is invariant
    \cite[Section~``Parent Hamiltonians'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_mps_g_injective_source_form, thm:peps_group_averaging}
    Invariance of $C$ is invariance of the map $X\mapsto(\tr[C^iX])_i$ under
    conjugation, and an invariant map does not see the average over the
    group. For the second claim, $U_gYZU_g^{-1}=(U_gYU_g^{-1})Z$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The concatenated left inverse on traces]%
    \label{lem:peps_mps_g_injective_left_inverse_traces}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.concatLeftInverse_apply_trace_mul}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_mps_g_injective_concatenation}
    Let $L_A$ be any linear map and let $L_B\mathcal{P}(B)=\sigma$. The map
    $L_C\ket{j,k}=|G|\,(L_A\ket{k})\,\Delta\,(L_B\ket{j})$ of the concatenation
    $C^{jk}=B^jA^k$ sends the vector $(\tr[B^jY_k])_{j,k}$ to
    $|G|\sum_k(L_A\ket{k})\,\Delta\,\sigma(Y_k)$, for every family of
    operators $Y_k$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Summing over $j$ first, $\sum_j\tr[B^jY_k]\,L_B\ket{j}
        =L_B\mathcal{P}(B)Y_k=\sigma(Y_k)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Intersection property for $G$-injective tensors]%
    \label{thm:peps_mps_g_injective_intersection}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.IsGInjective.intersection_property}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_mps_g_injective_source_form,
        thm:peps_mps_g_injective_concatenation}
    \begin{align*}
        \Big\{\sum_{ijk}\tr[A^iB^jM^k]\ket{ijk}\ \Big|\ M\Big\}\cap
        \Big\{\sum_{ijk}\tr[N^iB^jA^k]\ket{ijk}\ \Big|\ N\Big\}
        =\Big\{\sum_{ijk}\tr[A^iB^jA^kX]\ket{ijk}\ \Big|\ X\Big\}
    \end{align*}
    \cite[Section~``Parent Hamiltonians'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{lem:peps_mps_g_injective_twirl,
        lem:peps_mps_g_injective_left_inverse_traces}
    The right-hand side lies in both sets, with $M^k=A^kX$ and $N^i=XA^i$.
    Conversely, let $\tr[A^iB^jM^k]=\tr[N^iB^jA^k]$ for all $i,j,k$. Fix
    left inverses with $L_A\mathcal{P}(A)=L_B\mathcal{P}(B)=\sigma$, and let
    $L_C$ be the left inverse of $C^{jk}=B^jA^k$, so that
    $L_C\mathcal{P}(C)=\sigma$. For fixed $i$ the vector
    $(\tr[B^jA^kN^i])_{j,k}$ equals $(\tr[B^jM^kA^i])_{j,k}$, and applying
    $L_C$ gives
    \begin{align*}
        \sigma(N^i)=|G|\sum_k(L_A\ket{k})\,\Delta\,\sigma(M^kA^i)=XA^i,
        \qquad X=|G|\sum_k(L_A\ket{k})\,\Delta\,\sigma(M^k),
    \end{align*}
    because $A^i$ commutes with every $U_g$. Since $C$ is invariant,
    $\tr[N^iB^jA^k]=\tr[B^jA^k\sigma(N^i)]=\tr[A^iB^jA^kX]$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Closure property for $G$-injective tensors]%
    \label{thm:peps_mps_g_injective_closure}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.IsGInjective.eq_sum_of_closure,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGInjective.closure_property}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_mps_g_injective_source_form,
        thm:peps_mps_g_injective_concatenation, def:peps_delta_operator}
    \begin{align*}
        \Big\{\sum_{ij}\tr[B^iA^jM]\ket{ij}\ \Big|\ M\Big\}\cap
        \Big\{\sum_{ij}\tr[B^iNA^j]\ket{ij}\ \Big|\ N\Big\}
        =\Big\{\sum_g\lambda_g\sum_{ij}\tr[B^iA^jU_g]\ket{ij}\ \Big|\
        \lambda_g\in\C\Big\}.
    \end{align*}
    A vector in the intersection, given by $M$ and $N$, has
    $\lambda_g=\tr[U_{g^{-1}}\sigma(N)\Delta]$
    \cite[Section~``Parent Hamiltonians'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{lem:peps_mps_g_injective_twirl,
        lem:peps_mps_g_injective_left_inverse_traces}
    The right-hand side lies in both sets, with $M=N=\sum_g\lambda_gU_g$,
    since $U_g$ commutes with $A^j$. Conversely, let
    $\tr[B^iA^jM]=\tr[B^iNA^j]$ for all $i,j$, and let $L_C$ be the left
    inverse of $C^{ij}=B^iA^j$. Applying $L_C$ to the vector
    $(\tr[B^iNA^j])_{i,j}$, and then the identity
    $\sum_j(L_A\ket{j})\,Y\,A^j=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_h\tr[U_{h^{-1}}Y]\,U_h$ with
    $Y=\Delta\sigma(N)$,
    \begin{align*}
        \sigma(M)=|G|\sum_j(L_A\ket{j})\,\Delta\,\sigma(N)\,A^j
        =\sum_h\tr[U_{h^{-1}}\Delta\sigma(N)]\,U_h .
    \end{align*}
    Since $C$ is invariant, $\tr[B^iA^jM]=\tr[B^iA^j\sigma(M)]$, and
    $\tr[U_{h^{-1}}\Delta\sigma(N)]=\tr[U_{h^{-1}}\sigma(N)\Delta]$ because
    $\sigma(N)$ commutes with $U_{h^{-1}}$.
\end{proof}

The two properties give the parent Hamiltonian of a $G$-injective tensor, as
the intersection and closure properties of injective tensors give Theorem 3.5
of the source \cite[Section~``Parent Hamiltonians'']{Schuch2010PEPS}. For a
tensor $A$ and a ring of $N$ sites, write $\mathcal G_{N,L}(A)$ for the space
of Definition~\ref{def:chain_ground_space}, the common kernel of the parent
interactions on all windows of $L$ consecutive sites of the ring, and write
$\ket{\mathcal M(A|K)}=\sum_{\sigma}\tr[A^{\sigma_1}\cdots A^{\sigma_N}K]
    \ket{\sigma}$ for the matrix product state with closure $K$. The source's
two-site interaction is $L=2$. In the rest of this subsection $A$ is
$G$-injective for the representation $U$.

\begin{lemma}[The open chain of a $G$-injective tensor]%
    \label{lem:peps_mps_g_injective_open_chain}
    \lean{MPSTensor.mem_groundSpace_add_two_of_isGInjective,
        MPSTensor.chainGroundSpace_le_groundSpace_of_isGInjective}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_mps_g_injective_intersection,
        cor:peps_mps_g_injective_blocking, def:chain_ground_space}
    Let $K\ge1$. A vector on $K+2$ sites whose restrictions to the first and to
    the last $K+1$ sites lie in
    $\{\sum_{\sigma}\tr[A^{\sigma_1}\cdots A^{\sigma_{K+1}}X]\ket{\sigma}\mid X\}$
    lies in the corresponding space on $K+2$ sites. Consequently, if
    $2\le L\le N$, every vector of $\mathcal G_{N,L}(A)$ has the form
    $\sum_{\sigma}\tr[A^{\sigma_1}\cdots A^{\sigma_N}X]\ket{\sigma}$
    \cite[Section~``Parent Hamiltonians'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Fixing the last letter $k$ writes the vector as $\tr[A^iB^wM^k]$ and fixing
    the first letter $i$ as $\tr[N^iB^wA^k]$, where $B$ is the tensor of $K$
    blocked sites, which is $G$-injective by
    Corollary~\ref{cor:peps_mps_g_injective_blocking}. The intersection
    property gives the form $\tr[A^iB^wA^kX]$. Iterating along the
    non-wrapping windows of the ring gives the second claim, as in the proof of
    Theorem 3.5 of the source.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Parent Hamiltonians of $G$-injective tensors]%
    \label{thm:peps_mps_g_injective_parent_hamiltonian}
    \lean{MPSTensor.groundSpaceMap_mem_span_closure_of_isGInjective,
        MPSTensor.groundSpaceMap_rep_mem_chainGroundSpace,
        MPSTensor.chainGroundSpace_eq_span_closure_of_isGInjective}
    \leanok
    \uses{lem:peps_mps_g_injective_open_chain,
        thm:peps_mps_g_injective_closure}
    If $2\le L\le N$, then
    \begin{align*}
        \mathcal G_{N,L}(A)=\spn_{\C}\bigl\{\ket{\mathcal M(A|U_g)}\ \big|\
        g\in G\bigr\}.
    \end{align*}
    In particular, for the two-site interaction on a ring of $N\ge2$ sites
    the frustration-free ground space of the parent Hamiltonian is spanned by
    the states $\ket{\mathcal M(A|U_g)}$
    \cite[Theorem~4.11]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Each $U_g$ commutes with every $A^i$, so it can be moved to any bond, and
    every window of $\ket{\mathcal M(A|U_g)}$ lies in the local ground space.
    Conversely, by Lemma~\ref{lem:peps_mps_g_injective_open_chain} a vector
    of $\mathcal G_{N,L}(A)$ is $\tr[A^{\sigma_1}\cdots A^{\sigma_N}X]$, and
    its translate by one site is $\tr[A^{\sigma_1}\cdots A^{\sigma_N}Y]$. Hence
    $\tr[A^aB^wX]=\tr[A^aYB^w]$, where $B$ is the tensor of the last $N-1$
    blocked sites. The closure property, applied to $A$ and $B$, writes the
    vector as a combination of the states $\ket{\mathcal M(A|U_g)}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Twirled representation operators]%
    \label{lem:peps_twirled_representation}
    \lean{Representation.rep_mul_charProjector,
        Representation.apply_conj_of_mem_irreducibleCharacters,
        Representation.sum_rep_conj_apply_of_mem,
        Representation.averageMap_linHom_rep,
        Representation.linearIndependent_charProjector,
        Representation.linearIndependent_of_isSemiRegular,
        Representation.linearIndependent_averageMap_conjClasses}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_isotypic_projectors, thm:peps_group_averaging,
        thm:peps_semiregular_trace}
    The isotypic projectors $P_i$ commute with every $U_g$, are linearly
    independent, and
    \begin{align*}
        \sigma(U_g)=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_hU_{hgh^{-1}}
        =\sum_{i=1}^{r}\frac{\chi_i(g)}{\chi_i(1)}\,P_i .
    \end{align*}
    If $U$ is semi-regular, then the operators $U_g$ are linearly independent,
    and so are the twirls $\sigma(U_g)$ of one representative $g$ of each
    conjugacy class \cite[Section~``Parent Hamiltonians'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    On an irreducible subrepresentation with character $\chi_i$ the operator
    $\sum_hU_{hgh^{-1}}$ commutes with the representation, so by Schur's lemma
    it is a scalar, and its trace $|G|\chi_i(g)$ fixes the scalar as
    $|G|\chi_i(g)/\chi_i(1)$; the projector $P_i$ acts there as the identity
    and every other projector as zero. Evaluating $\sum_i\mu_iP_i$ on a nonzero
    vector of such a subrepresentation gives $\mu_i$ times the vector. For
    semi-regular $U$, the functional $X\mapsto\tr[U_{g^{-1}}X\Delta]$ takes the
    value $\delta_{g,h}$ on $U_h$ by
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_semiregular_trace}; on $\sigma(U_k)$ it is
    $\frac{1}{|G|}$ times the number of $h$ with $hkh^{-1}=g$, which vanishes
    unless $k$ is conjugate to $g$ and is positive for $k=g$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Structure of the ground space of a $G$-injective tensor]%
    \label{thm:peps_mps_g_injective_ground_space_structure}
    \lean{MPSTensor.groundSpaceMap_toMatrix'_averageMap,
        MPSTensor.chainGroundSpace_eq_span_charProjector_of_isGInjective,
        MPSTensor.linearIndependent_groundSpaceMap_charProjector_of_isGInjective,
        MPSTensor.finrank_chainGroundSpace_of_isGInjective,
        MPSTensor.chainGroundSpace_eq_span_conjClasses_of_isGInjective,
        MPSTensor.linearIndependent_groundSpaceMap_conjClasses_of_isSemiRegular,
        MPSTensor.finrank_chainGroundSpace_of_isSemiRegular,
        MPSTensor.card_irreducibleCharacterFinset_of_isSemiRegular}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_mps_g_injective_parent_hamiltonian,
        lem:peps_twirled_representation, def:peps_semiregular}
    Let $2\le L\le N$, and let $\chi_1,\dots,\chi_I$ be the distinct
    characters of the irreducible representations occurring in $U$, with
    isotypic projectors $P_i$. The states $\ket{\mathcal M(A|P_i)}$ are
    linearly independent and span $\mathcal G_{N,L}(A)$, so this space has
    dimension $I$. If $U$ is semi-regular, then $I$ is the number of conjugacy
    classes of $G$, and for any choice of one representative $g$ of each
    conjugacy class the states $\ket{\mathcal M(A|U_g)}$ are linearly
    independent and span $\mathcal G_{N,L}(A)$
    \cite[Theorem~4.12]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Since $A$ is invariant, $\ket{\mathcal M(A|K)}=\ket{\mathcal M(A|\sigma(K))}$
    for every closure $K$; in particular the closures of one conjugacy class
    give the same state. By Lemma~\ref{lem:peps_twirled_representation} each
    $\ket{\mathcal M(A|U_g)}$ is a combination of the states
    $\ket{\mathcal M(A|P_i)}$, and conversely each $P_i$ is a combination of
    the $U_g$, so Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_mps_g_injective_parent_hamiltonian}
    gives the spanning claims. The tensor of $N$ blocked sites is
    $G$-injective, so $K\mapsto\ket{\mathcal M(A|K)}$ is injective on the
    operators commuting with every $U_g$. These contain the projectors $P_i$
    and the twirls $\sigma(U_g)$, and
    Lemma~\ref{lem:peps_twirled_representation} gives the linear independence
    of both families. Computing the dimension of $\mathcal G_{2,2}(A)$ in the
    two ways gives $I$ equal to the number of conjugacy classes. The source
    instead quotes that the numbers of conjugacy classes and of irreducible
    representations agree, and passes from the $U_g$ to the $P_i$ through the
    completeness of the characters in the class functions.
\end{proof}

In two dimensions every link of the network carries its own representation,
and the two tensors on a link act with the same one
\cite[Section~``Two dimensions: $G$-injective PEPS'']{Schuch2010PEPS}. A
leg entered by $U_g$ is a space $E$ carrying the representation $\tau$, and
the leg at the other end of the link, left by $U_g^{-1}$, is the dual space
$E^*$ carrying the contragredient representation
$\tau^*(g)f=f\circ\tau(g^{-1})$. For a basis $e_i$ of $E$ with dual basis
$e_i^*$, contracting the two legs is pairing against
$\omega=\sum_ie_i^*\otimes e_i$, and a tensor is identified with its map
$\mathcal{P}$ from the virtual to the physical system.

\begin{lemma}[Contracted links]%
    \label{lem:peps_link_contraction}
    \lean{Representation.averageMap_apply_eq_sum,
        Representation.map_dual_coevaluation,
        contractRight_map_coevaluation, contractRight_map_dual,
        TNLean.PEPS.linkVector, TNLean.PEPS.map_comm_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.map_dual_linkVector, TNLean.PEPS.linkPairing,
        TNLean.PEPS.linkPairing_tmul, TNLean.PEPS.linkPairing_comm,
        TNLean.PEPS.linkPairing_linkVector,
        TNLean.PEPS.linkPairing_deltaOperator_map}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_delta_operator, def:peps_semiregular,
        thm:peps_semiregular_trace}
    The vector $\omega$ is invariant under $\tau^*(g)\otimes\tau(g)$ for every
    $g$. For an operator $M$ on $E$, the pairing $f\otimes e\mapsto f(Me)$
    sends $\omega$ to $\tr M$ and $(\tau^*(g)\otimes\tau(h))\,\omega$ to
    $\tr[U_{g^{-1}}MU_h]$. If $\tau$ is semi-regular and $M=\Delta$, the
    latter is $\delta_{g,h}$
    \cite[Section~``Two dimensions: $G$-injective PEPS'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Expanding $\tau^*(g)e_i^*$ in the dual basis,
    $(\tau^*(g)\otimes\tau(g))\,\omega
        =\sum_j e_j^*\otimes\tau(g)\sum_ie_i^*\big(\tau(g^{-1})e_j\big)e_i
        =\sum_je_j^*\otimes e_j$. The pairing of $\omega$ is
    $\sum_ie_i^*(Me_i)=\tr M$, and the pairing of
    $(\tau^*(g)\otimes\tau(h))\,\omega$ is
    $\sum_ie_i^*\big(\tau(g^{-1})M\tau(h)e_i\big)$. For $M=\Delta$ this is
    $\tr[U_hU_{g^{-1}}\Delta]=\delta_{g,h}$ by
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_semiregular_trace}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Stability of $G$-injectivity under concatenation]%
    \label{thm:peps_g_injective_concatenation}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.linkContraction, TNLean.PEPS.linkContraction_tmul,
        TNLean.PEPS.linkContraction_comp_tprod,
        TNLean.PEPS.innerContraction, TNLean.PEPS.innerContraction_tmul,
        TNLean.PEPS.innerContraction_map,
        TNLean.PEPS.linkContractionLeftInverse,
        TNLean.PEPS.linkContractionLeftInverse_comp,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGInjective.linkContraction}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_g_injective, def:peps_delta_operator,
        def:peps_semiregular, thm:peps_g_injective_left_inverse,
        lem:peps_link_contraction, thm:peps_semiregular_tensor_product}
    Let $A$ be a tensor with virtual system $W_A\otimes E^*$ and group action
    $\sigma_A\otimes\tau^*$, where $E^*$ is its right leg and $W_A$ collects
    its left, up and down legs, and let $B$ be a tensor with virtual system
    $E\otimes W_B$ and group action $\tau\otimes\sigma_B$, where $E$ is its
    left leg and $W_B$ collects its up, right and down legs. Let $\tau$ be
    semi-regular, and let $C$ be the tensor obtained by contracting the right
    leg of $A$ with the left leg of $B$, with the up, down and physical
    indices blocked:
    \begin{align*}
        \mathcal{P}(C)(x\otimes y)
        =\sum_i\mathcal{P}(A)(x\otimes e_i^*)\otimes\mathcal{P}(B)(e_i\otimes y).
    \end{align*}
    If $A$ and $B$ are $G$-injective, then $C$ is $G$-injective for
    $\sigma_A\otimes\sigma_B$. Precisely, if
    $L_A\mathcal{P}(A)=\Pi_{\sigma_A\otimes\tau^*}$ and
    $L_B\mathcal{P}(B)=\Pi_{\tau\otimes\sigma_B}$, then the map $L_C$ that
    applies $L_A\otimes L_B$ and contracts the two inner legs through $\Delta$,
    $(x\otimes f)\otimes(e\otimes y)\mapsto|G|\,f(\Delta e)\,x\otimes y$,
    satisfies $L_C\mathcal{P}(C)=\Pi_{\sigma_A\otimes\sigma_B}$. If moreover
    $\sigma_A$ and $\sigma_B$ are semi-regular, then so is
    $\sigma_A\otimes\sigma_B$ \cite[Lemma~5.2]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Invariance of $A$ gives
    $\mathcal{P}(A)(\sigma_A(g)x\otimes f)=\mathcal{P}(A)(x\otimes\tau^*(g^{-1})f)$
    and similarly for $B$, so acting with $\sigma_A(g)\otimes\sigma_B(g)$ on
    the virtual system of $C$ acts with $\tau^*(g^{-1})\otimes\tau(g^{-1})$
    on $\omega$, which leaves it invariant by
    Lemma~\ref{lem:peps_link_contraction}. For the left inverse,
    $L_A\mathcal{P}(A)(x\otimes f)
        =\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_g\sigma_A(g)x\otimes\tau^*(g)f$ and
    $L_B\mathcal{P}(B)(e\otimes y)
        =\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_h\tau(h)e\otimes\sigma_B(h)y$. Contracting through
    $\Delta$ gives
    \begin{align*}
        L_C\mathcal{P}(C)(x\otimes y)
        =\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g,h}\tr[U_{g^{-1}}\Delta U_h]\,
        \sigma_A(g)x\otimes\sigma_B(h)y
        =\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_g\sigma_A(g)x\otimes\sigma_B(g)y
    \end{align*}
    by Lemma~\ref{lem:peps_link_contraction}. Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_g_injective_left_inverse}
    turns invariance and the left inverse into $G$-injectivity, and
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_semiregular_tensor_product} gives the last claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Contracting legs of a connected block]%
    \label{thm:peps_g_injective_leg_contraction}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.legContraction,
        TNLean.PEPS.legContractionLeftInverse,
        TNLean.PEPS.legContraction_comp,
        TNLean.PEPS.legContractionLeftInverse_comp,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGInjective.legContraction}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_g_injective, def:peps_delta_operator,
        def:peps_semiregular, thm:peps_g_injective_left_inverse,
        lem:peps_link_contraction}
    Let a tensor have virtual system $W\otimes E^*\otimes E$ with group action
    $\sigma\otimes\tau^*\otimes\tau$, as for the up leg of $A$ and the down leg
    of $B$ in the block $C$ above. Contracting the legs $E^*$ and $E$ gives
    the map $x\mapsto\sum_i\mathcal{P}(x\otimes e_i^*\otimes e_i)$. If
    $L\mathcal{P}=\Pi_{\sigma\otimes\tau^*\otimes\tau}$ and $\tr M\ne0$, then
    contracting the corresponding legs of $L$ through $M$ and dividing by
    $\tr M$ gives a left inverse of the contracted map up to $\Pi_\sigma$.
    Consequently, if the tensor is $G$-injective and $\tau$ is semi-regular,
    the contracted map is $G$-injective for $\sigma$, with $M=\Delta$, whose
    trace is $1$ \cite[Observation~5.3]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    The group elements attached to the two contracted legs cancel, because
    $\omega$ is invariant under $\tau^*(g)\otimes\tau(g)$. Hence
    $\Pi_{\sigma\otimes\tau^*\otimes\tau}(x\otimes\omega)
        =\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_g\sigma(g)x\otimes\omega$, and pairing $\omega$
    through $M$ gives $\tr M$. By Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_semiregular_trace}
    with $g=h=1$, $\tr\Delta=1$ for semi-regular $\tau$.
\end{proof}

For a tensor $A$ let $A^\dagger$ be the tensor with complex conjugate entries
and the roles of the incoming and outgoing legs exchanged, so that
$\mathcal{P}(A^\dagger)=\mathcal{P}(A)^\dagger$. For a matrix product tensor
with bond space $\mathbb{C}[G]$, on which the left-regular representation acts
by $L_g\ket{h}=\ket{gh}$ and the group elements are orthonormal,
$\mathcal{P}(A^\dagger)\ket{i}=(A^i)^\dagger$ under the identification above
\cite[Section~``Isometric PEPS'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.

\begin{definition}[$G$-isometric matrix product tensor]%
    \label{def:peps_mps_g_isometric}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularAdjoint, TNLean.PEPS.regularAdjoint_mul,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularAdjoint_smul, TNLean.PEPS.mpsAdjointSiteMap,
        TNLean.PEPS.mpsAdjointSiteMap_single, TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometricMPS}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_mps_g_injective}
    A matrix product tensor $A$ with bond space $\mathbb{C}[G]$ is
    $G$-isometric if $L_gA^iL_g^{-1}=A^i$ for all $g$ and $i$ and
    $\mathcal{P}(A^\dagger)\mathcal{P}(A)=c\,\sigma$ for one constant $c>0$,
    where $\sigma$ is the twirl onto the commutant of the left-regular
    representation. For $c=1$ this is the condition
    $\mathcal{P}(A)^{-1}=\mathcal{P}(A^\dagger)$ of the source; the constant
    absorbs the normalization of the tensor
    \cite[Definition~6.1]{Schuch2010PEPS},
    \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[$G$-isometric tensors are $G$-injective]%
    \label{thm:peps_mps_g_isometric_injective}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometricMPS.isGInjective}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_mps_g_isometric, thm:peps_g_injective_left_inverse,
        thm:peps_mps_g_injective_source_form}
    A $G$-isometric matrix product tensor is $G$-injective for the left-regular
    representation, with left inverse $c^{-1}\mathcal{P}(A^\dagger)$
    \cite[Definition~6.1]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Invariance of $\mathcal{P}(A)$ under conjugation is equivalent to
    $L_gA^iL_g^{-1}=A^i$, and
    $c^{-1}\mathcal{P}(A^\dagger)\mathcal{P}(A)=\sigma$; the left-inverse form
    of $G$-injectivity applies.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The left inverse for the regular representation]%
    \label{lem:peps_regular_left_inverse}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.card_smul_deltaOperator_leftRegular,
        TNLean.PEPS.concatLeftInverse_leftRegular,
        TNLean.PEPS.innerContraction_smul,
        TNLean.PEPS.linkContractionLeftInverse_leftRegular}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_semiregular_trace, thm:peps_mps_g_injective_concatenation,
        thm:peps_g_injective_concatenation}
    For the left-regular representation $|G|\,\Delta=\mathbb{1}$. Hence the
    left inverse of a concatenation $C^{ij}=A^iB^j$ of matrix product tensors
    built from left inverses $L_A$ and $L_B$ is
    $L_C\ket{i,j}=(L_B\ket{j})(L_A\ket{i})$, and if the contracted link of two
    tensors in two dimensions carries the left-regular representation, the left
    inverse of the contracted tensor applies $L_A\otimes L_B$ and contracts the
    two ends of the link directly \cite[Lemma~6.2]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    By Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_semiregular_trace}, $\Delta=\frac{1}{|G|}\Id$ for
    the left-regular representation, that is, $|G|\,\Delta=\mathbb{1}$.
    Substitute this in the two left inverses.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Stability of isometry under concatenation of matrix product tensors]%
    \label{thm:peps_mps_g_isometric_concatenation}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.concatLeftInverse_mpsAdjointSiteMap,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometricMPS.concatTensor}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_mps_g_isometric, lem:peps_regular_left_inverse,
        thm:peps_mps_g_injective_concatenation}
    If the matrix product tensors $A$ and $B$ are $G$-isometric, so is
    $C^{ij}=A^iB^j$: the left inverse built from $\mathcal{P}(A^\dagger)$ and
    $\mathcal{P}(B^\dagger)$ is $\mathcal{P}(C^\dagger)$, and
    $\mathcal{P}(C^\dagger)\mathcal{P}(C)=c_Ac_B\,\sigma$. This is the
    one-dimensional case of the source's lemma; the two-dimensional case is
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_g_isometric_link_contraction}
    \cite[Lemma~6.2]{Schuch2010PEPS},
    \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    By Lemma~\ref{lem:peps_regular_left_inverse} the left inverse built from
    $c_A^{-1}\mathcal{P}(A^\dagger)$ and $c_B^{-1}\mathcal{P}(B^\dagger)$ sends
    $\ket{i,j}$ to $(c_Ac_B)^{-1}(B^j)^\dagger(A^i)^\dagger
        =(c_Ac_B)^{-1}(A^iB^j)^\dagger$, and by
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_mps_g_injective_concatenation} it is a left
    inverse of $\mathcal{P}(C)$. Invariance of $C$ holds as for
    $G$-injective tensors.
\end{proof}


\begin{theorem}[Adjoints and invariant inner products]%
    \label{thm:peps_isometric_adjoint}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.toMatrix_basisCoordinates,
        TNLean.PEPS.basisCoordinates,
        TNLean.PEPS.basisRepresentation,
        TNLean.PEPS.basisRepresentation_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.basisCoordinates_comp,
        TNLean.PEPS.toMatrix_basisRepresentation,
        TNLean.PEPS.matrix_mem_unitaryGroup_iff_dotProduct,
        TNLean.PEPS.basisRepresentation_unitary_iff,
        TNLean.PEPS.basisRepresentation_unitary_tensorProduct,
        TNLean.PEPS.basisRepresentation_unitary_dual,
        TNLean.PEPS.toMatrix_leftRegular,
        TNLean.PEPS.basisRepresentation_unitary_leftRegular,
        TNLean.PEPS.basisAdjoint,
        TNLean.PEPS.toMatrix_basisAdjoint,
        TNLean.PEPS.coordinateAdjoint,
        TNLean.PEPS.coordinateAdjoint_dotProduct,
        TNLean.PEPS.averageMap_dotProduct_of_unitary,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.exists_coordinateAdjoint_comp,
        TNLean.PEPS.isGIsometric_of_coordinateAdjoint_comp,
        TNLean.PEPS.basisCoordinates_averageMap,
        TNLean.PEPS.basisCoordinates_invariant_iff,
        TNLean.PEPS.basisCoordinates_smul,
        TNLean.PEPS.basisCoordinates_basisAdjoint,
        TNLean.PEPS.basisCoordinates_injective,
        TNLean.PEPS.basisAdjoint_comp_iff}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_g_injective}
    Choose orthonormal bases of the virtual and physical spaces. If the
    virtual representation $\rho$ is unitary, its averaging projector
    $\Pi_\rho$ is self-adjoint. An invariant map $T$ preserves the inner
    products of invariant vectors up to a positive factor $c$ exactly when
    $T^\dagger T=c\Pi_\rho$. Tensor products and contragredients of unitary
    representations are unitary; in particular this applies to every
    product of regular virtual legs \cite[Section~6]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_group_averaging}
    Unitarity gives $\langle\rho(g)x,y\rangle
        =\langle x,\rho(g^{-1})y\rangle$; averaging and replacing $g$ by
    $g^{-1}$ proves self-adjointness. Since $T\Pi_\rho=T$, the restricted
    inner-product identity gives
    $\langle Tx,Ty\rangle=c\langle x,\Pi_\rho y\rangle$, which is equivalent
    to the adjoint identity. Conversely, apply the identity to invariant
    vectors, on which $\Pi_\rho$ is the identity.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Isometry under contraction of one regular bond]%
    \label{thm:peps_g_isometric_concatenation}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.linkVector_eq_sum, TNLean.PEPS.toMatrix_linkContraction,
        TNLean.PEPS.toMatrix_innerContraction_map,
        TNLean.PEPS.basisAdjoint_linkContraction,
        TNLean.PEPS.basisAdjoint_linkContraction_comp,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.linkContraction_basis,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.linkContraction_fourLeg}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_g_injective, thm:peps_g_injective_concatenation, thm:peps_isometric_adjoint}
    Let $A$ and $B$ be $G$-isometric four-leg PEPS tensors with regular
    virtual legs. Contract the right leg of $A$ with the left leg of $B$,
    leaving the other six virtual legs open. The resulting tensor $C$ is
    $G$-isometric and satisfies
    $\mathcal P(C)^\dagger\mathcal P(C)=c_Ac_B\Pi$.
    This is the elementary concatenation step in
    \cite[Lemma~6.2]{Schuch2010PEPS}; the factors $c_A,c_B$
    record the tensor normalization convention
    \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
    More generally, the same conclusion holds for unitary representations
    on the remaining virtual spaces and a regular representation on the
    contracted link.

    \begin{center}
        % Source: SCP10 Lemma 6.2; coefficient C_(i,j),(a,b)=sum_k A_i,(a,k)B_j,(k,b).
        % Right virtual leg of A pairs with the left virtual leg of B.
        % Six virtual legs remain open; physical labels i,j are fixed.
        % Boundary signature: (V,P)=(6,0).
        \tenkzkernel
        \begin{tenkz}[rows={wire},cols=2,bonds=none]
            \tn[at=(1,1),skin=box,name=A,ports={180:virtual,0:virtual,90:virtual,270:virtual}]{A^i}
            \tn[at=(1,2),skin=box,name=B,ports={180:virtual,0:virtual,90:virtual,270:virtual}]{B^j}
            \tnwire{open w}{A.180}
            \tnwire{A.0}{B.180}
            \tnwire{B.0}{open e}
            \tnwire{open n}{A.90}
            \tnwire{open n}{B.90}
            \tnwire{A.270}{open s}
            \tnwire{B.270}{open s}
        \end{tenkz}
    \end{center}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{lem:peps_regular_left_inverse}
    In the product bases,
    $C_{(i,j),(a,b)}=\sum_k A_{i,(a,k)}B_{j,(k,b)}$.
    Complex conjugation proves that $\mathcal P(C)^\dagger$ applies
    $\mathcal P(A)^\dagger\otimes\mathcal P(B)^\dagger$ and pairs the inner
    legs. By Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_isometric_adjoint} the two left inverses
    are $c_A^{-1}\mathcal P(A)^\dagger$ and
    $c_B^{-1}\mathcal P(B)^\dagger$. Lemma~\ref{lem:peps_regular_left_inverse}
    therefore identifies the contracted left inverse with
    $(c_Ac_B)^{-1}\mathcal P(C)^\dagger$.
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_g_injective_concatenation} proves both invariance
    and the left-inverse equation, giving the assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Regular representations in relative coordinates]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_blocking_coordinates}
    \lean{Matrix.permMatrixHom_prodCongr,
        RegularRepresentation.blockingEquiv, RegularRepresentation.blockingEquiv_apply,
        RegularRepresentation.blockingEquiv_symm_apply,
        RegularRepresentation.blockingEquiv_conjugates,
        RegularRepresentation.blockingFamilyEquiv,
        RegularRepresentation.blockingFamilyEquiv_apply,
        RegularRepresentation.blockingFamilyEquiv_symm_apply,
        RegularRepresentation.blockingFamilyEquiv_smul,
        RegularRepresentation.blockingFamilyEquiv_conjugates,
        RegularRepresentation.blockingMatrix,
        RegularRepresentation.blockingMatrix_mem_unitaryGroup,
        RegularRepresentation.blockingMatrix_conjugates,
        RegularRepresentation.blockingFamilyMatrix,
        RegularRepresentation.blockingFamilyMatrix_mem_unitaryGroup,
        RegularRepresentation.blockingFamilyMatrix_conjugates}
    \leanok
    The basis permutation $T\ket{a,b}=\ket{a,ab}$ is unitary and satisfies
    $T^\dagger(L_g\otimes L_g)T=L_g\otimes\mathbb 1$, where
    $L_g\ket a=\ket{ga}$. For a finite family of additional labels, the
    permutation $\ket{a,(b_i)_i}\mapsto\ket{a,(ab_i)_i}$ similarly transforms
    simultaneous left multiplication into left multiplication of $a$
    alone \cite[Section~6, ``Blocking and renormalization'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    The inverse change of coordinates is $(a,(c_i)_i)\mapsto
        (a,(a^{-1}c_i)_i)$. Thus simultaneous multiplication by $g$ sends
    $(a,(ab_i)_i)$ to $(ga,(gab_i)_i)$, and in relative coordinates only
    the first label changes. The permutation matrices are unitary, and
    the matrix of a product permutation is the Kronecker product of the
    factor matrices.
\end{proof}

In two dimensions the concatenation contracts the right leg of a tensor $A$,
with virtual system $W_{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}[G]^{*}$, with the left leg of a
tensor $B$, with virtual system $\mathbb{C}[G]\otimes W_{B}$. In the basis of
group elements on the link and coordinate bases of the remaining legs and of
the physical systems, the contracted tensor has the entries
$C_{(k,l),(a,b)}=\sum_{g}A_{k,(a,g)}B_{l,(g,b)}$, and the representations of
the two virtual systems are $\sigma_{A}\otimes L$ and $L\otimes\sigma_{B}$,
where $L$ is the left-regular representation on $\mathbb{C}^{G}$: the
contragredient of a permutation representation has the same matrices in the
dual basis.

\begin{theorem}[Stability of isometry under the contraction of one link]%
    \label{thm:peps_g_isometric_link_contraction}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.leftRegularFun, TNLean.PEPS.leftRegularFun_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.coeff_leftRegular, TNLean.PEPS.toMatrix'_leftRegularFun,
        TNLean.PEPS.toMatrix'_leftRegularFun_mem_unitaryGroup,
        TNLean.PEPS.kroneckerRep, TNLean.PEPS.toMatrix'_kroneckerRep,
        TNLean.PEPS.toMatrix'_kroneckerRep_mem_unitaryGroup,
        TNLean.PEPS.star_averageMap_dotProduct,
        TNLean.PEPS.conjTranspose_toMatrix'_mul_toMatrix',
        TNLean.PEPS.linkContractionMatrix, TNLean.PEPS.linkContractionCoord,
        TNLean.PEPS.conjTranspose_linkContractionMatrix_mul,
        TNLean.PEPS.conjTranspose_linkContractionMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.toMatrix'_averageMap,
        TNLean.PEPS.conjTranspose_linkContractionMatrix_mul_eq,
        TNLean.PEPS.linkContractionCoord_comp_kroneckerRep,
        TNLean.PEPS.star_linkContractionCoord_dotProduct,
        TNLean.PEPS.toLin'_conjTranspose_comp_linkContractionCoord,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.linkContractionCoord,
        TNLean.PEPS.dualTensorHom_sum_coord_tmul, TNLean.PEPS.linkVector_eq_sum_basis,
        TNLean.PEPS.linkLeftCoord, TNLean.PEPS.linkRightCoord, TNLean.PEPS.pairCoord,
        TNLean.PEPS.linkLeftCoord_single, TNLean.PEPS.linkRightCoord_single,
        TNLean.PEPS.pairCoord_single, TNLean.PEPS.tensorCoord,
        TNLean.PEPS.tensorCoord_tmul,
        TNLean.PEPS.tensorCoord_comp_linkContraction_comp_pairCoord,
        TNLean.PEPS.linkLeftCoord_comp_kroneckerRep,
        TNLean.PEPS.linkRightCoord_comp_kroneckerRep,
        TNLean.PEPS.pairCoord_comp_kroneckerRep,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.linkContraction}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_g_injective, thm:peps_g_injective_concatenation}
    Let $\sigma_{A}$ and $\sigma_{B}$ be unitary representations on the remaining
    legs of $A$ and $B$, and let the contracted link carry the left-regular
    representation. If $A$ is $G$-isometric for $\sigma_{A}\otimes L$ with
    constant $c_{A}$ and $B$ is $G$-isometric for $L\otimes\sigma_{B}$ with
    constant $c_{B}$, then the contracted tensor $C$ is $G$-isometric for
    $\sigma_{A}\otimes\sigma_{B}$ with constant $c_{A}c_{B}$, and its left
    inverse is $(c_{A}c_{B})^{-1}\mathcal{P}(C)^{\dagger}$, the contraction of
    the adjoints of the two factors. In the source every leg carries the
    left-regular representation or its contragredient, so the unitarity of
    $\sigma_{A}$ and $\sigma_{B}$ follows from its hypotheses
    \cite[Lemma~6.2]{Schuch2010PEPS},
    \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    For a unitary representation, $G$-isometry with constant $c$ means
    $\mathcal{P}(A)^{\dagger}\mathcal{P}(A)=c\,\Pi$, since $\mathcal{P}(A)$ is
    invariant and $\Pi$ is self-adjoint. The Gram matrix of $C$ is
    \begin{align}
        (\mathcal{P}(C)^{\dagger}\mathcal{P}(C))_{(a,b),(a',b')}
        =\sum_{g,g'}(\mathcal{P}(A)^{\dagger}\mathcal{P}(A))_{(a,g),(a',g')}
        (\mathcal{P}(B)^{\dagger}\mathcal{P}(B))_{(g,b),(g',b')} .
        \label{eq:peps_link_contraction_gram}
    \end{align}
    Writing both projectors as averages over $G$, the link indices contribute
    $\sum_{g,g'}(L_{h})_{gg'}(L_{k})_{gg'}=|G|\,\delta_{h,k}$, which is the
    identity $\Delta=|G|^{-1}\mathbb{1}$ for the left-regular representation,
    so the right-hand side is $c_{A}c_{B}\,\Pi_{(a,b),(a',b')}$ for the
    projector $\Pi$ of $\sigma_{A}\otimes\sigma_{B}$. Hence
    $\langle\mathcal{P}(C)x,\mathcal{P}(C)y\rangle=c_{A}c_{B}\langle x,y\rangle$
    for invariant $x,y$, which also gives injectivity on the invariant
    subspace. Entrywise,
    $\mathcal{P}(C)^{\dagger}_{(a,b),(k,l)}
        =\sum_{g}\mathcal{P}(A)^{\dagger}_{(a,g),k}\mathcal{P}(B)^{\dagger}_{(g,b),l}$,
    so $\mathcal{P}(C)^{\dagger}$ is the contraction of the adjoints. Invariance of $C$ holds because the actions on the contracted
    link cancel, as in Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_g_injective_concatenation}.
\end{proof}

The source shows next that for a $G$-isometric tensor the virtual level,
restricted to the invariant subspace, and the physical level are related by a
unitary. Its standing convention is that the physical system of a site is the
range of $\mathcal{P}(A)$, which can always be arranged by a local isometry
\cite[Sections~``MPS and PEPS'' and ``Isometric PEPS'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.

\begin{theorem}[Virtual unitaries implementable on the physical level]%
    \label{thm:peps_g_isometric_virtual_unitaries}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.mem_unitaryGroup_of_dotProduct,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGInjective.leftInverse_mem_invariants_of_surjective,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.exists_unitary_comp_eq,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.exists_comp_eq_of_unitary}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_g_injective, thm:peps_g_injective_left_inverse}
    Let $A$ be $G$-isometric with $\mathcal{P}(A)$ onto the physical system.
    If $V$ is a unitary on the virtual level that commutes with $U_g$ for
    every $g$, there is a unitary $W$ on the physical system with
    $W\mathcal{P}(A)=\mathcal{P}(A)V$. Conversely, for every unitary $W$ on the
    physical system, $V=\mathcal{P}(A)^{-1}W\mathcal{P}(A)$ satisfies
    $VU_g=U_gV=V$ for every $g$, preserves the inner products of invariant
    vectors, and $\mathcal{P}(A)V=W\mathcal{P}(A)$
    \cite[Lemma~6.3]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Let $L$ be a left inverse with $L\mathcal{P}(A)=\Pi_U$. Since
    $\mathcal{P}(A)$ is onto, every physical vector is $\mathcal{P}(A)x$ with
    $x$ invariant, $Ly$ is invariant and $\mathcal{P}(A)Ly=y$. For the first
    claim let $W=\mathcal{P}(A)VL$. As $V$ commutes with $U_g$, it preserves
    the invariant subspace and commutes with $\Pi_U$, so
    $W\mathcal{P}(A)=\mathcal{P}(A)V\Pi_U=\mathcal{P}(A)\Pi_UV=\mathcal{P}(A)V$;
    and for invariant $x,x'$,
    $\langle W\mathcal{P}(A)x,W\mathcal{P}(A)x'\rangle
        =c\langle Vx,Vx'\rangle=c\langle x,x'\rangle
        =\langle\mathcal{P}(A)x,\mathcal{P}(A)x'\rangle$, so $W$ is unitary.
    For the converse, $V=LW\mathcal{P}(A)$ satisfies $VU_g=V$ because
    $\mathcal{P}(A)U_g=\mathcal{P}(A)$, and $U_gV=V$ because $V$ takes
    invariant values; $\mathcal{P}(A)V=W\mathcal{P}(A)$ because
    $\mathcal{P}(A)L$ is the identity, and the same computation as before
    shows that $V$ preserves inner products of invariant vectors.
\end{proof}

The parent Hamiltonian of a $G$-isometric matrix product state has commuting
local terms \cite[Section~``Commuting parent Hamiltonians'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.
Write $\Gamma_{n}(X)=\sum_{\sigma}\operatorname{tr}[A^{\sigma}X]\ket{\sigma}$ for
the parametrization of the local space $\mathcal{S}_{n}$ on $n$ sites by
boundary matrices, where $A^{\sigma}=A^{\sigma_{1}}\cdots A^{\sigma_{n}}$.

\begin{definition}[The operator $\Gamma_{n}\Gamma_{n}^{\dagger}$]%
    \label{def:peps_ground_space_frame}
    \lean{MPSTensor.groundSpaceMapAdjoint, MPSTensor.groundSpaceMapAdjoint_apply,
        MPSTensor.groundSpaceFrame, MPSTensor.groundSpaceFrame_apply}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:ground_space_parent}
    For a matrix product tensor $A$, let
    $\Gamma_{n}^{\dagger}\psi=\sum_{\tau}\psi(\tau)\,(A^{\tau})^{\dagger}$, the
    adjoint of $\Gamma_{n}$ for the Hilbert--Schmidt inner product, and let
    $P_{n}=\Gamma_{n}\Gamma_{n}^{\dagger}$, the operator on $n$ sites with matrix
    entries $\operatorname{tr}[A^{\sigma}(A^{\tau})^{\dagger}]$. For $n=2$ this is
    the operator of \cite[Lemma~6.11]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[A multiple of the projector onto the local space]%
    \label{thm:peps_ground_space_frame_projector}
    \lean{MPSTensor.groundSpaceFrame_isHermitian,
        MPSTensor.isIdempotentElem_smul_groundSpaceFrame,
        MPSTensor.range_groundSpaceFrame,
        MPSTensor.parentInteraction_eq_one_sub_smul_groundSpaceFrame}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_ground_space_frame, def:parent_interaction}
    The matrix of $P_{n}$ is Hermitian. If
    $\Gamma_{n}\Gamma_{n}^{\dagger}\Gamma_{n}=c\,\Gamma_{n}$ for some $c>0$, then
    $c^{-1}P_{n}$ is idempotent with range $\mathcal{S}_{n}$, so it is the
    orthogonal projector onto $\mathcal{S}_{n}$ and the parent interaction is
    $h=\mathbb{1}-c^{-1}P_{n}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    The entries satisfy
    $\overline{\operatorname{tr}[A^{\tau}(A^{\sigma})^{\dagger}]}
        =\operatorname{tr}[A^{\sigma}(A^{\tau})^{\dagger}]$. From
    $\Gamma_{n}\Gamma_{n}^{\dagger}\Gamma_{n}=c\,\Gamma_{n}$ one gets
    $P_{n}^{2}=c\,P_{n}$, and $P_{n}\Gamma_{n}X=c\,\Gamma_{n}X$ shows that the
    range of $P_{n}$ is that of $\Gamma_{n}$. A Hermitian idempotent is the
    orthogonal projector onto its range.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The matrix tensor of a tensor on $\mathbb{C}\lbrack G\rbrack$]%
    \label{def:peps_regular_mps_tensor}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularBondBasis, TNLean.PEPS.regularBondMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBondMatrix_regularAdjoint,
        TNLean.PEPS.trace_regularBondMatrix, TNLean.PEPS.regularMPSTensor}
    \leanok
    For a matrix product tensor $A$ with bond space $\mathbb{C}[G]$, write each
    $A^{i}$ as a matrix in the basis of group elements. In this basis the
    adjoint $(A^{i})^{\dagger}$ is the conjugate transpose, and the parent
    Hamiltonian of $A$ is that of these matrices.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[The local terms for $G$-isometric matrix product states]%
    \label{thm:peps_mps_g_isometric_local_terms}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.sum_trace_concatTensor_mul_trace_regularAdjoint,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometricMPS.groundSpaceMap_comp_regularMPSTensor,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometricMPS.parentInteraction_regularMPSTensor}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_mps_g_isometric, thm:peps_mps_g_isometric_concatenation,
        thm:peps_ground_space_frame_projector, def:peps_regular_mps_tensor}
    Let $A$ be a $G$-isometric matrix product tensor. There is $c>0$ such that
    the operator
    $P_{2}=\sum_{ijkl}\operatorname{tr}[A^{i}A^{j}(A^{k}A^{l})^{\dagger}]
        \ket{ij}\bra{kl}$ is $c$ times the orthogonal projector onto
    $\mathcal{S}_{2}$; the two-site term of the parent Hamiltonian is
    $h=\mathbb{1}-c^{-1}P_{2}$. The source names $P_{2}$ itself the local term,
    while its proof shows that $P_{2}$ is the projector onto $\mathcal{S}_{2}$,
    so $P_{2}$ is $\mathbb{1}-h$ up to the constant
    \cite[Lemma~6.11]{Schuch2010PEPS},
    \cite{gap:scp10_g_isometric_commuting_parent_hamiltonian}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    By Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_mps_g_isometric_concatenation} the two-site tensor
    $C^{ab}=A^{a}A^{b}$ is $G$-isometric with some constant $c$, so
    $\mathcal{P}(C^{\dagger})\mathcal{P}(C)=c\,\sigma$. Since $\mathcal{P}(C)$
    is invariant, $\mathcal{P}(C)\sigma=\mathcal{P}(C)$, and
    $\Gamma_{2}\Gamma_{2}^{\dagger}\Gamma_{2}=\mathcal{P}(C)\mathcal{P}(C^{\dagger})
        \mathcal{P}(C)=c\,\Gamma_{2}$. Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_ground_space_frame_projector}
    applies.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Commuting parent Hamiltonians for $G$-isometric matrix product states]%
    \label{thm:peps_mps_g_isometric_commuting}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularAdjoint_leftRegular,
        TNLean.PEPS.commute_leftRegular_regularAdjoint,
        TNLean.PEPS.sum_trace_mul_smul_regularAdjoint,
        TNLean.PEPS.sum_trace_mul_regularAdjoint_mul_trace,
        TNLean.PEPS.commute_leftRegular_of_conj,
        TNLean.PEPS.sum_trace_regularAdjoint_left,
        TNLean.PEPS.sum_trace_regularAdjoint_right,
        MPSTensor.leftPairLift_mul_rightPairLift_groundSpaceFrame,
        MPSTensor.rightPairLift_mul_leftPairLift_groundSpaceFrame,
        MPSTensor.isNNCPH_of_pairLift_groundSpaceFrame,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometricMPS.pairLift_groundSpaceFrame_regularMPSTensor,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometricMPS.isNNCPH_regularMPSTensor}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_mps_g_isometric_local_terms, def:is_nncph,
        thm:local_term_two_three_ax_xb_lifts,
        thm:three_site_commutator_chain_transport,
        lem:nncph_from_adjacent_two_site_commutation}
    Let $A$ be a $G$-isometric matrix product tensor. On three sites, the
    products of $P_{2}$ acting on the first two sites with $P_{2}$ acting on the
    last two, in either order, both equal $c\,P_{3}$ for some $c>0$. Hence on
    every ring of $N\geq 3$ sites the two-site terms of the parent Hamiltonian
    commute. The source states this theorem for PEPS and writes its proof for
    matrix product states; only the matrix product case is formalized, and
    rings of two sites are not covered
    \cite[Theorem~6.12]{Schuch2010PEPS},
    \cite{gap:scp10_g_isometric_commuting_parent_hamiltonian}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Let $\mathcal{P}(A^{\dagger})\mathcal{P}(A)=c\,\sigma$. For an operator $N$
    commuting with every $L_{g}$ this gives
    $\sum_{b}\operatorname{tr}[A^{b}N]\,(A^{b})^{\dagger}=c\,N$, hence
    $\sum_{b}\operatorname{tr}[M(A^{b})^{\dagger}]\operatorname{tr}[A^{b}N]
        =c\operatorname{tr}[MN]$ for every $M$. The matrices $A^{i}$ commute with
    every $L_{g}$, and so do their adjoints since $L_{g}$ is unitary; so does
    every product of them. The matrix entry of the first product is
    \begin{align}
        \sum_{b}\operatorname{tr}\bigl[A^{s_{0}}A^{s_{1}}(A^{w_{0}}A^{b})^{\dagger}\bigr]
        \operatorname{tr}\bigl[A^{b}A^{s_{2}}(A^{w_{1}}A^{w_{2}})^{\dagger}\bigr],
        \label{eq:peps_mps_g_isometric_three_site_entry}
    \end{align}
    which by cyclicity of the trace is of this form with
    $M=(A^{w_{0}})^{\dagger}A^{s_{0}}A^{s_{1}}$ and
    $N=A^{s_{2}}(A^{w_{2}})^{\dagger}(A^{w_{1}})^{\dagger}$, so it equals
    $c\operatorname{tr}[A^{s_{0}}A^{s_{1}}A^{s_{2}}
            (A^{w_{0}}A^{w_{1}}A^{w_{2}})^{\dagger}]$; the second product is treated
    in the same way. The sum over $g$ in the twirl drops out because $N$
    commutes with every $L_{g}$, which is the step in which the source moves the
    group action from one bond to the other. By
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_mps_g_isometric_local_terms} the local terms are
    $\mathbb{1}-c_{2}^{-1}P_{2}$, so the two translated terms on three sites
    commute; the commutation transports to every three consecutive sites of a
    ring of $N\geq 3$ sites, and terms with disjoint supports commute.
\end{proof}

The source asks for a tensor that is $G$-isometric with respect to the
left-regular representation. In the definition above the representation is a
parameter; the dual quantum-double tensor below is $G$-isometric for its
color-shift representation, and the constant $c$ absorbs the
normalization of the tensor \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.

The invariance of a $G$-injective tensor lets a string of $U_g$'s on the
virtual bonds be deformed across the sites of a network
\cite[Section~``Two dimensions: $G$-injective PEPS'']{Schuch2010PEPS}. To
state this on the torus, orient the horizontal edges to the right and the
vertical edges downwards, as in the source, and let the bonds carry
operators.

\begin{definition}[Torus network with operators on the bonds]%
    \label{def:peps_torus_bond_network}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.torusBondNetwork, TNLean.PEPS.torusLegKernel,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusLegKernel_apply, TNLean.PEPS.torusLegKernel_mul,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusLegMatrix, TNLean.PEPS.torusLegRep,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusLegRep_apply, TNLean.PEPS.torusDress}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_torus_site_tensor}
    Let every site $v$ of the torus carry a tensor $A_v$ with four virtual
    indices, ordered top, right, down, left, and let every bond carry a
    $D\times D$ matrix $O$: $O^{\mathrm h}_v$ on the horizontal bond from $v$
    to $v+(1,0)$ and $O^{\mathrm v}_v$ on the vertical bond from $v+(0,1)$ to
    $v$. Split every bond into its two ends, and give a labelling of the ends
    the weight $O_{ij}$ on each bond whose head reads $i$ and whose tail
    reads $j$. The network is the sum over all labellings of the ends of the
    product of these weights and of the entries of the site tensors, each
    reading the ends of its four legs. For a representation $g\mapsto U_g$ on
    one bond, $U_g$ acts on the virtual system of a site by $U_g$ on the
    incoming legs (top and left) and $U_g^{-1}$ on the outgoing legs (right
    and down), each along the orientation of its edge
    \cite[Section~``Two dimensions: $G$-injective PEPS'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Diagonal operators on the bonds]%
    \label{lem:peps_torus_bond_network_diagonal}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.torusBondNetwork_diagonal,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusBondNetwork_one,
        TNLean.PEPS.stateCoeff_torusSiteTensor_eq_torusBondNetwork}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_torus_bond_network, lem:peps_torus_site_tensor_contraction}
    If every bond carries a diagonal matrix, the network is the sum over one
    index per bond of the product of the diagonal entries and of the site
    tensors. In particular, with the identity on every bond it is the
    contraction of the site tensors, and for $w,h\ge3$ and $A_v=a^{\sigma(v)}$
    it is the coefficient of the torus PEPS of $a$ at $\sigma$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    The weight of a labelling vanishes unless the two ends of every bond
    carry the same index, and the labellings with equal ends correspond to
    the assignments of one index per bond.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Moving bond operators into the site tensors]%
    \label{lem:peps_torus_bond_network_gauge}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.torusBondNetwork_gauge}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_torus_bond_network}
    Let $P_v$ and $Q_v$ be matrices at every site. Replacing the matrix $O$
    of every bond by $P_{\text{head}}\,O\,Q_{\text{tail}}$ gives the same
    network as keeping $O$ and replacing every site tensor $A_v$ by the
    tensor that has absorbed $P_v$ into its incoming legs and $Q_v$ into its
    outgoing legs.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Expand each product $P\,O\,Q$ into its entries. The resulting sum runs
    over the labellings of the bond ends and over new intermediate indices;
    exchanging the roles of the two families, the intermediate indices
    become the labelling of the bond ends and the old labels become the
    summation indices of the absorbed matrices at the sites. The factors
    $P_{v+(1,0)}$ and $Q_{v+(0,1)}$ are reindexed by a unit shift of the
    torus.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Deformation of a string of $U_g$'s]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_operator_string_deformation}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.vecMul_torusLegMatrix_of_comp_eq,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusWestSouthOperatorString,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusNorthEastOperatorString,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusBondNetwork_westSouthOperatorString_eq,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGInjective.torusBondNetwork_westSouthOperatorString_eq}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_g_injective, lem:peps_torus_bond_network_gauge}
    Let $a$ be a four-leg tensor with $\mathcal{P}(a)U_g=\mathcal{P}(a)$ for
    every $g$, the invariance clause of $G$-injectivity for the action of
    Definition~\ref{def:peps_torus_bond_network}. Fix a rectangle of $m<w$
    columns and $n<h$ rows of sites. A string of $U_g$'s carries $U_g$ on
    the vertical bonds, which it crosses going right, and $U_g^{-1}$ on the
    horizontal bonds, which it crosses going down. In the torus network of
    $a$, the string along the left and bottom sides of the rectangle and the
    string along its top and right sides give the same network
    \cite[Section~``Two dimensions: $G$-injective PEPS'', eq.~(2d:move-strings)
        and Lemma~``Deformations of the string'']{Schuch2010PEPS}. Only the
    invariance is used: the source assumes that $U_g$ contains every
    irreducible representation, and its argument, like this one, does not
    use it.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Let $\Phi_v=g^{-1}$ on the sites of the rectangle and $\Phi_v=1$
    elsewhere, and apply Lemma~\ref{lem:peps_torus_bond_network_gauge} with
    $P_v=U_{\Phi_v}$ and $Q_v=U_{\Phi_v}^{-1}$ to the second string. A bond
    with both ends inside or both outside the rectangle carries the identity
    in both strings and keeps it. A
    bond entering the rectangle across its top side is multiplied by
    $U_g^{-1}$ at its head, which cancels the $U_g$ of the second string; a
    bond leaving it across its right side is multiplied by $U_g$ at its
    tail, which cancels its $U_g^{-1}$. A bond entering across the left side
    acquires $U_g^{-1}$ and a bond leaving across the bottom side acquires
    $U_g$, which is the first string. By the invariance every site tensor
    absorbs $U_{\Phi_v}$ on its incoming and $U_{\Phi_v}^{-1}$ on its
    outgoing legs unchanged.
\end{proof}



\begin{definition}[The genuine open tensor of a finite region]%
    \label{def:peps_open_region_contraction}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.RegionIncidentConfig, TNLean.PEPS.regionIncidentBoundaryLabel,
        TNLean.PEPS.isRegionIncidentEdge_of_regionVertex, TNLean.PEPS.regionIncidentWeight,
        TNLean.PEPS.openRegionWeight, TNLean.PEPS.openRegionMap,
        TNLean.PEPS.openRegionMap_apply, TNLean.PEPS.openRegionMap_single,
        TNLean.PEPS.openRegionCoefficientMatrix, TNLean.PEPS.openRegionMap_apply_eq_mulVec,
        TNLean.PEPS.toMatrix_openRegionMap,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionExteriorMultiplicity}
    \leanok
    For a region $R$ in a finite PEPS graph, let $T_R(\mu;\sigma)$ be
    the contraction of its site tensors with physical configuration
    $\sigma$, fixing the crossing-bond labels $\mu$ and summing only
    the internal-bond labels. It defines the linear map from the
    crossing virtual indices to the physical system of $R$.
    No edge wholly outside $R$ enters this contraction. This is the
    finite-region tensor used when blocking and cutting the PEPS in
    \cite[Section~6, proof of Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Exact contraction across a physical cut]%
    \label{thm:peps_open_region_cut}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regionBlockedWeight_eq_exterior_mul_openRegionWeight,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionExteriorMultiplicity_eq_prod,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionInteriorBondProd_eq_exterior_mul_exterior,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionExteriorMultiplicity_ne_zero,
        TNLean.PEPS.stateCoeff_eq_openRegionComplement,
        TNLean.PEPS.stateCoeff_eq_openRegionComplement_of_regularBonds,
        TNLean.PEPS.stateCoeff_rectangle_eq_openRegionComplement_of_regularBonds}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_open_region_contraction}
    Suppose every bond space has positive dimension. The coefficient
    of the PEPS at physical configurations $\sigma$ on $R$ and $\tau$
    on its complement is exactly
    \begin{align}
        \psi(\sigma,\tau)=\sum_\mu T_R(\mu;\sigma)T_{R^c}(\mu;\tau).
        \label{eq:peps_open_region_cut}
    \end{align}
    In particular this holds for regular bonds of any finite group.
    If the region coefficient is instead defined by summing global
    edge assignments, it equals $T_R$ multiplied by the product of
    the dimensions of all edges wholly outside $R$.

    This is an exact finite-region contraction identity. Identifying
    its cut maps with isometries on the regular invariant boundary
    remains a separate step in \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    Split each edge assignment into its incident and exterior parts.
    Exterior labels do not occur in any tensor of $R$, so their sum
    gives the stated multiplicity. Apply the existing coefficient
    identity for the globally indexed region tensors on the two sides.
    Their exterior multiplicities multiply to the product of all
    non-crossing bond dimensions; cancelling this nonzero product
    gives (\ref{eq:peps_open_region_cut}). Regular bond dimensions are $|G|>0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Regular coordinates of the physical cut]%
    \label{def:peps_regular_physical_cut}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.groupBondTensor,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularLabelTranslation,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionBoundaryConfigEquiv,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularOpenRegionMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularOpenComplementMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularPhysicalCutMatrix}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_open_region_contraction}
    Express every bond in a group basis, and enumerate the $b=n+1$
    crossing bonds. Let $A$ and $B$ be the genuine open tensors of the
    region and its complement, with their columns indexed by $G^b$ in
    the same crossing-bond order. Let $K$ be the matrix of physical
    PEPS coefficients on these two systems. These are the actual cut
    maps used before the virtual reduction in
    \cite[Proof of Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Boundary symmetry and projector insertion at the cut]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_open_region_projector}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularLabelTranslation_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionIncidentBoundaryLabel_translation,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionIncidentWeight_translation,
        TNLean.PEPS.openRegionWeight_translation,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularOpenRegionMatrix_translation,
        TNLean.PEPS.matrix_mul_regularBoundaryProjector_of_translation,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularOpenRegionMatrix_mul_projector,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularPhysicalCutMatrix_eq_mul_transpose,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularPhysicalCutMatrix_eq_mul_projector_mul_transpose,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularOpenRegionMatrix_mul_schmidtMatrix_mul_transpose}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_regular_physical_cut, def:peps_regular_boundary_state}
    Suppose the coefficient tensor at every site is invariant under
    simultaneous left translation of all its virtual group labels.
    Then the genuine open tensor of every region is invariant under
    simultaneous left translation of its crossing labels. Consequently
    $AP=A$ and the physical cut satisfies
    \begin{align}
        K=AB^{\mathsf T}=APB^{\mathsf T},\qquad
        ACB^{\mathsf T}=\frac{K}{\sqrt r},
        \quad C=\frac P{\sqrt r},\quad r=|G|^n.
        \label{eq:peps_open_region_projector_cut}
    \end{align}
    In the group bases of regular and dual regular legs, this is the
    inherited boundary symmetry of the proof of
    \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}. It identifies the contraction
    with transport of the virtual boundary vector, up to the scalar
    $\sqrt r$ in (\ref{eq:peps_open_region_projector_cut}). It does not assert isometry of $A,B$ or normalization of
    the physical vector.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_open_region_cut}
    Translate every incident-edge assignment by the same group element.
    This permutes the internal summation and translates the crossing
    labels; local invariance leaves the product of site coefficients
    unchanged. Averaging this symmetry gives $AP=A$. The exact cut
    identity gives $K=AB^{\mathsf T}$, and inserting $P$ and then
    $C=P/\sqrt r$ yields the remaining formulas.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Independent finite choices]%
    \label{thm:peps_finite_indicator_sum}
    \lean{Fintype.prod_sum_boole}
    \leanok
    Let $I$ be a finite set, let $J_i$ be finite sets indexed by $i\in I$,
    and let $P_i$ be a condition on $J_i$. In every commutative semiring,
    \begin{align}
        \prod_{i\in I}\sum_{j\in J_i}\mathbf1_{P_i(j)}
        =\sum_{f\in\prod_{i\in I}J_i}
        \mathbf1_{\{P_i(f_i)\text{ for every }i\in I\}}.
    \end{align}
    Thus multiplying the separate numbers of admissible choices counts
    the admissible simultaneous choices.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
