    Distribute the finite product over the finite sums. A product of
    zero-one indicators is the indicator of the conjunction of their conditions.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The canonical regular-projector region]%
    \label{def:peps_regular_projector_open_region}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.RegionHalfEdgeConfig,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularProjectorOpenRegionMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularSiteMap_regularLegProjector,
        TNLean.PEPS.isGIsometric_regularLegProjector,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularProjectorOpenRegionMatrix_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionIncidentBoundaryLabel_regularGroup_iff}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_open_region_contraction, def:peps_regular_site_gram,
        thm:peps_finite_indicator_sum}
    Let $R$ be a finite region of the original graph, $E_R$ its incident
    edges, and $\partial R$ its crossing edges. Write $E_v$ for the
    incident edges at $v$ and put $\kappa=|G|$. The canonical physical
    configuration is $\alpha=(\alpha_v)_{v\in R}$, with
    $\alpha_v\in G^{E_v}$. The local canonical site map is the regular
    averaging projector $P_v$; it is $G$-isometric with factor one.
    Define the canonical open-region coefficient matrix by
    \begin{align}
        B_R(\alpha,\theta)
         & =\sum_{\substack{\eta\in G^{E_R}                    \\
                \eta|_{\partial R}=\theta}}
        \prod_{v\in R}P_v(\alpha_v,\eta|_{E_v})\notag          \\
         & =\kappa^{-|R|}\sum_{\eta\in G^{E_R}}\sum_{q\in G^R}
        \mathbf1_{\{\eta|_{\partial R}=\theta\}}
        \mathbf1_{\{\alpha_v=q_v\eta|_{E_v}\ (v\in R)\}}.
        \label{eq:peps_projector_region}
    \end{align}
    Every internal bond is summed once, and no exterior bond label
    occurs. Encoding group labels in finite coordinates preserves
    the boundary condition. These are the exposed virtual coordinates
    of \cite[Observation after Lemma~6.3]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Accessibility of an actual regular region]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_open_region_accessibility}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.IsGInjective.exists_regularProjectorCoefficients,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.exists_regularProjectorCoefficients,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGInjective.regularSiteMap_projector_coefficients,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionPhysicalMap_eq_regularProjectorOpenRegionMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionPhysicalProductMatrix_mul_regularOpenRegionMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionPhysicalMap_regularProjectorOpenRegionMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.exists_regularProjectorOpenRegionMatrix}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_regular_projector_open_region, def:peps_g_injective}
    Suppose the local regular site maps $A_v$ are $G$-isometric,
    with positive factors $c_v$. Let $T_R(\theta)$ be the actual
    contracted physical column with crossing labels $\theta$,
    and write $B_R(\theta)$ for the column in
    Definition~\ref{def:peps_regular_projector_open_region}. With
    $L_v=c_v^{-1}A_v^\dagger$, one has
    \begin{align}
        \left(\bigotimes_{v\in R}L_v\right)T_R(\theta)
         & =B_R(\theta),\notag \\
        \left(\bigotimes_{v\in R}A_v\right)B_R(\theta)
         & =T_R(\theta).
        \label{eq:peps_region_accessibility}
    \end{align}
    The reverse identity needs only the local invariance of the
    $G$-injective site maps. More generally, any local matrices
    $F_v$ satisfying $F_vA_v=P_v$ give the forward identity.
    Such matrices also exist for arbitrary regular $G$-injective sites,
    by their local left-inverse property.
    These assertions concern the actual contracted columns and
    do not require the physical maps to be inverse on unused
    coordinates. No region Gram identity is assumed.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_site_gram, thm:peps_region_physical_maps}
    The local identities are
    $c_v^{-1}A_v^\dagger A_v=P_v$ and $A_vP_v=A_v$.
    The first follows from the local regular Gram kernel; the second
    follows from virtual invariance by averaging the group action.
    Apply the physical product maps inside the actual internal-bond
    contraction. Each local coefficient becomes the corresponding
    matrix product, giving~(\ref{eq:peps_region_accessibility}).
    Expanding $P_v$ and distributing the finite products gives
    the translation formula~(\ref{eq:peps_projector_region}).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Physical maps commute with the open-region contraction]%
    \label{thm:peps_region_physical_maps}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regionPhysicalProductMatrix_mul,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionPhysicalProductMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionPhysicalProductMatrix_one,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionPhysicalProductMatrix_conjTranspose,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionPhysicalProductMatrix_isIsometry,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionPhysicalMap,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionPhysicalMap_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionPhysicalMap_comp,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionPhysicalMap_openRegionWeight}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_open_region_contraction}
    For a region $R$, let $F_v$ be a physical matrix at each vertex, allowing
    different input and output dimensions at different vertices. Their product
    matrix is
    \begin{align}
        F_R(\tau,\sigma)=\prod_{v\in R}F_v(\tau_v,\sigma_v).
    \end{align}
    Then $(FL)_R=F_RL_R$, $(F^\dagger)_R=F_R^\dagger$, and identity local maps
    give the identity on the physical space of the region. In particular,
    local isometries give an isometry $F_R$.

    Applying $F_R$ to the actual open-region tensor is the contraction of the
    transformed site coefficients:
    \begin{align}
        (F_R T_R)(\tau,\mu)=
        \sum_{\eta:\eta|_{\partial R}=\mu}
        \prod_{v\in R}\sum_s F_v(\tau_v,s)A_v(s,\eta|_v).
    \end{align}
    This is the physical operation on a block used in
    \cite[\S6.1, accessible virtual coordinates]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    Expand the matrix products and interchange the finite sums over physical
    and virtual labels. The sum over independent physical labels factors at
    each vertex. Composition follows from the same finite distributivity;
    complex conjugation gives the adjoint identity. Applying these identities
    to $F_v^\dagger F_v=\mathbb 1$ proves the isometry assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Local regular Gram kernel]%
    \label{def:peps_regular_site_gram}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularLegRepresentation,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularLegRepresentation_fin,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularLegRepresentation_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularLegRepresentation_unitary,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularLegProjector,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularLegProjector_fin,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularLegProjector_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularSiteMap,
        TNLean.PEPS.toMatrix_regularSiteMap,
        TNLean.PEPS.tensorSiteGram}
    \leanok
    Let $E_v$ be the set of virtual legs incident to a site $v$.
    In the group basis, its coefficient map $A_v$ has entries
    $A_v(s,\eta)=a_v(\eta;s)$, for $\eta\in G^{E_v}$.
    Simultaneous left translation acts on functions by
    $(U_gf)(\eta)=f(g^{-1}\eta)$, and its invariant projector has entries
    \begin{align}
        P_v(\eta,\theta)=\frac1{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}
        \mathbf 1_{\{\eta=g\theta\}}.
    \end{align}
    The local Gram kernel is
    $\sum_s\overline{a_v(\eta;s)}a_v(\theta;s)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Gram formula at an isometric regular site]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_site_gram}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.exists_regularSiteGram,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGInjective.regularSiteMap_translation}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_regular_site_gram}
    If $A_v$ is $G$-isometric, there is $c_v>0$ such that
    \begin{align}
        \sum_s\overline{a_v(\eta;s)}a_v(\theta;s)
        =\frac{c_v}{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}
        \mathbf 1_{\{\eta=g\theta\}}.
        \label{eq:peps_regular_site_gram}
    \end{align}
    Its coefficients are invariant under simultaneous left translation;
    this invariance already follows from $G$-injectivity.
    This is the group-basis form of
    \cite[Definition~6.1]{Schuch2010PEPS}, with the positive scalar
    convention of \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    The defining adjoint identity is $A_v^\dagger A_v=c_vP_v$.
    Evaluating both sides in the group basis gives
    (\ref{eq:peps_regular_site_gram}).
    The identity $A_vU_g=A_v$ gives coefficient invariance.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Expansion of the Gram matrix of a region]%
    \label{thm:peps_open_region_gram_expansion}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.sum_star_regionIncidentWeight_mul,
        TNLean.PEPS.openRegionCoefficientMatrix_gram_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularOpenRegionMatrix_gram_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionIncidentConfigEquiv,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionIncidentBoundaryLabel_regular_iff,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularOpenRegionMatrix_gram_apply_group}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_open_region_contraction, def:peps_regular_site_gram}
    For any finite region $R$, the entry $(\mu,\nu)$ of
    $T_R^\dagger T_R$ is the sum over pairs of incident-edge assignments
    with crossing labels $\mu,\nu$ of
    \begin{align}
        \prod_{v\in R}\sum_s
        \overline{a_v(\eta|_{E_v};s)}a_v(\theta|_{E_v};s).
    \end{align}
    This identity requires no isometry assumption.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    Expand both copies of the region tensor. Interchange the finite
    sums, and distribute the independent physical sums over the
    vertices. Each factor is the corresponding site Gram kernel.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Synchronization of group labels in a connected region]%
    \label{thm:peps_region_group_synchronization}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regionLabels_eq_of_internalEdge_eq,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionLabels_constant_iff_internalEdge_eq,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsRegionLabelCompatible,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsRegionLabelCompatible.internalEdge_eq,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsRegionLabelCompatible.exists_common_label,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsRegionLabelCompatible.exists_translation,
        TNLean.PEPS.isRegionLabelCompatible_iff_exists_translation,
        TNLean.PEPS.sum_regionLabelCompatible_eq_sum_translation}
    \leanok
    Suppose the induced graph on $R$ is nonempty and connected.
    The compatibility equations
    $\eta_e=q_v\theta_e$ at every incident leg hold precisely when
    $q_v=g$ at every vertex and $\eta=g\theta$, for one $g\in G$.
    Thus the sum over compatible vertex translations reduces to
    the sum over simultaneous translations.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    For an internal edge with endpoints $u,v$, the two equations give
    $q_u\theta_e=q_v\theta_e$, hence $q_u=q_v$.
    Equality propagates along paths. Every incident edge has an
    endpoint in $R$, so its label is translated by this common element.
    Nonemptiness makes the assignment $g\mapsto(q_v=g)$ injective.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Counting free internal regular bonds]%
    \label{thm:peps_region_internal_bond_count}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.sum_boundaryRestriction_eq_card,
        TNLean.PEPS.sum_translated_boundaryRestriction_eq_card,
        TNLean.PEPS.sum_boundaryRestriction_translations_eq_card,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionInternalEdgeEquiv,
        TNLean.PEPS.card_regionIncident_not_boundary,
        TNLean.PEPS.sum_regionBoundary_translations_eq_card}
    \leanok
    Fix the crossing configurations $\mu,\nu$ and a simultaneous
    translation $g$. Pairs of incident configurations satisfying
    $\eta=g\theta$ and these prescribed boundary values number
    $|G|^{I_R}$ if $\mu=g\nu$, and zero otherwise, where $I_R$ is
    the number of edges with both endpoints in $R$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    The second configuration has one free label on each internal
    edge, and fixes the first configuration by translation.
    An incident edge that is not a crossing edge has both endpoints
    in $R$, which identifies these free indices with the internal bonds.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Gram operator of a connected regular region]%
    \label{thm:peps_connected_regular_region_gram}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.prod_regularSiteGram_eq_sum_compatible,
        TNLean.PEPS.sum_regularBoundary_translation_eq_card_mul_projector,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionGramScalar,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularOpenRegionMatrix_gram_of_connected}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_open_region_gram_expansion, thm:peps_region_group_synchronization, thm:peps_region_internal_bond_count}
    Let $R$ induce a nonempty connected graph, with $V_R$ vertices
    and $I_R$ internal edges. Suppose the local Gram kernels are
    $A_v^\dagger A_v=c_vP_v$. For unnormalized regular bonds and
    no inserted bond operators, the genuine open-region tensor satisfies
    \begin{align}
        T_R^\dagger T_R=\kappa_R P_{\partial R},\qquad
        \kappa_R=\left(\prod_{v\in R}c_v\right)
        \frac{|G|^{I_R+1}}{|G|^{V_R}}.
    \end{align}
    The boundary may be empty. In particular, positive local Gram
    factors give an isometry on the invariant boundary space after
    dividing by $\sqrt{\kappa_R}$, also when the region contains cycles.
    This is the untwisted connected-region contraction underlying
    \cite[Lemma~6.2 and proof of Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_site_gram}
    Expand the Gram matrix into local pairings. Their averages contribute
    $|G|^{-V_R}$ and a sum over vertex group labels. Compatibility
    reduces this to one common translation. For each such translation,
    the internal labels contribute $|G|^{I_R}$. The remaining boundary
    translation sum is $|G|P_{\partial R}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Physical implementation of a supported canonical unitary]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_physical_unitary_transport}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.exists_positive_regionPhysicalProductMatrix_gram,
        TNLean.PEPS.exists_unitary_regionPhysicalProductMatrix_mul_of_projector_commute}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_region_physical_maps}
    Let $A_v$ be the physical map of a regular $G$-isometric site,
    with arbitrary finite physical dimension, and let $P_v$ be its
    regular invariant projector. On a finite region $R$, put
    $T=\bigotimes_{v\in R}A_v$ and $P=\bigotimes_{v\in R}P_v$.
    There is $c>0$ such that $T^\dagger T=cP$, derived from local
    isometry alone. If a unitary $U$ on the canonical half-edge coordinate space
    satisfies $UP=PU$, then there is a unitary $W$ on the original
    physical region satisfying
    \begin{align}
        WT & =TU.
    \end{align}
    No surjectivity of the site maps is required. This auxiliary
    form of physical accessibility extends the operation to any unused
    physical directions; compare \cite[Lemma~6.3 and \S6.2]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_site_gram}
    Local isometry gives $A_v^\dagger A_v=c_vP_v$, with $c_v>0$.
    Products and adjoints of the product physical maps are computed
    site by site, so $T^\dagger T=cP$, where $c=\prod_{v\in R}c_v$.
    Hence
    \begin{align}
        (TU)^\dagger(TU) & =cU^\dagger PU=cP=T^\dagger T.
    \end{align}
    Equal Gram matrices define an isometry between the two ranges.
    Extend it to a unitary on the finite-dimensional physical space.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Spanning-tree coordinates for regular half-edge labels]%
    \label{def:peps_regular_region_coordinates}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.RootedGroupLabels,
        TNLean.PEPS.rootedGroupLabelsEquiv,
        TNLean.PEPS.rootedTreeGradient,
        TNLean.PEPS.rootedTreeGradientEquiv,
        TNLean.PEPS.RegularRegionCoordinates,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionCoordinatesEquiv,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionCoordinatesEquiv_symm_internal_tail,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionCoordinatesEquiv_symm_internal_head,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionCoordinatesEquiv_symm_boundary_tail,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionCoordinatesEquiv_symm_boundary_head}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_open_region_contraction}
    Let $G$ be a finite group and $R$ a finite region. Choose a spanning
    tree $T$ of its induced graph and a root $o\in R$. Write $I$ for the
    internal edges and $\partial R$ for the crossing edges, and orient
    each internal edge $e=(u,v)$ by the ordering of its endpoints.
    The tree gradient $r\mapsto(r_vr_u^{-1})_{e\in T}$ is a bijection
    from vertex labels satisfying $r_o=1$ to arbitrary labels on $T$.
    Such normalized vertex labels are themselves in bijection with
    arbitrary labels on $R\setminus\{o\}$.

    Physical labels $\alpha_{v,e}\in G$, one for each incident half-edge
    at every $v\in R$, are in bijection with coordinates
    $y\in G^{\partial R}$, $a\in G^I$, normalized vertex labels $r$,
    and $z\in G^{I\setminus T}$. The reconstruction is
    \begin{align}
        \alpha_{v,e} & =r_vy_e
                     &         & (e\in\partial R,\ v\in R),
        \label{eq:peps_region_boundary_coordinates}         \\
        \alpha_{u,e} & =r_ua_e
                     &         & (e=(u,v)\in I),\notag      \\
        \alpha_{v,e} & =
        \begin{cases}
            r_va_e,    & e\in T,            \\
            r_vz_ea_e, & e\in I\setminus T.
        \end{cases}
        \label{eq:peps_region_internal_coordinates}
    \end{align}
    This is a reversible change of regular basis labels associated with
    the accessible virtual systems and blocking operations in
    \cite[\S6.2--6.3]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{definition}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_region_cycle_exponent}
    If two normalized vertex labellings have the same tree gradient,
    their relative label $r_v^{-1}s_v$ is constant along every tree edge.
    Connectedness and the root normalization give $r=s$. A tree has
    $|R|-1$ edges, so the domain and codomain of the gradient each have
    $|G|^{|R|-1}$ elements. Its injectivity therefore gives bijectivity.

    Given $\alpha$, recover $r$ from the tree transports
    $\alpha_{v,e}\alpha_{u,e}^{-1}$. Then set
    $y_e=r_v^{-1}\alpha_{v,e}$ on the boundary,
    $a_e=r_u^{-1}\alpha_{u,e}$ on each internal edge, and
    $z_e=r_v^{-1}\alpha_{v,e}a_e^{-1}$ outside the tree.
    Substitution into
    (\ref{eq:peps_region_boundary_coordinates})--(\ref{eq:peps_region_internal_coordinates})
    recovers every original label. Conversely, the reconstructed tree
    transports equal $r_vr_u^{-1}$, so uniqueness recovers $r$, and then
    the same formulas recover $y$, $a$, and $z$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Canonical regular block in spanning-tree coordinates]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_region_projector_coordinates}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionCoordinates_rootLabels_of_tree_compatibility,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularProjectorOpenRegionMatrix_coordinates,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularProjectorOpenRegionMatrix_exists_normalized_ancilla}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_regular_region_coordinates}
    Let $R$ have a spanning tree $T$ rooted at $o$, and let every local
    tensor be the simultaneous regular averaging projector. Write
    $\alpha(y,a,r,z)$ for the reconstructed half-edge labels in
    (\ref{eq:peps_region_boundary_coordinates})--(\ref{eq:peps_region_internal_coordinates}).
    The actual open-region coefficient matrix $M_R$ satisfies
    \begin{align}
        M_R\bigl(\alpha(y,a,r,z),\theta\bigr)
         & = |G|^{1-|R|}P_{\partial R}(y,\theta)
        \mathbf 1_{\{z=1\}}.
        \label{eq:peps_region_projector_coordinates}
    \end{align}
    No nonempty-boundary assumption is required.

    Let $N$ be the number of pairs $(a,r)$, with $r_o=1$, and put
    $\phi(a,r,z)=N^{-1/2}\mathbf 1_{\{z=1\}}$. Then $\|\phi\|^2=1$,
    and (\ref{eq:peps_region_projector_coordinates}) factors as
    $\gamma P_{\partial R}(y,\theta)\phi(a,r,z)$, where
    $\gamma=|G|^{1-|R|}\sqrt N>0$.
    This is the coefficient form of the regular blocking construction
    in \cite[\S6.2--6.3]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_open_region_projector}
    Expand the original internal-bond contraction into a sum over bond
    labels $\eta$ and vertex translations $q$. On each tree edge
    $e=(u,v)$, compatibility gives
    $r_ua_e=q_u\eta_e$ and $r_va_e=q_v\eta_e$.
    Thus $q_vq_u^{-1}=r_vr_u^{-1}$. The uniqueness of rooted tree
    gradients gives $q_v=r_vx$, where $x=q_o$; this conclusion already
    follows from compatibility on the tree alone.

    Compatibility on the remaining internal edges forces $z_e=1$.
    The bond labels are then uniquely determined by $x$, and boundary
    compatibility is precisely $y=x\theta$. Consequently the coefficient
    is $|G|^{-|R|}\sum_x\mathbf 1_{\{y=x\theta,\ z=1\}}$,
    which is (\ref{eq:peps_region_projector_coordinates}). There are
    exactly $N$ nonzero ancillary coefficients, each of modulus
    $N^{-1/2}$, proving the stated normalization.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Isometry of an actual connected regular block]%
    \label{thm:peps_connected_regular_region_isometry}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularOpenRegionMatrix_invariant,
        TNLean.PEPS.isGIsometric_regularOpenRegionMatrix_of_connected}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_connected_regular_region_gram}
    Let every site be regular $G$-isometric, and let $R$ induce a nonempty
    connected graph. The genuine open-region map $T_R$ is $G$-isometric for
    simultaneous regular translation on the crossing bonds. Its derived
    positive factor is
    \begin{align}
        c_R=\left(\prod_{v\in R}c_v\right)
        |G|^{I_R+1-|R|},
    \end{align}
    where $I_R$ is the number of internal edges. This includes blocks with
    internal cycles and blocks with no crossing bonds. It is the actual
    finite-region form of the stability and blocking argument in
    \cite[Lemma~6.2 and \S6.2]{Schuch2010PEPS}, with the positive-factor
    convention recorded in \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_open_region_projector}
    Translating all virtual labels in the contraction proves boundary
    invariance. The proved connected-region Gram formula gives
    $T_R^\dagger T_R=c_R\Pi_{\partial R}$ with $c_R>0$.
    Dividing the adjoint by $c_R$ gives a left inverse on the invariant
    boundary space. The same identity gives the required inner-product
    identity there.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Removing bond operators on a spanning tree]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_region_tree_gauge}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionTreeGauge_root,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionTreeGauge,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionTreeGauge_gradient,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionGaugeResidual,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionGaugeResidual_transport,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionGaugeResidual_eq_one_of_tree,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionTreeCycleResidual,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionGaugeInternalLabels,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionGaugeInternalLabels_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionGaugePhysicalLabels,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionGaugePhysicalLabels_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionBoundaryTransport,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionBoundaryTransport_tail,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionBoundaryTransport_head,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularTwistedLabels_gauge}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_regular_region_coordinates,
        def:peps_regular_twisted_region}
    Let $G$ be finite, choose a spanning tree $T$ of the region $R$,
    and fix a root $o$. Assign a group operator $u_e$ to each oriented
    edge $e=(s,t)$. There is a unique vertex gauge $k$ with $k_o=1$
    and $k_tk_s^{-1}=u_e$ on $T$. The internal residual operators
    $\omega_e=k_t^{-1}u_ek_s$ therefore equal the identity on $T$;
    their values on the remaining internal edges are unrestricted.

    Internal references and physical half-edge labels have reversible
    transports $\eta'_e=k_s^{-1}\eta_e$ and
    $\alpha'_{v,e}=k_v^{-1}\alpha_{v,e}$. Crossing labels have the
    reversible transport
    \begin{align}
        \theta'_e & =
        \begin{cases}
            k_s^{-1}\theta_e,    & s\in R,\ t\notin R, \\
            k_t^{-1}u_e\theta_e, & s\notin R,\ t\in R.
        \end{cases}
        \label{eq:peps_tree_boundary_transport}
    \end{align}
    On every internal edge,
    $\omega_e\eta'_e=k_t^{-1}u_e\eta_e$.
    More generally, for any ambient vertex labels $K$, set
    $\widetilde u_e=K_t^{-1}u_eK_s$ and
    $\widetilde\eta_{v,e}=K_s^{-1}\eta_{v,e}$.
    If $D_u$ inserts $u_e$ at the head of each edge and leaves its
    tail label unchanged, then
    $D_{\widetilde u}(\widetilde\eta)_{v,e}
        =K_v^{-1}D_u(\eta)_{v,e}$.
    These are group-coordinate identities associated with the reversible
    regular blocking operations in \cite[\S6.2--6.3]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{def:peps_regular_region_coordinates,
        def:peps_regular_twisted_region}
    Invert the tree-gradient bijection at $(u_e)_{e\in T}$ to obtain
    the normalized $k$. On a tree edge,
    $k_t^{-1}u_ek_s=k_t^{-1}(k_tk_s^{-1})k_s=1$.
    The reference identity follows from
    $(k_t^{-1}u_ek_s)(k_s^{-1}\eta_e)=k_t^{-1}u_e\eta_e$.
    Each transport is multiplication by a fixed group element at each
    coordinate, hence is reversible. At a head endpoint the native
    twisted-label identity is
    $(K_t^{-1}u_eK_s)(K_s^{-1}\eta_{t,e})
        =K_t^{-1}u_e\eta_{t,e}$;
    at a tail endpoint both expressions are $K_s^{-1}\eta_{s,e}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Integer fibers and affine lifts of the additive circle]%
    \label{thm:peps_additive_circle_lift_coordinates}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.exists_intCast_eq_of_addCircle_eq_zmod_val,
        TNLean.PEPS.eq_affine_of_addCircle_eq}
    \leanok
    Let $n>0$ be an integer, let $z\in\mathbb Z/n\mathbb Z$, and let
    $\bar z\in\{0,\ldots,n-1\}$ be its standard representative.
    If $x\in\mathbb R$ has the same image as $\bar z$ in
    $\mathbb R/n\mathbb Z$, there is an integer $j$ with $j=x$ and
    $j\equiv z\pmod n$.

    For any real period $p$, let $f:[0,1]\to\mathbb R$ be continuous.
    If its image in the additive circle satisfies $[f(t)]=[f(0)]+[td]$
    for every $t\in[0,1]$, then $f(t)=f(0)+td$ throughout the interval.
    These are auxiliary
    arithmetic and covering-space identities used for the cell and
    edge paths of the block domains in \cite[\S6.3]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    The first equality means that $x-\bar z$ is an integer multiple
    of $n$, so $x$ is an integer with the stated residue. In the
    second assertion, $f$ and the affine path have identical projections
    and agree initially; uniqueness of a covering lift on the connected
    interval makes them equal.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Finite compact cell images]%
    \label{thm:peps_finite_compact_cell_images}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.locallyPathConnectedSpace_iUnion_range}
    \leanok
    Let $K$ be compact and locally path connected, let $Y$ be Hausdorff,
    and let $f_i:K\to Y$ be a finite family of continuous maps. Then
    $\bigcup_i f_i(K)$ is locally path connected.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    The finite disjoint union of copies of $K$ is compact and locally
    path connected. Its natural continuous surjection onto the stated
    union is closed, hence is a quotient map. Local path connectedness
    passes through this quotient map.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Closed-cell regions and geometric coordinate lifts]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_region_realization}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.torusRealProjection_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusRealProjection,
        TNLean.PEPS.continuous_torusRealProjection,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusVertexPoint,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusVertexPoint_eq_projection,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusVertexPoint_intCast,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusVertexPoint_right,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusVertexPoint_up,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusClosedUnitCell,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusRegionRealization,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusVertexPoint_mem_torusRegionRealization,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusRightUnitPath,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusUpUnitPath,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusRightUnitPath_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusUpUnitPath_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.isCompact_torusClosedUnitCell,
        TNLean.PEPS.isCompact_torusRegionRealization,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusRegionRealization_locallyPathConnectedSpace,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusRightUnitPath_mem_torusRegionRealization,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusUpUnitPath_mem_torusRegionRealization,
        TNLean.PEPS.exists_isTorusRegionIntegerLift_of_continuousLift,
        TNLean.PEPS.exists_continuousLift_torusRegionRealization,
        TNLean.PEPS.exists_isTorusRegionIntegerLift_of_isSimplyConnected}
    \leanok
    Let $w,H>0$, let
    $\pi:\mathbb R^2\to(\mathbb R/w\mathbb Z)\times
        (\mathbb R/H\mathbb Z)$ be the quotient map, and let $p_v$
    be the image of the native vertex coordinates. Define the closed
    cell and the realized region by
    \begin{align}
        Q_v & =p_v+\pi([-1/2,1/2]^2), &
        X_R & =\bigcup_{v\in R}Q_v.
    \end{align}
    The cells and their finite union are compact, and $X_R$ is locally
    path connected. Every native site in $R$ lies in $X_R$. The paths
    $t\mapsto p_v+\pi(t,0)$ and $t\mapsto p_v+\pi(0,t)$ have the
    native neighboring sites as their endpoints; whenever both sites
    belong to $R$, the whole path lies in $X_R$, including across a
    periodic seam.

    Any continuous lift $F:X_R\to\mathbb R^2$ of the inclusion
    yields integer coordinates $L:R\to\mathbb Z^2$ which project to
    the native vertices and preserve every internal horizontal and
    vertical unit step. In particular, if $X_R$ is simply connected
    and a site $o\in R$ is fixed, such a continuous lift and such
    integer coordinates exist.

    This closed-cell convention is an auxiliary geometric realization
    of the block domains drawn in \cite[\S6.3]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
    Simple connectedness is the ordinary topological hypothesis on
    the actual cell union; no disk predicate, group flatness assumption,
    or entropy conclusion is introduced. Its relation to arbitrary
    disk regions in Theorem~6.9 remains recorded in
    \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_additive_circle_lift_coordinates, thm:peps_finite_compact_cell_images}
    The quotient projection is continuous, so each projected closed
    square is compact. The finite coproduct of the compact convex
    squares maps continuously and surjectively onto $X_R$. This is a
    closed quotient map into a Hausdorff space, and local path
    connectedness therefore passes to $X_R$. Split a native unit
    path at its midpoint: its first half lies in the initial cell and
    its second half in the final cell.

    Apply covering-space lifting to the two coordinate maps into
    $\mathbb R/w\mathbb Z$ and $\mathbb R/H\mathbb Z$ separately.
    Every lift of a native site has integer coordinates. Along an
    internal unit path, uniqueness of the lift identifies the lifted
    path with the corresponding affine unit segment. Its endpoints
    thus satisfy the exact integer unit-step equations.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The genuine planar lift of the closed-cell region]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_region_planar_lift}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.continuousLift_torusClosedUnitCell,
        TNLean.PEPS.continuousLift_torusRegionRealization_isClosedEmbedding,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusRegionPlanarRealization,
        TNLean.PEPS.range_continuousLift_torusRegionRealization,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusRegionLiftHomeomorph,
        TNLean.PEPS.exists_integerLift_torusRegionPlanarRealization}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_torus_region_realization}
    Let $F:X_R\to\mathbb R^2$ be a continuous lift of the inclusion
    into the real torus. On every native cell,
    \begin{align}
        F(p_v+\pi(d)) & =F(p_v)+d,
                      & d          & \in[-1/2,1/2]^2.
    \end{align}
    The lift is a closed embedding. There are integer site coordinates
    $L(v)=F(p_v)$ which project to the original native vertices and
    preserve every internal unit step, and
    \begin{align}
        F(X_R) & =P_L:=\bigcup_{v\in R}
        \bigl(L(v)+[-1/2,1/2]^2\bigr).
    \end{align}
    Thus $F$ induces a homeomorphism from the actual torus cell union
    to this finite planar cell union.

    This is an auxiliary geometric consequence of the covering lift,
    supporting the block domains of \cite[\S6.3]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
    The homeomorphism transfers simple connectedness to the planar
    union, which is the domain of the exterior contour argument below.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_additive_circle_lift_coordinates}
    The radial segment from $p_v$ to $p_v+\pi(d)$ stays in the same
    closed cell. Uniqueness of its covering lift identifies the lifted
    segment with $t\mapsto F(p_v)+td$. Two neighboring cells have a
    common side midpoint; evaluating its lift from the two cells shows
    that their lifted centers differ by the prescribed unit step.
    The fibers over native sites have integer coordinates. These
    identities identify the image cell by cell. Since $\pi\circ F$
    is the inclusion, $F$ is injective. Compactness of $X_R$ and the
    Hausdorff property of the plane make this continuous injection a
    closed embedding, giving the stated homeomorphism.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Separation of distinct period copies of a lifted region]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_region_period_separation}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.torusRealProjection_periodVector,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusPeriodVector,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusPeriodVector_eq_zero_iff,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusRealProjection_eq_iff_exists_periodVector,
        TNLean.PEPS.continuousLift_torusRegionRealization_periodTranslate_disjoint,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusRegionPlanarRealization_periodTranslate_disjoint,
        TNLean.PEPS.preimage_torusRegionRealization_eq_iUnion_periodTranslate,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusRegionPlanarRealization_cell_periodTranslate_disjoint,
        TNLean.PEPS.exists_integerLift_periodTranslate_disjoint_of_isSimplyConnected}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_torus_region_realization,
        thm:peps_torus_region_planar_lift}
    Let the torus periods $w,H$ be positive, and let
    $F:X_R\to\mathbb R^2$ be a continuous lift of the inclusion.
    For $j=(j_1,j_2)\in\mathbb Z^2$, write
    $\delta_j=(j_1w,j_2H)$. Two plane points have the same projection
    exactly when their difference is some $\delta_j$, and
    \begin{align}
        \pi^{-1}(X_R) & =\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb Z^2}(P_L+\delta_j).
    \end{align}
    If $j\ne0$, then
    \begin{align}
        F(X_R)\cap\bigl(F(X_R)+\delta_j\bigr) & =\varnothing, \\
        P_L\cap(P_L+\delta_j)                 & =\varnothing.
    \end{align}
    In particular, individual closed cells in distinct period copies
    cannot meet, even along a side or at a corner. If the actual cell
    union $X_R$ is simply connected and a site in $R$ is fixed, there
    exists an integer unit-step lift $L$ satisfying this separation
    for every nonzero $j$.

    This is an auxiliary geometric statement for the block domains of
    \cite[\S6.3]{Schuch2010PEPS}. The exterior path-existence argument
    remains separate; see \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    The additive-circle fibers are integer multiples of the two periods.
    Comparing a point with the lift of its projection gives the stated
    union of all period copies. The projection of $\delta_j$ is zero. If
    $F(q)+\delta_j=F(p)$, projecting to the torus gives $q=p$,
    because the lift is a section of the projection. Subtracting
    $F(p)$ then gives $\delta_j=0$. Positivity of both periods
    forces $j=0$, a contradiction. The exact image description in
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_torus_region_planar_lift} gives the statement
    for $P_L$, and restricting to individual cells gives the last
    separation assertion. Simple connectedness supplies the continuous
    lift and hence the required integer coordinates.
\end{proof}

% Geometric follow-up target (SCP10, local source lines 1935--1990):
% Let \Gamma be the native torus graph with both dimensions at least three,
% let \Gamma[R] be nonempty and connected, and suppose X_R is simply connected.
% For every crossing edge f write a_f\in R and b_f\in R^c for its endpoints.
% Given f_0,f and an internal walk P:a_{f_0}\leadsto a_f, prove that there is
% an exterior walk Q:b_{f_0}\leadsto b_f in \Gamma[R^c] such that
% \operatorname{wind}(\iota Q)=
% \operatorname{wind}([b_{f_0},a_{f_0}]*(\iota P)*[a_f,b_f]).
% Here \iota includes an induced-graph walk into \Gamma and * concatenates
% walks in traversal order. Appending the chosen exterior root paths on both
% sides then gives a complement loop with the winding of the combined
% boundary route, whose holonomy is m_f m_{f_0}^{-1}.

\begin{theorem}[No finite holes in simply connected closed-cell regions]%
    \label{thm:peps_integer_cell_no_holes}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.sum_integerGrid_curl_eq_boundary,
        TNLean.PEPS.integerClosedCellUnion,
        TNLean.PEPS.integerClosedCellUnion_locallyPathConnectedSpace,
        TNLean.PEPS.not_finite_integerCellHole_of_isSimplyConnected,
        TNLean.PEPS.infinite_integerCellComplement_component_of_isSimplyConnected,
        TNLean.PEPS.integerClosedCellUnion_image_eq_torusRegionPlanarRealization,
        TNLean.PEPS.exists_integerLift_no_finite_cellHole_of_isSimplyConnected}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_torus_region_realization, thm:peps_torus_region_planar_lift}
    For a finite set $A\subset\mathbb Z^2$, put
    \begin{align}
        P_A & =\bigcup_{a\in A}\bigl(a+[-1/2,1/2]^2\bigr).
    \end{align}
    This union is locally path connected. If $P_A$ is simply connected,
    every component of $\mathbb Z^2\setminus A$ under horizontal and
    vertical unit steps is infinite. More explicitly, no finite set
    $K\subset\mathbb Z^2$ containing a point outside $A$ can have all
    four neighbors of each of its points in $K\cup A$.

    The cancellation used here holds for arbitrary horizontal and
    vertical real edge values $H_a,V_a$: the sum over $K$ of
    $H_a+V_{a+(1,0)}-H_{a+(0,1)}-V_a$ is the corresponding signed sum
    over the exposed boundary edges of its cells.

    Consequently, if the actual torus realization $X_R$ is simply
    connected and $o\in R$, there exist integer coordinates $L$ which
    preserve every native internal unit step and for which all missing
    cell components of the planar realization $P_L$ are infinite.
    Here $P_L=P_A$ for $A=L(R)$, so the conclusion concerns the genuine
    image of the torus covering lift. This is an auxiliary no-hole
    theorem for \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}. The contour
    argument below strengthens it to paths in the exterior collar.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_finite_compact_cell_images}
    Local path connectedness follows from the finite compact-cell
    quotient theorem. Suppose $K$ is such a finite candidate and choose
    a missing cell center in $K$. Translate it to the origin. Simple
    connectedness gives a continuous real lift of the angular map on
    $P_A$. Subtract this lift from the principal argument and divide
    by $2\pi$. At cell corners this gives a real potential whose
    horizontal increments vanish. Its vertical increment is one
    precisely when the edge crosses the negative real ray from the
    missing center, and is zero otherwise.

    The potential is defined along every exposed side of $K$, since
    its neighboring cell belongs to $A$. Summing its square curls
    cancels all interior edges and gives zero. The same boundary
    increments, evaluated by the branch-cut indicator, give total
    curl minus one. This contradiction excludes the finite candidate,
    and hence excludes every finite missing-cell component. Finally,
    the actual covering lift identifies $X_R$ homeomorphically with
    its integer cell union, so the torus conclusion follows.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Safe graph of an exterior integer-cell collar]%
    \label{def:peps_integer_exterior_collar_graph}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.integerCellCenter,
        TNLean.PEPS.integerClosedCell,
        TNLean.PEPS.integerExteriorCollar,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsIntegerCellNear,
        TNLean.PEPS.integerExteriorBand,
        TNLean.PEPS.integerExteriorCollarGraph}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_integer_cell_no_holes}
    Let $A\subset\mathbb Z^2$ be finite, with occupied closed-cell union
    $P_A$. Its genuine exterior collar is
    \begin{align}
        E_A=\bigl(P_A+[-3/4,3/4]^2\bigr)\setminus P_A.
    \end{align}
    The exterior band consists of the unoccupied integer centers $q$
    such that $\|q-a\|_\infty\leq1$ for some $a\in A$.
    Join two such centers by an edge precisely when they are horizontal
    or vertical neighbors and one common occupied center $a\in A$
    satisfies both $\|q-a\|_\infty\leq1$ and $\|r-a\|_\infty\leq1$.
    The common-center requirement controls the whole segment, rather
    than its endpoints alone.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Integer centers of the actual collar]%
    \label{thm:peps_integer_exterior_collar_centers}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.mem_integerClosedCell_iff,
        TNLean.PEPS.mem_integerClosedCell_add_of_abs_le_one,
        TNLean.PEPS.integerCellCenter_mem_closedCellUnion_iff,
        TNLean.PEPS.integerCellCenter_mem_exteriorCollar_iff}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_integer_exterior_collar_graph}
    The closed cell at $a$ consists exactly of the points whose two
    coordinate distances from $a$ are at most $1/2$. An integer center
    belongs to $P_A$ exactly when it belongs to $A$, and belongs to
    $E_A$ exactly when it belongs to the exterior band.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    A distinct integer center cannot be within $1/2$ in both coordinates.
    In the expanded cells the coordinate distances are at most $5/4$;
    integer distances are therefore at most one. Conversely, a distance
    at most one splits into an offset of at most $1/2$ within the cell
    and an offset of at most $1/2$ within the collar square.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Continuous collar paths from safe exterior walks]%
    \label{thm:peps_integer_exterior_collar_paths}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.integerExteriorCollarGraph_segment_subset,
        TNLean.PEPS.joinedIn_integerExteriorCollar_of_reachable}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_integer_exterior_collar_graph}
    Every edge of the safe exterior graph has its entire straight
    segment inside $E_A$. Hence any graph walk between two band centers
    gives a genuine continuous path between them contained in $E_A$.
    This is an auxiliary geometric assertion for
    \cite[proof of Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}. The contour theorem below derives connectivity of the safe graph
    from simple connectedness of the occupied closed-cell union.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_integer_exterior_collar_centers}
    Both endpoint centers lie within coordinate distance one of their
    common occupied center. Convexity preserves these bounds along the
    segment, which is therefore inside the collar enlargement. If a
    closed occupied cell met a horizontal or vertical unit segment,
    integer arithmetic would force its center to be one of the two
    endpoints. Both endpoints are unoccupied, so the segment is outside
    $P_A$. Concatenating the edge segments proves the path assertion.
\end{proof}

\input{chapter/ch24_peps_integer_contact_connectivity}

\begin{definition}[Horizontal-ray potential of a finite edge flow]%
    \label{def:peps_integer_contour_ray_potential}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.integerContourRayPotential}\leanok
    Index each oriented lattice edge by its starting corner
    $a-(1/2,1/2)$, with horizontal edges pointing east and vertical
    edges pointing north. For a finitely supported signed vertical
    flow $V:\mathbb Z^2\to\mathbb Z$, define its ray potential at the
    cell center $q$ by
    \begin{align}
        W(q)=\sum_{\substack{a_2=q_2 \\q_1<a_1}}V(a).
    \end{align}
    The sum is finite because $V$ is finitely supported.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Discrete gradients of the contour ray potential]%
    \label{thm:peps_integer_contour_ray_potential}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.integerContourRayPotential_east,
        TNLean.PEPS.integerContourRayPotential_north}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_integer_contour_ray_potential}
    Write $e=(1,0)$ and $n=(0,1)$. For any finitely supported signed
    vertical flow,
    \begin{align}
        W(q+e)-W(q)=-V(q+e).
    \end{align}
    If a finitely supported horizontal flow $H$ satisfies conservation
    \begin{align}
        H(a)+V(a)-H(a-e)-V(a-n)=0
        \qquad(a\in\mathbb Z^2),
    \end{align}
    then also $W(q+n)-W(q)=H(q+n)$.
    These are auxiliary winding-potential identities for the contour
    argument associated with \cite[proof of Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
    Identification of the flow with an actual exposed contour and
    connectivity of that contour are separate geometric assertions.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    Moving the ray one cell east removes exactly the vertical edge
    indexed by $q+e$. Conservation writes the difference between the
    vertical flow and its north translate as the east translate of
    the horizontal flow minus the horizontal flow itself. Apply the
    additive finite ray sum to this equality. Translation of the
    finite supports and the east-increment identity telescope the
    horizontal difference to the single term $H(q+n)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Connected missing-cell lattice of a simply connected region]%
    \label{thm:peps_integer_cell_exterior_connected}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.integerCellComplement_connected_of_isSimplyConnected,
        TNLean.PEPS.exists_integerLift_integerCellComplement_connected_of_isSimplyConnected}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_integer_cell_no_holes}
    Let $A\subset\mathbb Z^2$ be finite, and suppose its closed-cell union
    $P_A=\bigcup_{a\in A}(a+[-1/2,1/2]^2)$ is simply connected. Then
    the four-neighbor lattice on $\mathbb Z^2\setminus A$ is connected.
    Consequently, a simply connected actual torus region admits an
    integer unit-step lift whose entire missing-cell lattice is connected.

    These are plane-complement walks. They need not stay in the
    exterior collar, so their torus projections are not yet asserted
    to avoid all period copies of the region. The following contour theorem restricts the walks to the genuine
    exterior collar, as required in
    \cite[proof of Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    Enclose $A$ in a finite integer box. All vertices outside that box
    can be connected to one exterior corner by integer row and column
    walks avoiding the box. Every missing-cell component is infinite
    by the preceding no-hole theorem, and hence contains a vertex
    outside the box. Each component therefore meets the same exterior
    component, proving connectedness. The genuine torus lift supplied
    by the no-hole theorem has the same infinitude property, so the
    argument applies to its integer centers as well.
\end{proof}

\input{chapter/ch24_peps_integer_boundary_contour}

\begin{theorem}[Exclusion of foreign period copies from an exterior collar]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_region_exterior_collar}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.disjoint_torusRegionPlanarRealization_add_collar_periodTranslate,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusRealProjection_not_mem_of_mem_planarExteriorCollar,
        TNLean.PEPS.exists_integerLift_exteriorCollar_of_isSimplyConnected}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_torus_region_period_separation,
        thm:peps_torus_region_realization,
        thm:peps_torus_region_planar_lift}
    Let $w,H>0$, and let a genuine continuous lift of the actual
    closed-cell region $X_R$ have planar image
    $P=\bigcup_{v\in R}(L(v)+[-1/2,1/2]^2)$ with integer centers.
    Put $E=[-3/4,3/4]^2$, and let $P_j=P+(wj_1,Hj_2)$ be its period
    copies. Then
    \begin{align}
        (P+E)\cap P_j                   & =\varnothing\qquad(j\neq0), \\
        \pi\bigl((P+E)\setminus P\bigr) & \subseteq X_R^c.
    \end{align}
    Here addition of sets is Minkowski addition. If $X_R$ is simply
    connected and contains a native site, there is an integer unit-step
    lift $L$ with this exterior-collar property; no covering lift is
    supplied in that conclusion.

    This is auxiliary exterior geometry for
    \cite[proof of Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}. Collar paths between exterior integer centers follow from the
    connected safe exterior graph established above.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    Disjoint closed unit squares with integer centers differ by at
    least two in some coordinate; otherwise their midpoint belongs
    to both. A point in an enlarged cell and in a foreign unit cell
    would put the two center coordinates within
    $1/2+3/4+1/2=7/4<2$ in both directions, a contradiction.
    The full preimage of $X_R$ is the union of the period copies $P_j$.
    A point of the exterior collar lies in neither $P$ nor any foreign
    copy, proving the projection assertion. In the simply connected
    case, covering-space lifting supplies the genuine lift and its
    integer centers, so the same argument applies.
\end{proof}

\input{chapter/ch24_peps_torus_integer_cell_lift}

\begin{theorem}[Crossing numbers of projected integer steps]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_integer_unit_step_winding}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.torusDirectedWinding_eq_of_integerUnitStep,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusIntegerDeckCoordinate}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_torus_walk_winding,
        thm:peps_torus_region_integer_lift_gauge}
    Let the torus periods $w,H$ be at least three. For $a\in\mathbb Z^2$,
    put
    \begin{align}
        D(a) & =\left(\left\lfloor a_1/w\right\rfloor,
        \left\lfloor a_2/H\right\rfloor\right).
    \end{align}
    If $a,b$ differ by one horizontal or vertical unit step, then the
    oriented crossing pair of their projected native edge is $D(b)-D(a)$.
    This applies to interior, boundary, and exterior steps alike.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    In a positive horizontal step, Euclidean division changes precisely
    when the standard residue crosses the horizontal seam. The vertical
    case is identical. Reversing a step negates its crossing pair.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Lifted exterior endpoints of a region route]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_region_boundary_lift}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.torusVertexPoint_mem_torusRegionRealization_iff,
        TNLean.PEPS.exists_exterior_integerUnitStep_of_isTorusRegionIntegerLift,
        TNLean.PEPS.exists_exterior_integerEndpoints_of_regionWalk}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_torus_integer_unit_step_winding, thm:peps_torus_region_planar_lift}
    A native site center belongs to the closed-cell union $X_R$ exactly
    when its site belongs to $R$. For the crossing and walk assertions,
    take both torus periods at least three. Suppose $L$ is an integer lift
    preserving the unit steps within $R$, and put
    $P=\bigcup_{v\in R}(L(v)+[-1/2,1/2]^2)$.
    An edge from $v\in R$ to $t\notin R$ lifts to a unit step from
    $L(v)$ to an integer point $b$ projecting to $t$, with
    \begin{align}
        b        & \in (P+[-3/4,3/4]^2)\setminus P, \\
        N([v,t]) & =D(b)-D(L(v)).
    \end{align}
    More generally, let $p:v_0\to v$ be any native region walk, and
    let $t_0,t$ be exterior neighbors of its endpoints. There are lifted
    exterior integer endpoints $b_0,b$ in the same collar, projecting
    to $t_0,t$, such that
    \begin{align}
        N([t_0,v_0]*p*[v,t]) & =D(b)-D(b_0).
    \end{align}
    Concatenation is written in traversal order. This supplies the
    endpoints and winding pair required in the complement argument of
    \cite[proof of Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}; the connected safe exterior graph gives a collar path between them.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_region_walk_winding}
    A site center in a closed cell differs from its integer center,
    modulo a period vector, by an integer in each interval $[-1/2,1/2]$.
    Both differences vanish, proving the center assertion. Lift a boundary
    edge by its horizontal or vertical unit direction. Its exterior
    endpoint is the sum of its midpoint in the interior closed cell and
    a half-unit displacement, so it belongs to the collar. It cannot
    belong to $P$, since projection would put its exterior native center
    in $X_R$. The preceding theorem gives the crossing pair of this step.
    The interior walk has crossing pair $D(L(v))-D(L(v_0))$; adding the
    two boundary crossings cancels the interior endpoint coordinates.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exterior lattice walks from a supplied plane path]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_exterior_path_approximation}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.exists_complementWalk_of_exterior_planePath}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_torus_region_realization,
        thm:peps_torus_walk_winding}
    Let the torus periods $w,H$ be at least three, and let $X_R$ be
    the actual closed-cell realization of a finite native region $R$.
    Let $a,b\in\mathbb Z^2$ project to exterior native vertices $v,t$.
    Suppose a continuous plane path $\gamma$ joins $a$ to $b$ and
    its torus projection avoids $X_R$ at every time. There is a walk
    $Q:v\to t$ in the induced native complement with crossing numbers
    \begin{align}
        N(Q)=\left(
        \left\lfloor\frac{b_1}{w}\right\rfloor
        -\left\lfloor\frac{a_1}{w}\right\rfloor,
        \left\lfloor\frac{b_2}{H}\right\rfloor
        -\left\lfloor\frac{a_2}{H}\right\rfloor
        \right).
    \end{align}
    Thus the lattice walk has the prescribed lifted endpoint displacement.
    This is an auxiliary path approximation for the complement argument
    in \cite[proof of Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}. The path $\gamma$ is supplied in this approximation theorem;
    the preceding contour construction provides such paths for the
    required exterior centers of simply connected regions.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    An exterior point belongs to an open rectangle containing one cell,
    two cells sharing a side, or four cells sharing a corner, all with
    exterior lattice centers. At a side or corner every touching closed
    cell must be exterior, since the point avoids $X_R$. These rectangles
    cover the lifted exterior. Subdivide the compact parameter interval
    so that each subpath lies in one rectangle. Rounded endpoints in
    that rectangle can be connected through exterior lattice centers
    by a horizontal and a vertical step. Concatenating gives an integer
    lattice walk between $a$ and $b$. Projection gives a native
    complement walk. The crossing pair of each unit step is the change
    in its integer deck coordinates; summation telescopes to the stated
    endpoint difference.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Closure gauges from a supplied integer lift]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_region_integer_lift_gauge}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.IsTorusRegionIntegerLift.injective,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsTorusRegionIntegerLift,
        TNLean.PEPS.int_ediv_step_eq_add_seam_indicator,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusRegionLiftGauge,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusRegionLiftGauge_right,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusRegionLiftGauge_up,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusRegionLiftGauge_gradient,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionGaugeResidual_torusRegionLiftGauge,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusRegionLiftRootGauge,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusRegionLiftRootGauge_gradient,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionTreeGauge_eq_torusRegionLiftRootGauge,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionTreeCycleResidual_torusClosure_eq_one}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_region_tree_gauge,
        thm:peps_regular_torus_closure_cut_identification}
    Let both torus circumferences $w,H$ be at least three, and let
    $R$ be a set of native torus vertices. Suppose a supplied map
    $L:R\to\mathbb Z^2$ projects modulo $(w,H)$ to the original
    vertices and preserves every internal rightward and upward unit
    step. In particular, $L$ is injective. The arithmetic deck coordinate
    $\lfloor j/n\rfloor$ increases by one under $j\mapsto j+1$ precisely
    when $j+1\equiv0\pmod n$, and otherwise stays unchanged. For commuting $g,h\in G$,
    define
    \begin{align}
        k_v & =h^{\lfloor L_x(v)/w\rfloor}
        g^{-\lfloor L_y(v)/H\rfloor}.
    \end{align}
    On a native rightward step the gauge changes by left multiplication
    by $h$ precisely at the horizontal seam; on an upward step it
    changes by left multiplication by $g^{-1}$ precisely at the vertical
    seam. For every internal edge, ordered from $s$ to $t$,
    \begin{align}
        k_tk_s^{-1} & =u_e,\qquad k_t^{-1}u_ek_s=1,
    \end{align}
    where the native closure assignment is $h^{-1}$ on a horizontal
    seam edge, $g$ on a vertical seam edge, and the identity elsewhere.
    Right normalization $k_v\mapsto k_vk_o^{-1}$ preserves the gradient.
    For finite $G$, this normalized gauge equals the gauge obtained from
    any spanning tree rooted at $o$; hence all its cycle residuals equal
    the identity.

    This is an auxiliary consequence of the supplied arithmetic lift,
    supporting the seam and disentangling arguments in
    \cite[\S5.3 and Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}. It neither defines a
    disk nor establishes lifts for arbitrary disk regions. The latter
    geometric theorem and the removal of sector-dependent crossing
    transports remain separate, as recorded in
    \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    Euclidean division shows that the quotient of an integer coordinate
    increases by one exactly when its residue wraps through zero.
    The unit-step hypotheses therefore give the two gauge-step
    identities; commutativity moves the vertical factor past the
    horizontal factor. A seam reverses the native ordered-edge
    orientation, yielding $h^{-1}$ and $g$ in the respective gradients.
    All other internal gradients are the identity. Right normalization
    cancels in each gradient. Uniqueness in the tree-gradient bijection
    identifies the normalized gauge with the spanning-tree gauge.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Twisted canonical block in spanning-tree coordinates]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_twisted_projector_coordinates}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularProjectorTwistedRegionMatrix_coordinates}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_regular_region_coordinates,
        thm:peps_regular_region_tree_gauge,
        def:peps_regular_projector_twisted_region}
    Choose a spanning tree $T$ rooted at $o$ in the finite region $R$,
    and insert arbitrary operators $u_e\in G$ on its incident bonds.
    Let $k_o=1$, $k_tk_s^{-1}=u_e$ on $T$, and
    $\omega_e=k_t^{-1}u_ek_s$ on the internal edges.
    Let $\theta'$ be the crossing configuration transported by
    (\ref{eq:peps_tree_boundary_transport}). In the original physical
    coordinates $\alpha(y,a,r,z)$, the actual twisted canonical matrix satisfies
    \begin{align}
        M_R^u\bigl(\alpha(y,a,r,z),\theta\bigr)
         & = |G|^{-|R|}\sum_{x\in G}
        \mathbf 1_{\{y=x\theta'\}}
        \mathbf 1_{\{z_e=x\omega_ex^{-1}\ (e\in I\setminus T)\}}.
        \label{eq:peps_twisted_projector_coordinates}
    \end{align}
    The reference labels $a$ and rooted vertex labels $r$ do not affect
    the coefficient. Operators on crossing bonds are allowed, and no
    condition is imposed on the internal residuals or the size of the
    boundary. This is an actual finite-contraction form of the regular
    coordinate changes in \cite[\S6.2--6.3]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    Expand the original contraction into its internal bond labels
    $\eta$ and local translations $q$. On the tree put
    $q'_v=q_vk_v$ and $\eta'_e=k_s^{-1}\eta_e$.
    The endpoint equations become
    $r_sa_e=q'_s\eta'_e$ and $r_ta_e=q'_t\eta'_e$.
    Rooted tree-gradient uniqueness yields $q'_v=r_vx$, with $x=q_o$.
    Thus $q_v=r_vxk_v^{-1}$, and internal references are uniquely fixed
    as $\eta_e=k_sx^{-1}a_e$. On each remaining internal edge the
    head equation is equivalent to $z_e=x\omega_ex^{-1}$.

    At a crossing edge, the unique reference equals
    $k_sx^{-1}y_e$ when $s\in R$, and
    $u_e^{-1}k_tx^{-1}y_e$ when $t\in R$.
    Fixing these references to $\theta$ is exactly $y=x\theta'$.
    Conversely, these boundary and cycle equations reconstruct a
    compatible pair $(\eta,q)$. Hence the compatible pairs are in
    bijection with the surviving $x\in G$, and the original local
    averages contribute the factor $|G|^{-|R|}$ in
    (\ref{eq:peps_twisted_projector_coordinates}).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Mixed Gram matrices of twisted canonical blocks]%
    \label{thm:peps_twisted_projector_cross_gram_coordinates}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularProjectorTwistedRegionMatrix_crossGram_coordinates,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularProjectorTwistedRegionMatrix_crossGram_eq_zero_of_cycles_not_conjugate}
    \leanok
    Fix a region $R$, a spanning tree, and a root, and let $u,w$ be
    arbitrary bond insertions, including crossing operators. Write
    $\omega^u,\omega^w$ for their cycle residual tuples and
    $b_u,b_w$ for their respective boundary transports. Let $N$ be
    the number of pairs $(a,r)$ of internal references and vertex
    labels normalized at the root. The actual canonical matrices obey
    \begin{align}
        \bigl((M_R^u)^\dagger M_R^w\bigr)(\theta,\vartheta)
         & = |G|^{-2|R|}N\sum_{x,t\in G}
        \mathbf 1_{\{x b_u(\theta)=t b_w(\vartheta)\}}
        \mathbf 1_{\{x\omega^ux^{-1}=t\omega^wt^{-1}\}}.
        \label{eq:peps_twisted_projector_cross_gram}
    \end{align}
    In particular, if no $s\in G$ satisfies
    $\omega^u=s\omega^ws^{-1}$ coordinatewise, then
    $(M_R^u)^\dagger M_R^w=0$. Thus these two actual blocks have
    orthogonal physical ranges. The boundary may be empty; neither
    flatness nor a mixed Gram identity is assumed.
    This is a finite-region comparison supporting the coherent closure
    arguments in \cite[\S6.3--6.4]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_twisted_projector_coordinates}
    Reindex the physical row sum by the reversible spanning-tree
    coordinates and substitute
    (\ref{eq:peps_twisted_projector_coordinates}) for each coefficient.
    The factors indexed by $(a,r)$ are constant, contributing $N$.
    Summing over the shared boundary and cycle coordinates pairs the
    two indicators, leaving precisely the equalities in
    (\ref{eq:peps_twisted_projector_cross_gram}).
    Any surviving cycle equality implies
    $\omega^u=(x^{-1}t)\omega^w(x^{-1}t)^{-1}$.
    If simultaneous conjugacy fails, every summand vanishes.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Vertex gauges in an actual regular-region contraction]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_region_gauge_contraction}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionGaugeIncidentLabels_boundary,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionGaugeEdgeOperators,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionGaugeIncidentLabels,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionGaugeIncidentLabels_twisted,
        TNLean.PEPS.openRegionWeight_regularRegionGauge,
        TNLean.PEPS.openRegionWeight_eq_untwisted_of_regularRegionGaugeResidual_eq_one}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_regular_twisted_region, def:peps_open_region_contraction}
    Suppose each original site coefficient is invariant under simultaneous
    left translation of its incident regular labels. Let $k_v\in G$ be
    arbitrary gauges at the vertices of a finite region $R$, and let $u_e$
    be arbitrary inserted operators, including those on crossing bonds.
    Put $\widetilde u_e=k_t^{-1}u_ek_s$ on internal edges $e=(s,t)$,
    and put $\widetilde u_e=1$ on all other edges. With the transported
    crossing configuration $\theta'$ of
    (\ref{eq:peps_tree_boundary_transport}), the actual physical columns satisfy
    \begin{align}
        T_R^u(\theta) & =T_R^{\widetilde u}(\theta').
    \end{align}
    If every internal residual is the identity, this reduces to
    $T_R^u(\theta)=T_R(\theta')$ for the original untwisted region.
    No connectedness or Gram identity is required. These identities
    implement the regular-coordinate changes in
    \cite[\S6.2--6.3]{Schuch2010PEPS} inside the original finite contraction.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_region_tree_gauge}
    Reindex the actual sum over incident edge references. On an internal
    edge multiply the reference by $k_s^{-1}$; on a crossing edge use
    the transport in (\ref{eq:peps_tree_boundary_transport}). Each change
    is a bijection. Its boundary restriction is exactly $\theta\mapsto\theta'$.
    At every region vertex, the resulting twisted half-edge labels are
    $k_v^{-1}$ times the original twisted labels. Local simultaneous
    invariance therefore leaves each site coefficient unchanged, and
    hence leaves their product and the contracted sum unchanged.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The transported boundary projector]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_region_gauge_boundary}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionBoundaryGaugeEquiv_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionBoundaryGaugeEquiv,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionGaugeBoundaryProjector,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionGaugeBoundaryProjector_mul_self,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionGaugeBoundaryProjector_conjTranspose,
        TNLean.PEPS.rank_regularRegionGaugeBoundaryProjector,
        TNLean.PEPS.trace_regularRegionGaugeBoundaryProjector}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_region_tree_gauge}
    Enumerate the crossing bonds of a region by $\{1,\ldots,b\}$, and let
    $F$ be the resulting permutation of boundary configurations induced
    by the vertex gauge and the crossing operators. The pulled-back
    averaging projector is
    \begin{align}
        \Pi_{\partial R}^{k,u}(x,y)=\Pi_{\partial R}(F(x),F(y)).
    \end{align}
    It is idempotent. If $b\geq1$, it is self-adjoint, has rank
    $|G|^{b-1}$, and has trace $|G|^{b-1}$. This coordinate change is
    part of the boundary disentangling argument of
    \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}; it imposes no condition on
    internal twists.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Reindex the common summation variable in the square of the matrix
    by $F$. Idempotence then follows from that of the original averaging
    projector. Taking the adjoint commutes with applying the same
    permutation to both indices. Finally, a basis permutation preserves
    rank, and reindexing the diagonal sum preserves trace.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The actual Gram matrix with removable internal operators]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_region_gauge_gram}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularTwistedOpenRegionMatrix_regularRegionGauge,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularTwistedOpenRegionMatrix_eq_submatrix_of_regularRegionGaugeResidual_eq_one,
        TNLean.PEPS.exists_positive_gram_regularTwistedOpenRegionMatrix_of_regularRegionGaugeResidual_eq_one,
        TNLean.PEPS.rank_regularTwistedOpenRegionMatrix_of_regularRegionGaugeResidual_eq_one}
    \leanok
    Number the crossing bonds and write $F$ for the boundary permutation
    induced by an arbitrary vertex gauge $k$ and inserted operators $u$.
    If the original sites have simultaneous regular invariance, the actual
    ordered coefficient matrices satisfy
    \begin{align}
        M_R^u(\sigma,x) & =M_R^{\widetilde u}(\sigma,F(x)).
    \end{align}
    Suppose further that $R$ is connected, the sites are $G$-isometric,
    and every internal residual $k_t^{-1}u_ek_s$ is the identity.
    Then $M_R^u(\sigma,x)=M_R(\sigma,F(x))$ and there is a positive
    scalar $c_R$ such that
    \begin{align}
        (M_R^u)^\dagger M_R^u & =c_R\Pi_{\partial R}^{k,u}.
    \end{align}
    If the boundary contains $b>0$ bonds, the actual matrix has rank
    $|G|^{b-1}$. The Gram statement allows an empty boundary and derives the block Gram
    identity from the local sites. It is the removable-operator case of
    the boundary-isometry argument in \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS},
    not the unrestricted disk theorem
    \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_region_gauge_contraction, thm:peps_regular_region_gauge_boundary, thm:peps_connected_regular_region_isometry}
    The ordered coefficient identities follow from the actual contraction
    identity in Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_regular_region_gauge_contraction}.
    With identity internal residuals, the matrix is the untwisted block
    with its columns permuted by $F$. Local regular isometry and
    connectedness give $M_R^\dagger M_R=c_R\Pi_{\partial R}$ with $c_R>0$.
    Pulling both boundary indices back along $F$ therefore gives the
    claimed Gram matrix. Its rank equals the rank of the actual matrix
    because $\operatorname{rank}(M^\dagger M)=\operatorname{rank}(M)$;
    the positive scalar does not change the rank of the boundary projector.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Actual closure-region Grams from an integer lift]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_region_integer_lift_gram}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.regularTwistedOpenRegionMatrix_torusRegionLift,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.exists_positive_gram_torusClosureRegionMatrix_of_integerLift,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.rank_torusClosureRegionMatrix_of_integerLift}
    \leanok
    Let the torus circumferences be at least three, and let $A$ be a
    native regular $G$-isometric four-leg tensor. Equip a region $R$ with
    an integer lift preserving all internal unit steps, and let $g,h$
    commute. For the actual region matrix $M_R^{g,h}$ of the native
    closure, the explicit lift gauge $k$ gives
    \begin{align}
        M_R^{g,h}(\sigma,x) & =M_R(\sigma,F_{g,h}(x)).
    \end{align}
    If $R$ is connected, there is a positive scalar $c_R$ such that
    \begin{align}
        (M_R^{g,h})^\dagger M_R^{g,h}
         & =c_R\Pi_{\partial R}^{k,u(g,h)}.
    \end{align}
    When the boundary has $b>0$ bonds,
    $\operatorname{rank}M_R^{g,h}=|G|^{b-1}$.
    These conclusions follow from the supplied arithmetic lift, without
    any separate flatness or block Gram hypothesis. They support the
    seam-deformation argument of \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS};
    they do not establish lifts for arbitrary disk regions or identify
    boundary transports for superpositions of distinct closures
    \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_torus_region_integer_lift_gauge, thm:peps_regular_region_gauge_gram, thm:peps_regular_torus_closure_cut_identification}
    Express the native four-leg tensor on its four incident graph edges.
    This preserves local regular isometry. The arithmetic lift gauge
    has the native closure assignment as its internal gradient, so
    every internal residual equals the identity. Apply
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_regular_region_gauge_gram} to the resulting
    actual region matrix, with the boundary transport of this explicit gauge.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Actual open-region rank from geometric simple connectedness]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_simply_connected_region_rank}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.rank_torusClosureRegionMatrix_of_isSimplyConnected}
    \leanok
    Let both torus circumferences be at least three and let $A$ be a
    native regular $G$-isometric four-leg tensor. Suppose the induced
    native graph on a finite region $R$ is connected and the actual
    closed-cell realization of $R$ in the real torus is simply connected.
    For a commuting closure pair $g,h$ and a numbering of its $b>0$
    crossing bonds, the actual twisted open-region coefficient matrix satisfies
    \begin{align}
        \operatorname{rank}M_R^{g,h} & =|G|^{b-1}.
    \end{align}
    No arithmetic lift, residual-flatness condition, or block Gram
    identity is supplied. Geometric simple connectedness and native
    graph connectivity are separate assumptions: closed cells may
    meet at diagonal points. This is an auxiliary open-region rank
    consequence of the boundary calculation in
    \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}, not the full disk entropy theorem
    or a coherent-superposition result
    \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_torus_region_realization, thm:peps_torus_region_integer_lift_gram}
    Graph connectivity supplies a region vertex. Simple connectedness
    of the actual cell realization gives an integer lift preserving
    every internal native unit step by
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_torus_region_realization}. Apply the actual
    integer-lifted region rank theorem,
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_torus_region_integer_lift_gram}, to this lift.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Vertex gauges of a closed regular PEPS]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_closed_gauge}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.sameState_regularVertexGauge,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularVertexGaugeOperators}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_region_gauge_contraction}
    Suppose the site coefficients are invariant under simultaneous left
    translation of their incident group labels. For arbitrary vertex
    gauges $k_v\in G$, replacing the inserted bond operators by
    \begin{align}
        u_e' = k_t^{-1}u_e k_s,\qquad e=(s,t),
    \end{align}
    leaves every coefficient of the closed physical PEPS unchanged.
    Neither connectedness nor isometry is required. This is the
    regular-basis gauge identity underlying deformation of closure
    strings in \cite[\S5.3]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    In the original finite sum, replace the reference label of each
    oriented bond by $k_s^{-1}$ times that label. At its tail, the
    resulting half-edge label is multiplied by $k_s^{-1}$; at its head,
    cancellation gives multiplication by $k_t^{-1}$. Thus every site's
    incident labels have undergone one common translation, which leaves
    its coefficient unchanged. The change of summed labels is bijective.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Nonabelian holonomy along native walks]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_walk_holonomy}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularDirectedTransport_of_lt,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularDirectedTransport,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularDirectedTransport_of_gt,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularDirectedTransport_symm,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularDirectedTransport_gauge,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularWalkHolonomy,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularWalkHolonomy_nil,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularWalkHolonomy_cons,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularWalkHolonomy_append,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularWalkHolonomy_reverse,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularWalkHolonomy_gauge,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularWalkHolonomy_gauge_loop,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularWalkHolonomy_gradient,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularWalkHolonomy_gradient_loop}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_closed_gauge}
    Let $G$ be any group, and assign $u_e\in G$ to each edge of a graph
    with ordered vertices. A directed step along $e=(s,t)$ uses $u_e$
    from $s$ to $t$ and $u_e^{-1}$ in the reverse direction. For a walk
    $p$, let $H(u,p)$ be the product of these operators in reverse
    traversal order; the empty walk has product one. If $p:v\to w$
    and $q:w\to z$, and if $u'_e=k_t^{-1}u_ek_s$, then
    \begin{align}
        H(u,pq)     & =H(u,q)H(u,p),      \\
        H(u,p^{-1}) & =H(u,p)^{-1},       \\
        H(u',p)     & =k_w^{-1}H(u,p)k_v.
        \label{eq:peps_walk_gauge}
    \end{align}
    In particular a closed-walk holonomy transforms by conjugation at
    its base vertex. A gradient assignment $u_e=k_tk_s^{-1}$ has
    $H(u,p)=k_wk_v^{-1}$ and hence identity holonomy on closed walks.
    These are group identities underlying the seam deformation and
    accessible complement cycle calculations in
    \cite[\S5.3 and Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}; no boundary-word
    relation or tensor factorization is assumed.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Reversing a directed edge takes its inverse. The concatenation and
    reversal formulas follow by induction on the walk. The directed
    gauged operator from $v$ to $w$ is $k_w^{-1}u_{v\to w}k_v$.
    In the reverse-ordered product, adjacent endpoint gauges cancel,
    leaving (\ref{eq:peps_walk_gauge}). For a gradient, the factors
    telescope directly to $k_wk_v^{-1}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Closure holonomy from oriented seam crossing numbers]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_walk_winding}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.torusDirectedWinding_right,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusEdgeWinding,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusDirectedWinding,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusWalkWinding,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusDirectedWinding_up,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusDirectedWinding_symm,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusWalkWinding_append,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusWalkWinding_reverse,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularWalkHolonomy_torusClosureEdgeAssignment,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularWalkHolonomy_torusClosure_eq_of_winding_eq}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_walk_holonomy, thm:peps_regular_torus_closure_cut_identification}
    Consider the native torus graph with both periods at least three.
    For a directed step, count $+1$ at a rightward horizontal seam
    crossing and at an upward vertical seam crossing, count $-1$ in
    the respective reverse directions, and count zero elsewhere.
    Let $N(p)=(n(p),m(p))\in\mathbb Z^2$ be the sum of these pairs
    along an actual walk $p$. Concatenation and reversal satisfy
    $N(pq)=N(p)+N(q)$ and $N(p^{-1})=-N(p)$.

    Let $g,h\in G$ commute, and let $u(g,h)$ be the actual ordered-edge
    closure assignment. Then
    \begin{align}
        H(u(g,h),p)=h^{n(p)}(g^{-1})^{m(p)}.
        \label{eq:peps_winding_holonomy}
    \end{align}
    Consequently any two walks with equal seam crossing numbers have
    equal closure transports, even when their endpoints differ.
    This is the walk identity used in the seam deformation and
    complement construction of
    \cite[\S5.3 and proof of Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Both native seam orientations are opposite to their ordered-edge
    orientations. Thus the rightward seam operator is $h$ and the
    upward seam operator is $g^{-1}$. Reversing a directed step
    negates its crossing numbers and inverts its operator. Every step
    therefore satisfies~(\ref{eq:peps_winding_holonomy}). Induction on
    the walk adds the integer exponents; commutativity of $g$ and $h$
    permits the two powers to be grouped. The addition and reversal
    rules for $N$ follow by induction from the corresponding rules for
    directed steps.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Region walk crossing numbers from geometric lifts]%
    \label{thm:peps_region_walk_winding}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.torusWalkWinding_of_isTorusRegionIntegerLift,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusWalkWinding_eq_of_isSimplyConnected}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_torus_walk_winding,
        thm:peps_torus_region_integer_lift_gauge,
        thm:peps_regular_boundary_route,
        thm:peps_torus_region_realization}
    Let $L:R\to\mathbb Z^2$ be an integer lift preserving each native
    horizontal and vertical unit step, and write the torus periods as
    $W$ and $H$. For any actual induced-region walk $p:v\to t$,
    its ambient seam crossing numbers are
    \begin{align}
        N(p)=\left(
        \left\lfloor\frac{L_1(t)}{W}\right\rfloor
        -\left\lfloor\frac{L_1(v)}{W}\right\rfloor,
        \left\lfloor\frac{L_2(t)}{H}\right\rfloor
        -\left\lfloor\frac{L_2(v)}{H}\right\rfloor
        \right).
        \label{eq:peps_region_deck_winding}
    \end{align}
