    If the actual closed-cell realization of $R$ is simply connected,
    any two induced-region walks with the same endpoints have equal
    seam crossing numbers; no integer lift is supplied in this case.
    These are auxiliary interior-walk identities for the path argument
    in \cite[proof of Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Use the additive group $\mathbb Z^2$, writing its operation
    multiplicatively, and take the universal commuting closure elements
    $g=(0,-1)$ and $h=(1,0)$. Its actual walk transport is $N(p)$ by
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_torus_walk_winding}. The lift gauge is the pair
    of integer deck coordinates. Its native gradient is the actual
    closure assignment on every internal edge, so its walk transport
    is the difference of the endpoint deck coordinates. Inclusion of
    the induced walk preserves the native transport, giving
    (\ref{eq:peps_region_deck_winding}). In the simply connected case,
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_torus_region_realization} supplies the lift;
    applying (\ref{eq:peps_region_deck_winding}) to both walks gives
    equality.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Actual walk words in the region cycle labels]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_region_cycle_holonomy}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionCycleWord_append,
        TNLean.PEPS.RegionCycleEdge,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionInternalOperators,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionCycleOperators,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionCycleWord,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionCycleWord_reverse,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionCycleWord_eval,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionCycleWord_eval_conj,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionCycleOperators_treeGauge,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionCycleWord_eval_treeResidual,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionCycleWord_eval_rootLoop,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularWalkHolonomy_eq_cycleWord_eval}
    \leanok
    Let $T$ be a chosen spanning tree of an induced region graph, rooted
    at $o$, and let $C$ be its native internal edges outside $T$.
    For an actual region walk $p$, construct a word $W_T(p)$ in the
    free group on $C$: traverse $p$, use the generator or its inverse
    on each non-tree edge according to its orientation, omit tree edges,
    and multiply in reverse traversal order. Given any tuple $z\in G^C$,
    write $\operatorname{ev}_z$ for evaluation of this word. Assigning
    identity operators to tree edges and $z_e$ to the remaining edges
    gives actual walk holonomy equal to $\operatorname{ev}_zW_T(p)$.
    Concatenation satisfies $W_T(pq)=W_T(q)W_T(p)$ and reversal satisfies
    $W_T(p^{-1})=W_T(p)^{-1}$.

    For arbitrary native bond operators $u$, let $k$ be the existing
    root-normalized tree gauge and $\omega$ its cycle residual tuple.
    Its reduced assignment is precisely identity on the tree and
    $\omega_e$ on $C$. For $p:v\to w$ one therefore has
    \begin{align}
        \operatorname{ev}_{\omega}W_T(p) & =k_w^{-1}H(u,p)k_v,                           \\
        H(u,p)                           & =k_w\operatorname{ev}_{\omega}W_T(p)k_v^{-1}, \\
        \operatorname{ev}_{xzx^{-1}}W_T(p)
                                         & =x\operatorname{ev}_zW_T(p)x^{-1}.
    \end{align}
    For a loop based at $o$, normalization gives
    $\operatorname{ev}_{\omega}W_T(p)=H(u,p)$.
    Word construction and conjugation require no finiteness or
    commutativity; the existing normalized tree gauge is used for finite $G$.
    These are actual walk-word identities for the accessible complement
    cycle argument in \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}. They do not
    assert existence of walks representing a prescribed boundary transport.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_walk_holonomy, thm:peps_regular_region_tree_gauge}
    Apply the native walk product to the free generators. Evaluation
    commutes with each product and inverse, giving the residual holonomy.
    The tree gauge removes each tree operator and retains exactly the
    prescribed cycle residual on every other edge. Endpoint gauge
    covariance gives the first two identities. Conjugating every
    residual operator conjugates the whole ordered product. At the
    normalized root the endpoint gauges are both the identity.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Cycle-controlled permutation of actual boundary coordinates]%
    \label{thm:peps_cycle_controlled_boundary}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularCycleControlledBoundaryMatrix_mem_unitaryGroup,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularCycleControlledBoundary,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularCycleControlledBoundaryMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularCycleControlledBoundaryMatrix_mul_coordinates,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularComplementWalkControlMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularComplementWalkControlMatrix_mem_unitaryGroup}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_regular_region_coordinates}
    Fix spanning-tree physical coordinates $(y,a,r,z)$ of a finite
    region and any function $W:G^C\to G^{\partial R}$ of its cycle
    labels. The permutation
    $\sigma_W(y,a,r,z)=(W(z)^{-1}y,a,r,z)$ is reversible, with inverse
    $(y,a,r,z)\mapsto(W(z)y,a,r,z)$, and its matrix $U_W$ on the
    original half-edge physical basis is unitary. In particular, fixed
    actual complementary walks define such a matrix by evaluating their
    cycle words; this construction is independent of all bond assignments.

    For the actual canonical block $M_R^u$, let $b_u$ be its boundary
    transport and $\omega$ its cycle residual tuple. Its transformed
    coefficients satisfy
    \begin{align}
        (U_WM_R^u)\bigl(\alpha(y,a,r,z),\theta\bigr)
         & =|G|^{-|R|}\sum_{x\in G}
        \mathbf 1_{\{y=W(z)^{-1}x b_u(\theta)\}}
        \mathbf 1_{\{z=x\omega x^{-1}\}}.
        \label{eq:peps_cycle_controlled_coefficients}
    \end{align}
    The cycle constraint is retained without any assumption on the
    relation between $W$ and the crossing transports.
    This is an algebraic coordinate operation associated with
    \cite[\S6.3]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_twisted_projector_coordinates}
    The inverse translates only $y$, so it leaves its own controlling
    tuple $z$ fixed. Group cancellation proves the inverse identities.
    A permutation of the physical group basis is unitary. Its action
    on coefficient functions is precomposition with $\sigma_W^{-1}$;
    substituting $W(z)y$ for $y$ in
    (\ref{eq:peps_twisted_projector_coordinates}) gives
    (\ref{eq:peps_cycle_controlled_coefficients}).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Combined boundary transport removal from supplied complement walks]%
    \label{thm:peps_cycle_controlled_relative_walks}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularCycleControlledBoundaryMatrix_mul_coordinates_of_combined_relative_walk_words}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_cycle_controlled_boundary,
        thm:peps_regular_region_cycle_holonomy,
        thm:peps_regular_boundary_route}
    Let $S$ be the complement of $R$, and identify their crossing bonds
    by the same native edges. Choose a region gauge $k_R$ and a rooted
    spanning tree in $S$, with normalized gauge $k_S$ and cycle residuals
    $\omega_S$. Let $F_R,F_S$ be the actual boundary transports and set
    $M=F_S\circ F_R^{-1}$ under this crossing identification. Write
    $m_f=M(\mathbf 1)_f$, so that $M(\theta)_f=m_f\theta_f$.
    Fix a reference crossing bond $f_0$ and an actual walk $p_f$ entirely
    in the induced graph on $S$ for each crossing bond $f$. Assume
    \begin{align}
        \operatorname{ev}_{\omega_S}W_T(p_f) & =m_fm_{f_0}^{-1}.
        \label{eq:peps_control_relative_walks}
    \end{align}
    Define $W(z)_f=\operatorname{ev}_zW_T(p_f)$. With the complementary
    boundary input reread from $F_R^{-1}\theta$, the actual coefficients
    satisfy
    \begin{align}
        (U_WM_S^u)\bigl(\alpha_S(y,a,r,z),F_R^{-1}\theta\bigr)
         & =|G|^{-|S|}\sum_{x\in G}
        \mathbf 1_{\{y_f=xm_{f_0}\theta_f\ (f\in\partial S)\}}
        \mathbf 1_{\{z=x\omega_S x^{-1}\}}.
        \label{eq:peps_control_common_transport}
    \end{align}
    Here $\theta_f$ denotes the label on the corresponding crossing bond
    of $R$. This is an auxiliary conditional statement in the complement
    argument of \cite[\S6.3]{Schuch2010PEPS}. Constructing the required
    complement walks with (\ref{eq:peps_control_relative_walks}) remains
    the geometric step described in
    \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    At the reindexed input, the actual complementary boundary transport
    is $M(\theta)_f=m_f\theta_f$. On the cycle support, conjugation
    of the constructed words gives
    \begin{align}
        W(x\omega_S x^{-1})_f   & =xm_fm_{f_0}^{-1}x^{-1}, \\
        W(z)_f^{-1}xm_f\theta_f & =xm_{f_0}\theta_f.
        \label{eq:peps_control_relative_cancellation}
    \end{align}
    Substituting~(\ref{eq:peps_control_relative_cancellation}) into
    (\ref{eq:peps_cycle_controlled_coefficients}) retains the cycle indicator.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Relative boundary factor of actual controlled complementary blocks]%
    \label{thm:peps_controlled_complement_boundary_factor}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularControlledComplementMatrix_factorization,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularControlledComplementMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularControlledComplementMatrix_sum_factorization}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_boundary_invariants}
    Assume the actual complement walks satisfy the relative-word
    identities of (\ref{eq:peps_control_relative_walks}). Number the
    $b=n+1$ crossing bonds and use reference boundary coordinates
    $y=(t,d)$ and $\theta=(a,r)$, where $d,r\in G^n$. For the actual
    controlled complementary matrix at the reindexed input
    $F_R^{-1}\theta$, put $x=t a^{-1}m_{f_0}^{-1}$. Its coefficient is
    \begin{align}
        |G|^{-|S|}\mathbf 1_{\{d=r\}}
        \mathbf 1_{\{z=x\omega_Sx^{-1}\}}.
    \end{align}
    The normalized boundary factor is
    $|G|^{-n/2}\mathbf 1_{\{d=r\}}$.
    Its remaining factor is
    $|G|^{n/2-|S|}\mathbf 1_{\{z=x\omega_Sx^{-1}\}}$.
    The trees, numbering, and control walks may be fixed before an
    arbitrary finite family of bond assignments. The same factor then
    separates from every coherent linear combination of their actual
    complementary matrices; the residual coefficients are combined
    with the same weights.

    This statement retains the virtual reference label $a$ in the
    residual coefficient. It is an auxiliary coefficient statement,
    rather than a conclusion about the full physical bipartite state;
    for commuting torus closures, the complementary loops of
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_torus_simply_connected_boundary_loops} realize
    its geometric walk condition.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_cycle_controlled_relative_walks}
    Equality under a common left translation is equivalent to equality
    of all relative labels and equality of the reference labels.
    The latter fixes $x=t a^{-1}m_{f_0}^{-1}$ uniquely. The finite
    translation sum therefore has one surviving term. Multiplication
    by $|G|^{n/2}$ isolates the normalized maximally entangled
    coefficient, and linearity gives the coherent sum identity.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Common boundary factor of actual controlled canonical cuts]%
    \label{thm:peps_controlled_projector_cut_boundary_factor}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularControlledProjectorCutMatrix_eq_native_contraction,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularControlledProjectorCutMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularControlledProjectorCutMatrix_coordinates,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularReferenceRegionAncilla,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularReferenceRegionAncilla_dotProduct,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularControlledProjectorCutMatrix_sum_factorization}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_controlled_complement_boundary_factor, def:peps_regular_region_coordinates}
    Retain the same actual complement walk identities, and suppose
    every actual cycle residual in $R$ is the identity. Contract the
    two canonical region matrices, applying the fixed controlled
    physical permutation only in $S$. Reindexing their common boundary
    sum by $F_R$ preserves the original contraction.

    Let $d_R,d_S\in G^n$ be the relative boundary labels. In the
    spanning-tree physical coordinates the actual contracted
    coefficient is
    \begin{align}
        |G|^{-|R|-|S|}\mathbf 1_{\{d_R=d_S\}}
        \mathbf 1_{\{z_R=\mathbf 1\}}
        \sum_{x\in G}\mathbf 1_{\{z_S=x\omega_Sx^{-1}\}}.
        \label{eq:peps_controlled_canonical_cut_factor}
    \end{align}
    In particular neither reference boundary label nor the common
    multiplier $m_{f_0}$ occurs in the result.

    Let $N_R$ be the number of choices of the reference boundary label,
    internal reference labels, and normalized vertex labels in $R$.
    The fixed region ancillary vector is
    $\phi_R=N_R^{-1/2}\mathbf 1_{\{z_R=\mathbf 1\}}$; it has norm
    one. For any finite sector family obeying the stated actual
    residual and walk conditions, and any coefficients $\lambda_i$,
    the coherent cut sum has the exact factorization
    \begin{align}
        \Omega_{|G|^n}(d_R,d_S)\,\phi_R\,
        \biggl(|G|^{n/2}\sqrt{N_R}\,|G|^{-|R|-|S|}
        \sum_i\lambda_i\sum_{x\in G}
        \mathbf 1_{\{z_S=x\omega_{S,i}x^{-1}\}}\biggr).
    \end{align}
    Both normalized factors are fixed before the sector family and
    its coefficients; all sector dependence is on the complementary
    cycle coordinates. These are auxiliary canonical-cut statements
    associated with \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}. The actual
    geometric conditions and transport to the original physical
    tensors remain separate steps, as recorded in
    \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_twisted_projector_coordinates}
    First change the common boundary summation variable by its
    reversible region transport. Substitute the two actual
    spanning-tree coefficient formulas. For each complementary
    translation, the complementary boundary indicator fixes the
    shared boundary configuration. Its relative labels equal $d_S$,
    and the interior translation sum gives
    $\mathbf 1_{\{d_R=d_S\}}$. This proves
    (\ref{eq:peps_controlled_canonical_cut_factor}). There are exactly
    $N_R$ unrestricted region ancillary configurations, while its
    cycle tuple is fixed, proving normalization. Extract the two
    square-root normalization factors and use linearity for the
    finite sector sum.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[A common canonical torus cut factor from simple connectedness and winding]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_controlled_boundary_factor}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.exists_unitary_torusControlledBoundaryFactor_of_isSimplyConnected_and_winding}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_controlled_projector_cut_boundary_factor,
        thm:peps_torus_region_realization,
        thm:peps_torus_region_integer_lift_gauge,
        thm:peps_torus_complement_cycle_words}
    Let both torus dimensions be at least three. Suppose the actual
    closed-cell realization of $R$ is simply connected. Choose rooted
    spanning trees in $R$ and $S$, number their nonempty crossing bonds
    by $\{0,\ldots,n\}$, and choose fixed actual complementary root
    loops $p_f$. Assume their ambient torus windings equal those of
    the native two-tree routes from the reference crossing to $f$.

    There exists one unitary $U$ on the original complementary
    half-edge physical basis, chosen before all closure sectors and
    coefficients, with the following property. For every finite family
    of commuting pairs $(g_i,h_i)$ and coefficients $\lambda_i$, form
    the actual canonical cut contractions
    \begin{align}
        K_i(\alpha,\beta)
         & =\sum_{\theta}
        M_R^{g_i,h_i}(\alpha,\theta)
        (UM_S^{g_i,h_i})(\beta,\theta),
        \label{eq:peps_torus_canonical_cut_contraction}
    \end{align}
    where the same native crossing edges are reread on the two sides.
    In the fixed spanning-tree and relative boundary coordinates,
    their coherent sum is
    \begin{align}
        \sum_i\lambda_i K_i
         & =\Omega_{|G|^n}\otimes\phi_R\otimes\chi_{\lambda},
    \end{align}
    with the same normalized maximally entangled factor and the same
    normalized region ancillary vector as in
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_controlled_projector_cut_boundary_factor}.
    The remaining complementary coefficient is
    \begin{align}
        \chi_{\lambda}(z_S)
         & =|G|^{n/2}\sqrt{N_R}\,|G|^{-|R|-|S|}
        \sum_i\lambda_i\sum_{x\in G}
        \mathbf 1_{\{z_S=x\omega_{S,i}x^{-1}\}}.
    \end{align}
    Thus no group-valued flatness, relative-word identity, mixed Gram
    formula, or tensor factorization is supplied as a hypothesis.
    This is an auxiliary canonical-cut consequence in the argument of
    \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}. Existence of the required
    complementary loops for the paper's disk geometry and transport
    to the original physical site tensors remain separate steps;
    see \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    Simple connectedness of the actual realization supplies an integer
    coordinate lift. For every commuting pair its normalized gauge
    gives identity region cycle residuals. Equality of the supplied
    loop windings with the native routes gives the actual complement
    relative-word identities simultaneously for all commuting pairs.
    Evaluate the fixed walk words to construct $U$, then apply the
    coherent canonical-cut factorization. Reindexing the shared
    boundary sum gives the original contraction
    (\ref{eq:peps_torus_canonical_cut_contraction}).
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Fixed boundary embedding and density]%
    \label{def:peps_fixed_boundary_factor_density}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.boundaryAncillaMap,
        TNLean.PEPS.fixedBoundaryFactorDensity,
        TNLean.PEPS.boundaryFactorSchmidtMatrix}
    \leanok
    Let $D$ be a nonempty finite set and $\phi\in\mathbb C^A$.
    Define $J_\phi:\mathbb C^D\to\mathbb C^{D\times A}$ by
    $(J_\phi)_{(d,a),s}=\mathbf 1_{\{d=s\}}\phi(a)$, and set
    \begin{align}
        \rho_\phi=|D|^{-1}J_\phi J_\phi^\dagger,
        \qquad K_{\phi,\chi}=|D|^{-1/2}J_\phi J_\chi^{\mathsf T}.
    \end{align}
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Reduced matrix of a fixed boundary factor]%
    \label{thm:peps_fixed_boundary_factor_density}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.boundaryAncillaMap_conjTranspose_mul,
        TNLean.PEPS.fixedBoundaryFactorDensity_properties,
        TNLean.PEPS.vonNeumannEntropy_fixedBoundaryFactorDensity,
        TNLean.PEPS.boundaryFactorSchmidtMatrix_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.boundaryFactorSchmidtMatrix_mul_conjTranspose,
        TNLean.PEPS.mul_unitary_transpose_mul_conjTranspose,
        TNLean.PEPS.boundaryFactorSchmidtMatrix_submatrix_mul_conjTranspose,
        TNLean.PEPS.fixedBoundaryFactorDensity_submatrix_properties,
        TNLean.PEPS.normalized_reducedMatrix_eq_of_scalar}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_fixed_boundary_factor_density}
    If $\|\phi\|=1$, then $J_\phi$ is an isometry and $\rho_\phi$ is
    a positive density of trace one and rank $|D|$, satisfying
    $\rho_\phi^2=|D|^{-1}\rho_\phi$. Moreover,
    \begin{align}
        K_{\phi,\chi}K_{\phi,\chi}^\dagger
        =\|\chi\|^2\rho_\phi.
    \end{align}
    These identities persist under bijective changes of physical coordinates.
    A unitary acting on the complementary indices leaves the reduced matrix
    unchanged. If a nonzero coefficient matrix has reduced matrix
    $a\rho_\phi$, its trace is positive and trace normalization gives
    exactly $\rho_\phi$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_flat_density_entropy}
    The Gram matrix is $J_\phi^\dagger J_\phi=\|\phi\|^2I$.
    Matrix multiplication gives the reduced identity. Cyclicity of trace,
    the isometry rank identity, and one further multiplication give the
    density properties. Complementary unitarity cancels between a matrix
    and its adjoint; positivity and nonvanishing give positive trace.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Fixed regular reference-boundary density]%
    \label{def:peps_regular_reference_boundary_density}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.RegularReferenceRegionLabels,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularReferenceRegionLabelsFintype,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionReferenceCoordinatesEquiv,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularReferenceBoundaryDensity}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_regular_region_coordinates,
        thm:peps_controlled_projector_cut_boundary_factor,
        def:peps_fixed_boundary_factor_density}
    Number the $n+1$ actual crossing bonds. The spanning-tree physical
    coordinates identify the region rows with $(d,a)$, where $d\in G^n$
    is the relative boundary label and $a$ consists of its reference label,
    internal references, normalized vertex labels, and cycle residuals.
    Pull back $\rho_{\phi_R}$ to the native half-edge physical rows, with
    the fixed normalized ancillary vector $\phi_R$ of
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_controlled_projector_cut_boundary_factor}.
    Denote this density by $\rho_R^0$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Spectrum of the fixed regular reference density]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_reference_boundary_density}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.star_dotProduct_regularReferenceRegionAncilla,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularReferenceBoundaryDensity_properties,
        TNLean.PEPS.vonNeumannEntropy_regularReferenceBoundaryDensity,
        TNLean.PEPS.renyiEntropy_regularReferenceBoundaryDensity}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_regular_reference_boundary_density}
    The density $\rho_R^0$ is positive, has trace one and rank $|G|^n$, and
    satisfies $(\rho_R^0)^2=|G|^{-n}\rho_R^0$. Its von Neumann entropy and
    every nonnegative finite-order R\'enyi entropy are $n\log|G|$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_fixed_boundary_factor_density, thm:peps_flat_density_renyi_entropy}
    The fixed ancillary vector has norm one. Apply the fixed-factor density
    identities and transport them through the actual coordinate bijection.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Common reduced density of actual canonical closure sums]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_controlled_boundary_density}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.torusControlledCut_reducedMatrix_eq_trace_smul,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusControlledCut_normalized_reducedMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusControlledCut_normalized_renyiEntropy}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_torus_controlled_boundary_factor}
    Retain the actual simple connectedness, fixed spanning trees, crossing
    numbering, and fixed complementary winding loops of
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_torus_controlled_boundary_factor}. For a finite
    commuting closure family, let $C_\lambda$ be its coherent actual
    canonical cut matrix, before the complementary controlled unitary.
    Then the fixed density $\rho_R^0$, chosen before the sectors and
    coefficients, satisfies
    \begin{align}
        C_\lambda C_\lambda^\dagger
        =\operatorname{tr}(C_\lambda C_\lambda^\dagger)\rho_R^0.
    \end{align}
    If $C_\lambda\ne0$, its trace is positive and its normalized reduced
    density equals $\rho_R^0$. Consequently its rank is $|G|^n$, its
    nonzero spectrum is flat, and its von Neumann and all nonnegative
    finite-order R\'enyi entropies are $n\log|G|$.

    This is a canonical-cut consequence with supplied fixed complementary
    winding loops. Their existence for the paper's arbitrary disk geometry
    and transfer to the original site tensors remain separate steps;
    see \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}. It is not an
    unrestricted formalization of \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_reference_boundary_density}
    The actual controlled cut is $K_{\phi_R,\chi_\lambda}$ in fixed physical
    coordinates. Its reduced matrix is $\|\chi_\lambda\|^2\rho_R^0$.
    Complementary unitarity gives the same reduced matrix before control.
    Since $\operatorname{tr}\rho_R^0=1$, the scalar is precisely its trace.
    Nonvanishing then permits normalization; the fixed spectrum gives all
    stated entropies.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Actual boundary routes through two rooted trees]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_boundary_route}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularWalkHolonomy_inducedWalk,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionTreeWalk,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionTreeWalk_holonomy,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryRoute,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryRoute_holonomy,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryTreeRoute,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryTreeRoute_holonomy,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularCombinedBoundaryTransport,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularCombinedBoundaryTransport_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularCombinedBoundaryTransport_tail,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularCombinedBoundaryTransport_head}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_walk_holonomy,
        thm:peps_regular_region_cycle_holonomy,
        thm:peps_regular_region_tree_gauge,
        def:peps_regionComplementBoundaryConfig}
    Choose rooted spanning trees in a region $R$ and its complement $S$,
    with normalized gauges $k_R,k_S$. Let $F_R,F_S$ be their actual
    crossing-label transports. After rereading each crossing bond as the
    same native edge on either side, define $M=F_S\circ F_R^{-1}$.
    If $f$ joins $v_f\in R$ to $w_f\in S$, write $D_f$ for its actual
    directed bond transport from $v_f$ to $w_f$. Then
    \begin{align}
        M(\theta)_f & =m_f\theta_f,
                    & m_f           & =k_S(w_f)^{-1}D_fk_R(v_f).
        \label{eq:peps_boundary_route_multiplier}
    \end{align}
    Thus $D_f=u_f$ when the region endpoint is the smaller endpoint,
    and $D_f=u_f^{-1}$ when it is the larger endpoint.

    Fix a reference crossing $f_0$. Starting at the complementary root,
    construct the actual closed walk $p_f$ by following its tree to
    $w_{f_0}$, crossing backwards to $v_{f_0}$, following the region tree
    to $v_f$, crossing to $w_f$, and following the complementary tree
    back to its root. Its holonomy is
    \begin{align}
        H(u,p_f) & =m_fm_{f_0}^{-1}.
    \end{align}
    The construction and identity permit arbitrary bond operators.
    Inclusion of an induced-region walk in the ambient graph preserves
    its holonomy. This supplies actual routes for the cycle argument in
    \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}; the route passes through $R$.
    For commuting torus closures, the complementary loops of
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_torus_simply_connected_boundary_loops} have
    the same winding, and hence the required transport.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    The two boundary equivalences multiply at the region and
    complementary endpoints, giving the crossing multiplier
    (\ref{eq:peps_boundary_route_multiplier}).
    Along each chosen tree path from $v$ to $w$, the original bond
    transport is $k(w)k(v)^{-1}$. Multiplying the five successive
    portions of $p_f$ in reverse traversal order and cancelling the
    root gauges gives $m_fm_{f_0}^{-1}$. Induced-walk inclusion retains
    each ordered native edge and hence each directed factor.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Complement cycle words from actual torus winding data]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_complement_cycle_words}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryCrossingAdj,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryInsideVertex,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryOutsideVertex,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularCombinedBoundaryTransport_insideOutside,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusBoundaryTreeRoute,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusBoundaryTreeRoute_holonomy,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionCycleWord_eval_torusClosure_of_winding_eq}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_boundary_route,
        thm:peps_regular_region_cycle_holonomy,
        thm:peps_torus_walk_winding}
    On a native torus of width and height at least three, choose rooted
    spanning trees in $R$ and $S=R^c$. For each native crossing bond $f$,
    membership determines its inside endpoint $v_f$, its outside endpoint
    $w_f$, and its directed step from $v_f$ to $w_f$. Choose a reference
    bond $f_0$, and form the actual two-tree route $q_f$ of
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_regular_boundary_route} from these endpoints.
    For arbitrary native bond operators its holonomy is
    \begin{align}
        H(u,q_f) & =m_fm_{f_0}^{-1},
                 & m_f               & =(F_S\circ F_R^{-1})(\mathbf 1)_f.
    \end{align}

    Suppose actual loops $p_f$ in the induced complementary graph,
    based at its normalized root, have been chosen independently of the
    closure labels. Assume their ambient seam crossing numbers satisfy
    \begin{align}
        \operatorname{wind}(p_f) & =\operatorname{wind}(q_f).
    \end{align}
    For every commuting pair $g,h$, let $u^{g,h}$ be the actual native
    closure assignment, $\omega_S^{g,h}$ its complementary tree residuals,
    and $m_f^{g,h}$ its combined crossing multipliers. Then the genuine
    complementary walk words satisfy
    \begin{align}
        \operatorname{ev}_{\omega_S^{g,h}}W_{T_S}(p_f)
         & =m_f^{g,h}(m_{f_0}^{g,h})^{-1}.
    \end{align}
    Thus the same words give the relative identity simultaneously for
    all commuting closure pairs. This is a geometry-to-group identity
    for the accessible complement argument in
    \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}, local source lines 1935--1990.
    It neither assumes a group-valued relative transport equation nor
    asserts existence of the complementary loops with these winding numbers;
    the remaining scope is recorded in
    \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Region membership selects the two endpoints of every crossing bond.
    The directed factor is its original operator or its inverse according
    to that selection, so the actual two-tree route has the stated
    combined multiplier ratio. Evaluation of a complementary word at
    its actual tree residual equals the original root-loop holonomy.
    Inclusion in the ambient graph preserves that holonomy. Equal winding
    numbers give equal commuting-closure transports, identifying the
    complementary loop transport with the actual reference-route ratio.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[One complementary unitary from fixed winding data]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_complement_disentangling}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.exists_unitary_torusComplementDisentangling_of_winding_eq}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_torus_complement_cycle_words, thm:peps_cycle_controlled_boundary}
    Choose the two rooted trees, reference crossing bond, and fixed
    complementary root loops with matching actual winding from
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_torus_complement_cycle_words}. There exists one
    unitary $U$ on the original complementary half-edge physical space,
    chosen independently of the commuting closure labels $g,h$.
    Write $S=R^c$, let $B_S^{g,h}$ be the actual canonical complementary
    block, and let $F_R^{g,h},F_S^{g,h}$ be the actual boundary transports.
    Reread each crossing label on $S$ under the same native edge, and put
    $m_f^{g,h}=(F_S^{g,h}\circ(F_R^{g,h})^{-1})(\mathbf 1)_f$.
    For every commuting pair and every complementary physical coordinate
    $(y,a,r,z)$, the actual coefficient formula is
    \begin{align}
         & (U B_S^{g,h})\bigl(\alpha(y,a,r,z),
        (F_R^{g,h})^{-1}\theta\bigr)           \\
         & \qquad=|G|^{-|S|}\sum_{x\in G}
        \mathbf 1_{\{y_f=xm_{f_0}^{g,h}\theta_f\ \text{for every }f\}}
        \mathbf 1_{\{z=x\omega_S^{g,h}x^{-1}\}}.
        \label{eq:peps_complement_disentangled_coefficient}
    \end{align}
    Thus the relative crossing multipliers are removed by one common
    complementary operation, while the reference multiplier and actual
    cycle constraint remain explicit. This is an auxiliary form of the
    disentangling calculation in
    \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}, local source lines 1935--1990.
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_torus_simply_connected_boundary_loops}
    supplies these winding loops from actual simple connectedness.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_cycle_controlled_relative_walks}
    At cycle coordinates $z$, translate the boundary coordinate $y_f$
    by the inverse of the evaluation of the fixed complementary word
    associated with $f$. Leave the other coordinates unchanged. This
    is a permutation of the original half-edge physical basis, hence
    gives a unitary independent of $g,h$. Matching winding derives the
    relative word identity for each commuting closure pair. Applying
    the exact controlled coefficient formula then cancels the relative
    multipliers, retaining the reference and cycle factors of
    (\ref{eq:peps_complement_disentangled_coefficient}).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Local invariant support of actual walk-controlled operations]%
    \label{thm:peps_cycle_controlled_local_support}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionCoordinatesEquiv_symm_coordinateTranslation,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionCoordinateTranslation,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularCycleControlledBoundary_coordinateTranslation,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionVertexTranslationMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularCycleControlledBoundaryMatrix_commute_vertexTranslation,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionVertexTranslationMatrix_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionPhysicalProductMatrix_regularLegProjector_eq_sum_vertexTranslation,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularCycleControlledBoundaryMatrix_commute_localProjector,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularCycleControlledBoundaryMatrix_maps_localProjector_range,
        TNLean.PEPS.exists_unitary_regionPhysicalProductMatrix_mul_cycleWords}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_regular_region_coordinates, thm:peps_regular_region_cycle_holonomy, thm:peps_cycle_controlled_boundary, thm:peps_region_physical_maps}
    Fix a rooted spanning tree in a finite region. Independent vertex
    translations $\lambda_v$ of its actual physical half-edge labels
    transform the spanning-tree coordinates by
    \begin{align}
        (y,a,r,z) & \longmapsto
        \bigl(\lambda_oy,\lambda_oa,
        (\lambda_vr_v\lambda_o^{-1})_v,
        \lambda_oz\lambda_o^{-1}\bigr).
        \label{eq:peps_local_translation_coordinates}
    \end{align}
    Reconstructing these coordinates gives precisely the original
    half-edge labels multiplied on the left by $\lambda_v$ at vertex $v$.
    For a fixed family of actual region walks, let $U$ be the native
    boundary permutation controlled by their constructed cycle words.
    This matrix commutes with every independent vertex translation.
    Their finite average is the actual product local invariant projector
    \begin{align}
        P & =\bigotimes_{v\in R}P_v
        =|G|^{-|R|}\sum_{\lambda\in G^R}L_\lambda,
          & UP                      & =PU.
        \label{eq:peps_controlled_local_projector}
    \end{align}
    In particular $U$ maps the genuine local invariant support
    $\operatorname{range}P$ into itself.

    If the original local physical maps $A_v$ are regular $G$-isometric,
    there exists a unitary $W$ on their physical region such that
    \begin{align}
        W\Bigl(\bigotimes_{v\in R}A_v\Bigr)
         & =\Bigl(\bigotimes_{v\in R}A_v\Bigr)U.
    \end{align}
    The fixed words and their physical implementation are chosen without
    closure labels. No equivariance premise on an arbitrary control
    function or surjectivity of the canonical projector is required.
    These are auxiliary physical support identities for the accessible
    complement calculation in \cite[\S6.2--6.3]{Schuch2010PEPS},
    local source lines 1765--1920 and 1935--1990.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_physical_unitary_transport}
    Apply the native reconstruction formulas at the two internal endpoints
    and the region endpoint of each crossing edge. The vertex coordinate
    in (\ref{eq:peps_local_translation_coordinates}) cancels its root inverse against the transformed reference
    or boundary label, giving the independent physical translation.
    Simultaneous conjugation conjugates evaluation of every actual walk
    word, so the controlled boundary translation commutes with this action.
    Taking the product of the local finite averages identifies their
    independent choices with $\lambda\in G^R$, yielding the product
    projector and commutation (\ref{eq:peps_controlled_local_projector}). This also proves range preservation.
    The supported physical unitary transport theorem then gives $W$ on the
    original region, including any unused physical directions.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Physical transfer of the actual canonical cut]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_physical_cut_transfer}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regionPhysicalProductMatrix_mul_regularProjectorTwistedRegionMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularProjectorTwistedCutMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionPhysicalProductMatrix_mul_regularProjectorTwistedCutMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularProjectorTwistedCutMatrix_mul_regionPhysicalProductMatrix_transpose,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularPhysicalCutMatrix_eq_physicalImage_regularProjectorTwistedCutMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.exists_positive_regularPhysicalCut_density_transfer}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_region_physical_maps}
    For arbitrary bond operators $u$, contract the two actual canonical
    projector regions over their common crossing labels:
    \begin{align}
        C_u(\alpha,\beta)
         & =\sum_\theta B_R^u(\alpha,\theta)
        B_{R^c}^u(\beta,\theta).
    \end{align}
    Here the complement reads the same crossing edge under its native
    boundary identification. Write $P_R=\bigotimes_{v\in R}P_v$ and
    $T_R=\bigotimes_{v\in R}A_v$ for the original local projector and
    physical maps. The actual physical coefficient matrix $M_u$ satisfies
    \begin{align}
        P_R C_u & =C_u,                        & C_uP_{R^c}^{\mathsf T} & =C_u, &
        M_u     & =T_R C_uT_{R^c}^{\mathsf T}.
    \end{align}
    If the local maps are regular $G$-isometric, choose their derived
    positive product Gram factors $c_R,c_{R^c}$. For every finite coherent
    sum $C=\sum_i\mu_iC_{u_i}$ and $M=\sum_i\mu_iM_{u_i}$, put
    $\tau_C=CC^\dagger$ and $\tau_M=MM^\dagger$. Then
    \begin{align}
        \tau_M & =c_{R^c}T_R\tau_CT_R^\dagger,       &
        \operatorname{tr}\tau_M
               & =c_Rc_{R^c}\operatorname{tr}\tau_C,   \\
        (\operatorname{tr}\tau_M)^{-1}\tau_M
               & =c_R^{-1}T_R
        \bigl((\operatorname{tr}\tau_C)^{-1}\tau_C\bigr)T_R^\dagger.
    \end{align}
    The inverse is read as zero when its argument is zero. For a nonzero
    state, the last identity is the transfer of normalized reduced density.
    This is an auxiliary physical accessibility calculation for
    \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}, local source lines 1765--1820 and
    1935--1990; no exterior routing or canonical factorization is assumed.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_physical_unitary_transport, thm:peps_regular_twisted_region_accessibility}
    Expand the actual half-edge contraction. Summing the physical row
    against the product local projector replaces each site factor
    $P_v$ by $P_v^2=P_v$. The two reverse accessibility identities give
    $M_u=T_RC_uT_{R^c}^{\mathsf T}$ directly. These equalities persist
    under finite coherent sums. Inserting
    $T_{R^c}^\dagger T_{R^c}=c_{R^c}P_{R^c}$ gives the reduced matrix;
    cyclicity of trace and $P_RC=C$ give its trace. Cancelling the
    positive product factors gives the normalized identity.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Spectrum under a supported physical map]%
    \label{thm:peps_supported_physical_density}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.normalizedPhysicalImageDensity_properties,
        TNLean.PEPS.normalizedPhysicalImageDensity,
        TNLean.PEPS.vonNeumannEntropy_normalizedPhysicalImageDensity}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_physical_cut_transfer}
    Let $\rho\geq0$ have trace one, and suppose a rectangular physical map
    $T$ and a positive scalar $c$ satisfy $T^\dagger T\rho=c\rho$.
    Define $\widehat\rho=c^{-1}T\rho T^\dagger$. Then
    $\widehat\rho\geq0$, $\operatorname{tr}\widehat\rho=1$, and
    $\operatorname{rank}\widehat\rho=\operatorname{rank}\rho$.
    If $\rho^2=r^{-1}\rho$, then
    \begin{align}
        \widehat\rho^2  & =r^{-1}\widehat\rho, &
        S(\widehat\rho) & =\log r.
    \end{align}
    This auxiliary finite-dimensional calculation expresses physical
    accessibility on the invariant support in
    \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}, local source lines 1765--1820 and
    2043--2072. It makes no assertion that $T$ is surjective.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_flat_density_entropy}
    Hermitian conjugation gives $\rho T^\dagger T=c\rho$.
    Positivity follows by conjugation and positive scaling; cyclicity of
    trace gives trace one. Moreover
    \begin{align}
        T^\dagger\widehat\rho T & =c\rho.
    \end{align}
    Applying the rank inequality to each physical image yields equality
    of ranks. Inserting $T^\dagger T$ between the two densities proves
    the flat-spectrum identity, which gives the stated entropy.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Common reduced density for original torus closures]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_physical_cut_density}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.exists_torusPhysicalCut_density_transfer,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.exists_torusPhysicalCut_common_density_of_isSimplyConnected_and_winding}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_physical_cut_transfer, thm:peps_supported_physical_density}
    Work on a native torus with both dimensions at least three, with an
    original regular $G$-isometric site map. Assume that the actual
    closed-cell realization of $R$ is simply connected. Choose rooted
    spanning trees on $R$ and its complement, a nonempty enumeration
    of the $b$ crossing bonds, and fixed complement-root loops whose
    winding equals the corresponding two-tree boundary routes.
    There exists one density $\rho_R$ on the original physical region,
    chosen before the closure pairs and their coherent coefficients.
    For every finite family of commuting pairs $(g_i,h_i)$ and every
    nonzero coherent closure superposition with actual cut matrix $M$,
    \begin{align}
        \frac{MM^\dagger}{\operatorname{tr}(MM^\dagger)} & =\rho_R,             &
        \rho_R                                           & \geq0,               & \operatorname{tr}\rho_R & =1, \\
        \operatorname{rank}\rho_R                        & =|G|^{b-1},          &
        \rho_R^2                                         & =|G|^{-(b-1)}\rho_R, &
        S(\rho_R)                                        & =(b-1)\log|G|.
    \end{align}
    For every nonnegative finite Rényi order $\alpha$, including order
    zero and the von Neumann value at order one,
    $S_\alpha(\rho_R)=(b-1)\log|G|$.
    More generally, the density of every actual coherent twisted cut
    is the supported physical image of its canonical density, with
    one positive normalization independent of the closure labels.
    The first conclusion requires no surjectivity of the original
    physical map, supplied block Gram, or tensor factorization.
    This is an auxiliary form of \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS},
    local source lines 1935--1990 and 2027--2072. The fixed exterior
    winding loops are explicit hypotheses here;
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_torus_simply_connected_physical_density}
    derives them from the contiguous simply connected region.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_torus_controlled_boundary_density, thm:peps_flat_density_renyi_entropy}
    Derive the incident-coordinate local isometries from the original
    four-leg isometry. Their physical product map has positive scalar
    Gram $T_R^\dagger T_R=c_RP_R$. The actual cut image shows that a
    nonzero original coherent cut has a nonzero canonical coherent cut.
    Its fixed normalized density $\rho_R^0$ is supported on $P_R$, so
    the physical density is the fixed matrix
    \begin{align}
        \rho_R & =c_R^{-1}T_R\rho_R^0T_R^\dagger.
    \end{align}
    The supported physical density theorem transfers positivity, trace,
    rank, and the flat spectrum. The spectral entropy calculation gives
    the stated boundary term at all nonnegative finite Rényi orders.
    Every physical map and normalization was
    chosen from the original site tensor before the coherent coefficients.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Canonical coordinates of a supplied lifted torus region]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_region_integer_lift_coordinates}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularProjectorTwistedRegionMatrix_coordinates_of_torusRegionIntegerLift,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularProjectorTwistedRegionMatrix_exists_normalized_ancilla_of_torusRegionIntegerLift}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_torus_region_integer_lift_gauge}
    Suppose a native torus region has the integer lift described in
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_torus_region_integer_lift_gauge}. Choose a spanning
    tree and root, and let $k^{g,h}$ be its normalized lift gauge. For
    commuting closure labels $g,h$, the actual canonical tensor satisfies
    \begin{align}
        B_R^{g,h}(y,a,r,z;\theta)
         & =B_R(y,a,r,z;F_{g,h}(\theta)),
    \end{align}
    where $F_{g,h}$ is the native transported crossing configuration.
    In particular, there are a positive constant $\gamma$ and one
    normalized vector $\phi(a,r,z)$, independent of $g,h$, such that
    \begin{align}
        B_R^{g,h}(y,a,r,z;\theta)
         & =\gamma\Pi_{\partial R}(y,F_{g,h}(\theta))\phi(a,r,z).
    \end{align}
    This is an auxiliary lifted-region form of the blocking and
    boundary-coordinate calculations in \cite[\S6.2--6.3]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
    It retains the sector-dependent boundary transport. The covering-lift
    and complementary-loop theorems supply the geometric choices for
    actual simply connected regions with torus periods at least three.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_twisted_projector_coordinates, thm:peps_regular_region_projector_coordinates}
    Every cycle residual is the identity by
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_torus_region_integer_lift_gauge}. The exact twisted
    coefficient formula therefore reduces to the untwisted coefficient
    formula at the transported crossing configuration. Apply the
    normalized factorization of
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_regular_region_projector_coordinates} to this
    configuration. Its constant and ancillary vector depend only on
    the region, the group, and the chosen tree and root.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Injectivity of an actual connected regular block]%
    \label{thm:peps_connected_regular_region_injectivity}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.isGInjective_regularOpenRegionMatrix_of_connected}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_g_injective}
    Let $R$ be a nonempty connected finite region whose site tensors
    are $G$-injective for the simultaneous regular action on their
    incident legs. Then the actual open tensor of $R$ is $G$-injective
    for the simultaneous regular action on its crossing legs.
    Internal cycles and an empty boundary are allowed.
    This is the regular-representation connected-region consequence of
    \cite[Lemma~5.2 and the following observation]{Schuch2010PEPS};
    the argument does not assume local isometry.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_region_projector_coordinates, thm:peps_regular_open_region_accessibility}
    Apply the product of the local inverses to the actual open tensor.
    The result is its canonical averaging-projector contraction.
    Choose a spanning tree and a root. In the coordinates of
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_regular_region_projector_coordinates}, the row
    with identity reference, normalized vertex, and residual cycle labels
    is $|G|^{1-|R|}\Pi_{\partial R}$. This scalar is nonzero, and
    $\Pi_{\partial R}$ fixes every invariant boundary vector. Thus a
    vanishing physical output implies a vanishing invariant input.
    Boundary invariance follows by translating every internal label
    in the actual contraction.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Cycle exponent of a connected region]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_region_cycle_exponent}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.card_inducedRegion_edgeSet,
        TNLean.PEPS.orderedEdgeEquivEdgeSet,
        TNLean.PEPS.inducedRegionEdgeEquiv,
        TNLean.PEPS.region_card_le_internalEdge_card_add_one,
        TNLean.PEPS.internalEdge_card_add_one_eq_card_of_isTree,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionGroupOrder_pow_div_pow_eq_cycleRank,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionGramScalar_eq_cycleRank,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionGramScalar_eq_prod_of_isTree}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_connected_regular_region_gram}
    In a nonempty connected region, $V_R\leq I_R+1$. Thus
    \begin{align}
        \frac{|G|^{I_R+1}}{|G|^{V_R}}
        =|G|^{I_R+1-V_R}.
    \end{align}
    The exponent is the cycle rank. For a tree it vanishes, so that
    the region Gram factor is just the product of the local factors.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    The internal edges are exactly the edges of the induced graph.
    A connected graph with $V_R$ vertices has at least $V_R-1$ edges,
    with equality for a tree. Since $|G|\ne0$, subtracting these
    nonnegative exponents gives the ratio identity.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Invariant regular boundary vectors]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_boundary_invariants}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryRepresentation_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryRepresentation,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryRelativeEquiv,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryRelativeEquiv_smul,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryInvariantsEquiv,
        TNLean.PEPS.finrank_regularBoundaryInvariants_succ,
        TNLean.PEPS.finrank_regularBoundaryInvariants,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryOrbitBasis,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryOrbitBasis_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryOrbitBasis_eq_sum,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryOrbitBasis_dotProduct}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_blocking_coordinates}
    Let $b\geq1$ and let $G$ act on $G^b$ by simultaneous left
    multiplication. Its invariant functions have dimension $|G|^{b-1}$.
    Writing $a=(a_0,\ldots,a_{b-1})$, the relative coordinates
    $(a_0^{-1}a_i)_{i=1}^{b-1}$ label the orbits. The equal-weight orbit
    vectors form an orthogonal basis, each of squared norm $|G|$.
    This is the invariant-boundary calculation used in the proof of
    \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    The bijection $a\mapsto(a_0,(a_0^{-1}a_i)_{i>0})$ changes the action to
    left multiplication of its first coordinate alone. Invariant functions
    are therefore arbitrary functions of the remaining $b-1$ labels.
    Distinct orbits are disjoint, and each contains exactly $|G|$ elements.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The virtual regular boundary state]%
    \label{def:peps_regular_boundary_state}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryProjector,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundarySchmidtMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryState,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryDensity,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryTranslationState}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_boundary_invariants, thm:peps_group_averaging}
    Set $b=n+1$ and $r=|G|^n$, and let $P$ be the averaging projector
    on $\mathbb C[G^b]$. The unnormalized bipartite boundary vector has
    coefficients
    \begin{align}
        \Psi(a,c)=\sum_{z\in G}\prod_{i=0}^{b-1}\delta_{a_i,zc_i}
        =|G|P_{a,c}.
    \end{align}
    Its normalized coefficient matrix is $C=P/\sqrt r$, and its reduced
    density operator is $\rho=P/r$. These are the virtual vectors used
    in the proof of \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Flat spectrum of the virtual regular boundary]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_boundary_density}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryProjector_mul_self,
        TNLean.PEPS.rank_regularBoundaryProjector,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryProjector_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryProjector_conjTranspose,
        TNLean.PEPS.trace_regularBoundaryProjector,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryProjector_transpose,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundarySchmidtMatrix_mul_projector,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryProjector_mul_schmidtMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundarySchmidtMatrix_mul_conjTranspose,
        TNLean.PEPS.schmidtCoeffMatrix_regularBoundaryState,
        TNLean.PEPS.partialTrace_regularBoundaryState,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryDensity_posSemidef,
        TNLean.PEPS.trace_regularBoundaryDensity,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryState_dotProduct,
        TNLean.PEPS.rank_regularBoundaryDensity,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryDensity_mul_self,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryProjector_mulVec_of_mem_invariants,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryDensity_mulVec_of_mem_invariants,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryTranslationState_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryTranslationState_eq_sum_prod,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryState_eq_smul_translationState}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_regular_boundary_state, thm:peps_regular_boundary_invariants}
    The averaging matrix $P$ is real, symmetric, and idempotent, with
    rank and trace $r$. Its entries are $|G|^{-1}$ on pairs in the same
    orbit and zero otherwise. The vector with coefficient matrix $C$
    has norm one, and tracing out its second factor gives
    $CC^\dagger=\rho=P/r$. Hence $\rho$ is positive, has trace one and
    rank $r$, and satisfies $\rho^2=r^{-1}\rho$. On the invariant
    boundary subspace it acts as multiplication by $r^{-1}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    For each pair in the same orbit, exactly one translation sends one
    configuration to the other. The entry formula follows by averaging.
    Its symmetry gives $P=P^\dagger$, and averaging twice gives $P^2=P$.
    The orbit count gives the rank; its trace follows either from the
    diagonal entries or from its projection property. Now
    $CC^\dagger=P/r$, whose trace is one. The remaining assertions follow
    from $P^2=P$ and from $P$ being the identity on invariant vectors.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Entropy of a flat density operator]%
    \label{thm:peps_flat_density_entropy}
    \lean{Matrix.IsHermitian.eigenvalues_eq_zero_or_inv_of_mul_self_eq_inv_smul,
        vonNeumannEntropy_of_mul_self_eq_inv_smul}
    \leanok
    Let $\rho$ be a finite-dimensional positive semidefinite matrix of
    trace one. If $\rho^2=r^{-1}\rho$ for a positive real number $r$, then
    $S(\rho)=\log r$. This is the spectral step used in the proof of
    \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    For each eigenvalue $\lambda$, the identity gives
    $\lambda(\lambda-r^{-1})=0$. In either case
    $-\lambda\log\lambda=\lambda\log r$, with the value at zero
    interpreted continuously. Summing and using
    $\sum\lambda=\operatorname{tr}\rho=1$ proves $S(\rho)=\log r$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Finite-order R\'enyi entropy]%
    \label{def:peps_renyi_entropy}
    \lean{renyiEntropy}\leanok
    For a positive density $\rho$ and $\alpha>0$, $\alpha\ne1$, define
    \begin{align}
        S_\alpha(\rho)=(1-\alpha)^{-1}
        \log\operatorname{tr}(\rho^\alpha),
    \end{align}
    where the real power is defined spectrally. Set $S_1(\rho)=S(\rho)$
    and $S_0(\rho)=\log\operatorname{rank}\rho$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[All finite R\'enyi orders of a flat density]%
    \label{thm:peps_flat_density_renyi_entropy}
    \lean{trace_cfc_rpow_of_mul_self_eq_inv_smul,
        renyiEntropy_of_mul_self_eq_inv_smul,
        renyiEntropy_of_mul_self_eq_inv_smul_of_rank}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_renyi_entropy}
    Let $\rho\geq0$ have trace one and satisfy $\rho^2=r^{-1}\rho$, with
    $r>0$. For every $\alpha>0$,
    $\operatorname{tr}(\rho^\alpha)=r^{1-\alpha}$ and
    $S_\alpha(\rho)=\log r$. If $\operatorname{rank}\rho=r$, the same
    entropy formula also holds at order zero.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_flat_density_entropy}
    Every eigenvalue is zero or $r^{-1}$. For positive order,
    $\lambda^\alpha=\lambda r^{1-\alpha}$. Summing uses trace one;
    taking logarithms proves the entropy formula away from order one.
    The von Neumann and rank conventions give the remaining orders.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Entropy of the virtual regular boundary]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_boundary_entropy}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.vonNeumannEntropy_regularBoundaryDensity,
        TNLean.PEPS.vonNeumannEntropy_regularBoundaryDensity_eq_mul_log,
        TNLean.PEPS.vonNeumannEntropy_regularBoundaryDensity_eq_boundaryLength}
    \leanok
    For $b=n+1$ regular boundary legs, the normalized virtual reduced
    density operator has von Neumann entropy
    \begin{align}
        S(\rho)=\log(|G|^n)=n\log|G|=b\log|G|-\log|G|.
    \end{align}
    This is the spectral calculation in
    \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}, before identifying the virtual
    boundary with a physical PEPS region.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_boundary_density, thm:peps_flat_density_entropy}
    The identity $\rho^2=r^{-1}\rho$ applied to each eigenvector gives
    eigenvalue $0$ or $r^{-1}$. With $0\log0=0$, each entropy summand
    is therefore its eigenvalue times $\log r$. Their sum is
    $\operatorname{tr}\rho\log r=\log r$. Since $r=|G|^n$ and $|G|>0$,
    the logarithm laws give the stated expressions.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Physical realization of a regular boundary state]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_boundary_physical_realization}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.physicalRegularBoundaryState_eq_kronecker_mulVec,
        TNLean.PEPS.physicalRegularBoundarySchmidtMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.physicalRegularBoundaryState,
        TNLean.PEPS.physicalRegularBoundaryProjector,
        TNLean.PEPS.physicalRegularBoundaryDensity,
        TNLean.PEPS.physicalRegularBoundarySchmidtMatrix_mul_conjTranspose,
        TNLean.PEPS.partialTrace_physicalRegularBoundaryState,
        TNLean.PEPS.physicalRegularBoundaryProjector_conjTranspose,
        TNLean.PEPS.physicalRegularBoundaryProjector_mul_self,
        TNLean.PEPS.physicalRegularBoundaryProjector_isStarProjection,
        TNLean.PEPS.rank_physicalRegularBoundaryProjector,
        TNLean.PEPS.trace_physicalRegularBoundaryProjector,
        TNLean.PEPS.physicalRegularBoundaryDensity_posSemidef,
        TNLean.PEPS.trace_physicalRegularBoundaryDensity,
        TNLean.PEPS.rank_physicalRegularBoundaryDensity,
        TNLean.PEPS.physicalRegularBoundaryDensity_mul_self,
        TNLean.PEPS.spectrum_physicalRegularBoundaryDensity_subset,
        TNLean.PEPS.vonNeumannEntropy_physicalRegularBoundaryDensity}
    \leanok
    Let $A:\mathbb C[G^b]\to\mathcal H_A$ and
    $B:\mathbb C[G^b]\to\mathcal H_B$ satisfy $A^\dagger A=B^\dagger B=P$.
    Applying $A\otimes B$ to the normalized virtual boundary vector gives
    coefficient matrix $ACB^{\mathsf T}$ and reduced density
    $\rho_A=APA^\dagger/r$. The matrix $Q=APA^\dagger$ is an orthogonal
    projector of rank $r$. Thus $\rho_A$ is positive, has trace one and
    rank $r$, and $\rho_A^2=r^{-1}\rho_A$; its spectrum is contained in
    $\{0,r^{-1}\}$, and $S(\rho_A)=n\log|G|$.

    This proves the transport of the boundary spectrum through specified
    maps isometric on the invariant subspace. Applying it to a physical
    PEPS region still requires identification of these maps and this
    boundary vector with the geometric contraction in
    \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_boundary_density, thm:peps_flat_density_entropy}
    The coefficient matrix transforms by $C\mapsto ACB^{\mathsf T}$.
    Since $P$ is real symmetric, $B^\dagger B=P$ gives
    $B^{\mathsf T}\overline B=P$, so the partial trace is
    $ACPC^\dagger A^\dagger=APA^\dagger/r$.
    The equation $A^\dagger A=P$ and $P^2=P$ show $Q^2=Q$ and
    $Q=Q^\dagger$. The map $A$ preserves inner products on
    $\operatorname{ran}P$, and is therefore injective there; its image
    is $\operatorname{ran}Q$, of dimension $r$. The trace and spectrum
    follow from the projection property, and
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_flat_density_entropy} gives the entropy.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Normalization of specified physical cut maps]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_boundary_normalization}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.normalizedRegularBoundaryMap_conjTranspose_mul,
        TNLean.PEPS.normalizedRegularBoundaryMap,
        TNLean.PEPS.physicalRegularBoundarySchmidtMatrix_normalizedRegularBoundaryMap,
        TNLean.PEPS.vonNeumannEntropy_normalizedRegularBoundaryState}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_boundary_physical_realization,
        thm:peps_regular_open_region_projector}
    Let $M^\dagger M=c_AP$ and $N^\dagger N=c_BP$, with $c_A,c_B>0$.
    Then $\widehat M=M/\sqrt{c_A}$ and
    $\widehat N=N/\sqrt{c_B}$ are isometric on the invariant boundary
    subspace. The normalized bipartite vector with coefficient matrix
    \begin{align}
        \widehat M C\widehat N^{\mathsf T}
        =\frac{MPN^{\mathsf T}}{\sqrt{c_A}\sqrt{c_B}\sqrt r}
        \label{eq:peps_regular_boundary_normalized_matrix}
    \end{align}
    has reduced von Neumann entropy $n\log|G|$.
    For the actual cut maps of
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_regular_open_region_projector}, the numerator
    is the physical PEPS coefficient matrix $K$.

    The Gram identities are explicit assumptions in this statement.
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_connected_regular_region_gram} derives them
    from local $G$-isometry for connected untwisted regions, including
    regions containing cycles. The twisted torus contractions of
    \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS} remain separate.
    The scalar convention is recorded in
    \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    Since $c_A>0$, the product of the normalization scalar and its
    conjugate is $c_A^{-1}$. Thus
    $\widehat M^\dagger\widehat M=P$, and similarly for $N$.
    Pulling the scalars out of the coefficient matrix gives
    (\ref{eq:peps_regular_boundary_normalized_matrix}). Apply
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_regular_boundary_physical_realization} to the
    normalized maps.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Entropy of a connected untwisted finite cut]%
    \label{thm:peps_connected_untwisted_cut_entropy}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionGramFactor_pos,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularRegionGramFactor,
        TNLean.PEPS.ofReal_regularRegionGramFactor,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularOpenComplementMatrix_eq_regularOpenRegionMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularPhysicalCutState,
        TNLean.PEPS.exists_positive_gram_regularOpenRegionMatrix_of_connected,
        TNLean.PEPS.exists_normalization_regularPhysicalCutState_of_connected}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_connected_regular_region_gram,
        thm:peps_regular_boundary_normalization}
    Consider a finite graph PEPS with regular bonds and a $G$-isometric
    tensor at every site. Assume that both $R$ and its complement
    induce nonempty connected graphs, and that $b>0$ bonds cross the cut.
    For the untwisted contraction there is a positive normalization of
    the actual physical PEPS vector to a unit vector. Its reduced state
    on $R$ has rank $r=|G|^{b-1}$ and a flat spectrum:
    $\rho_R^2=r^{-1}\rho_R$. Its von Neumann entropy is
    \begin{align}
        S(\rho_R)=(b-1)\log|G|.
    \end{align}
    This finite untwisted statement is a restricted consequence of the
    calculation in \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}; it does not assert
    the theorem for arbitrary torus closures. The remaining scope is
    recorded in \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    Each local isometry supplies a positive Gram factor. Apply
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_connected_regular_region_gram} on both sides
    of the cut, and normalize the two maps by their positive factors.
    The resulting coefficient matrix is a positive scalar multiple of
    the actual PEPS cut matrix. The regular boundary realization gives
    its norm and reduced entropy.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[A local controlled operation removing a boundary twist]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_boundary_untwisting}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularTweezerEquiv_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularTweezerEquiv,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularTweezerEquiv_symm_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularTweezer_relative_label,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularTweezerEquiv_symm_apply_twisted,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularTweezerEquiv_symm_apply_conjugated,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularTweezerMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularTweezerMatrix_mem_unitaryGroup,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularTweezerState,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularTweezerState_factorization,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularTweezerState_mulVec}
    \leanok
    The permutation
    \begin{align}
        F(a,b,c)=(cb^{-1}a,b,c),\qquad
        F^{-1}(a,b,c)=(bc^{-1}a,b,c)
    \end{align}
    acts unitarily in the group basis. If $c=tb$, then
    $F^{-1}(ta,b,tb)=(a,b,tb)$. In particular this removes the
    conjugated twist $t=x^{-1}gx$ from the first label using the
    accessible reference pair $b,c$.

    On the bipartite coefficient vector
    $\mathbf 1_{\{a=td\}}\mathbf 1_{\{c=tb\}}$, applying this inverse
    permutation to the first side gives
    $\mathbf 1_{\{a=d\}}\mathbf 1_{\{c=tb\}}$. All dependence on $t$
    is then confined to the reference pair on that side. This proves
    the local coordinate operation in
    \cite{Schuch2010PEPS}, displayed at local source lines 1961--1965.
    Identifying these coordinates with a whole torus contraction is
    a further step.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    Group cancellation verifies the two inverse permutations and
    $cb^{-1}=t$. A permutation of an orthonormal basis is unitary.
    Substitution in the two indicator functions yields the factorization.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Torus PEPS with group closures]%
    \label{def:peps_torus_g_closure}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.torusHorizontalClosure, TNLean.PEPS.torusVerticalClosure,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusGClosure}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_torus_bond_network}
    On a $w\times\ell$ torus let $H_v=U_h$ when $v+(1,0)$ crosses the
    horizontal seam and $H_v=\mathbb 1$ elsewhere; let $V_v=U_g$ when
    the vertical bond crosses the vertical seam and $V_v=\mathbb 1$
    elsewhere. Define the vector $\ket{\mathcal M(A\mid g,h)}$ by contracting
    the site tensors with these bond operators. Its coefficient at
    $\sigma$ is
    \begin{align}
        \mathcal M(A\mid g,h)_\sigma
        =\sum_{(t_v,r_v,b_v,l_v)_v}\prod_v
        A^{\sigma(v)}_{t_v,r_v,b_v,l_v}
        H_v(l_{v+(1,0)},r_v)V_v(t_v,b_{v+(0,1)}).
        \label{eq:peps_torus_closure_coeff}
    \end{align}
    This is \cite[Definition~5.6]{Schuch2010PEPS}, which uses a square
    lattice; the same contraction defines the vector on a rectangle.
    The four-site diagram suppresses the physical legs at fixed $\sigma$.
    Crossings without a vertex do not denote contractions.
\end{definition}

\begin{center}
    % Source/formula: SCP10 eq:2d:peps-with-ug-uh and
    % eq:peps_torus_closure_coeff on a 2-by-2 torus.
    % Each A has top/right/bottom/left virtual ports 90/0/270/180.
    % H1,H2 carry U_h on the two horizontal returns; G1,G2 carry
    % U_g on the two vertical returns. The physical indices are fixed.
    % Every port is contracted; resulting boundary signature is (V,P)=(0,0).
    % Crossing curves share no index unless they meet at a tensor.
    \tenkzkernel
    \begin{tenkz}[rows={wire,wire,wire,wire}, cols=4, bonds=none]
        \tn[at=(2,2), skin=box, name=NW, ports={0:virtual,90:virtual,180:virtual,270:virtual}]{A}
        \tn[at=(2,3), skin=box, name=NE, ports={0:virtual,90:virtual,180:virtual,270:virtual}]{A}
        \tn[at=(3,2), skin=box, name=SW, ports={0:virtual,90:virtual,180:virtual,270:virtual}]{A}
        \tn[at=(3,3), skin=box, name=SE, ports={0:virtual,90:virtual,180:virtual,270:virtual}]{A}
        \tn[at=(2,1), skin=box, name=H1, ports={0:virtual,180:virtual}]{U_h}
        \tn[at=(3,4), skin=box, name=H2, ports={0:virtual,180:virtual}]{U_h}
        \tn[at=(1,2), skin=box, name=G1, ports={90:virtual,270:virtual}]{U_g}
        \tn[at=(4,3), skin=box, name=G2, ports={90:virtual,270:virtual}]{U_g}
        \tnwire{NW.0}{NE.180}
        \tnwire{SW.0}{SE.180}
        \tnwire{NW.270}{SW.90}
        \tnwire{NE.270}{SE.90}
        \tnwire{H1.0}{NW.180}
        \tnwire[route=arc]{NE.0}{H1.180}
        \tnwire{SE.0}{H2.180}
        \tnwire[route=arc]{H2.0}{SW.180}
        \tnwire{G1.270}{NW.90}
        \tnwire[route=arc]{SW.270}{G1.90}
        \tnwire{SE.270}{G2.90}
        \tnwire[route=arc]{G2.270}{NE.90}
    \end{tenkz}
\end{center}


\begin{definition}[Pair-conjugacy classes of closures]%
    \label{def:peps_pair_conjugacy}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.PairConjugacyClass, TNLean.PEPS.pairConjugacyClass,
        TNLean.PEPS.pairConjugacyClass_eq_iff,
        TNLean.PEPS.commute_iff_of_pairConjugacyClass_eq,
        TNLean.PEPS.PairConjugacyClass.IsCommuting,
        TNLean.PEPS.PairConjugacyClass.isCommuting_pairConjugacyClass,
        TNLean.PEPS.CommutingPairConjugacyClass}
    \leanok
    Two pairs are equivalent if
    $(g,h)=(xg'x^{-1},xh'x^{-1})$ for some $x\in G$. Their equivalence
    classes are the pair-conjugacy classes of
    \cite[Definition~5.8]{Schuch2010PEPS}. Commutation is constant on each
    class, so the classes of commuting pairs are well-defined.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[The torus vector depends only on the pair-conjugacy class]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_pair_conjugacy}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.torusBondNetwork_conjugate, TNLean.PEPS.torusGClosure_conjugate,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusGClosure_eq_of_pairConjugacyClass_eq,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusGClosureClass, TNLean.PEPS.torusGClosureClass_pairConjugacyClass}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_torus_g_closure, def:peps_pair_conjugacy}
    Suppose $A$ is invariant under the virtual group action. Then
    $\ket{\mathcal M(A\mid xgx^{-1},xhx^{-1})}
        =\ket{\mathcal M(A\mid g,h)}$ for every $x\in G$.
    Consequently $\ket{\mathcal M(A\mid C)}$ is well-defined for each
    pair-conjugacy class $C$ \cite[Definition~5.8]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{lem:peps_torus_bond_network_gauge}
    In Lemma~\ref{lem:peps_torus_bond_network_gauge} take $P_v=U_x$ and
    $Q_v=U_{x^{-1}}$ at every site. The absorbed site tensor is unchanged
    by invariance. Each closure matrix $U_g$ or $U_h$ becomes
    $U_{xgx^{-1}}$ or $U_{xhx^{-1}}$, and each identity bond remains the
    identity. This proves the assertion for every coefficient in
    (\ref{eq:peps_torus_closure_coeff}).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Displacement of commuting closure seams]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_commuting_closure_seams}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.torusHorizontalClosureAt_zero,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusHorizontalClosureAt,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusVerticalClosureAt,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusVerticalClosureAt_zero,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusBondNetwork_closureAt_eq_torusGClosure}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_torus_g_closure}
    Let $A$ be invariant under the virtual group action and let $gh=hg$.
    For any column $c$ and row $r$, put
    \begin{align}
        H^c_v=\begin{cases}U_h,&v_x+1=c,\\\mathbb 1,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}
        \qquad
        V^r_v=\begin{cases}U_g,&v_y+1=r,\\\mathbb 1,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}
    \end{align}
    Contracting $A$ with these bond operators gives
    $\ket{\mathcal M(A\mid g,h)}$, independently of $c$ and $r$.
    This is the displacement of commuting loops described after
    the string-deformation display of \cite{Schuch2010PEPS}, local source
    lines 1638--1644; neither isometry
    nor semi-regularity is needed. The identity holds on every nonempty
    rectangular torus, including dimensions equal to one.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{lem:peps_torus_bond_network_gauge}
    Choose coordinate representatives between zero and the corresponding
    circumference minus one. To move the horizontal seam, use the vertex
    change of basis $U_{h^{-1}}$ on columns preceding $c$ and the identity
    elsewhere. The adjacent changes cancel on all ordinary bonds, remove
    the old seam at zero, and insert $U_h$ at the new seam. They commute
    with every $U_g$ on the transverse seam. Similarly, use $U_g$ on rows
    preceding $r$ to move the vertical seam, with the inverse convention
    required by its orientation. Local invariance removes every absorbed
    change of basis. The network gauge identity therefore preserves each
    physical coefficient.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Regular coordinates of a torus site]%
    \label{def:peps_torus_regular_coordinates}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.leftRegularMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.leftRegularMatrix_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.toMatrix_leftRegular_eq_leftRegularMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.leftRegularMatrixEquiv,
        TNLean.PEPS.isSemiRegular_leftRegularMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusLegMatrix_leftRegularMatrix_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusLegRep_leftRegularMatrix_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.finFourArrowEquiv_smul,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusLegRep_leftRegularMatrix_conj,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusLegRep_leftRegularMatrix_unitary,
        TNLean.PEPS.toMatrix_averageMap_torusLegRep_leftRegularMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.toMatrix_siteMap_regular,
        TNLean.PEPS.siteMap_fourCoordinates}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_torus_bond_network, def:peps_semiregular,
        thm:peps_semiregular_equivalence}
    In the group basis, $L_x\ket{k}=\ket{xk}$. The coordinate map
    $\sum_k c_k[k]\mapsto(c_k)_k$ intertwines the group-algebra regular
    representation with these permutation matrices. They are therefore
    semi-regular by Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_semiregular_equivalence}.
    The oriented four-leg
    action sends $(t,r,b,l)$ to $(xt,xr,xb,xl)$; its invariant projector
    has entries
    \begin{align}
        \Pi_{\eta,\theta}
        =\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{x\in G}\mathbf{1}_{\eta=x\theta}.
    \end{align}
    Thus the four regular coordinates agree with the oriented virtual
    action in Definition~6.1 of \cite{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[The local regular Gram identity]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_regular_site_gram}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.torusRegularSite_translation,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.exists_torusRegularSiteGram}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_torus_regular_coordinates, def:peps_g_injective}
    A regular $G$-isometric site tensor $A$ is invariant under common
    translation of its four virtual labels. There is $c>0$ such that
    \begin{align}
        \sum_s\overline{A_s(\eta)}A_s(\theta)
        =\frac{c}{|G|}\sum_{x\in G}\mathbf{1}_{\eta=x\theta}.
    \end{align}
    The factor $c$ records the normalization of the site tensor.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Express $A^\dagger A=c\Pi$ in the orthonormal group basis and use
    the matrix entries of the invariant projector.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Access to the virtual invariant space]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_accessible_virtual_site}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.exists_accessibleVirtualSite}
    \leanok
    If $A^\dagger A=c\Pi$ with $c>0$, then $J=c^{-1/2}A^\dagger$
    satisfies $JA=\sqrt{c}\Pi$ and preserves inner products on
    $\operatorname{ran}A$. It identifies the physical support with
    the invariant virtual space, as in Observation~6.4 of
    \cite{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_torus_regular_site_gram}
    For $u,v$ in the virtual space, $JA u=\sqrt{c}\Pi u$ and
    $\langle Au,Av\rangle=c\langle\Pi u,\Pi v\rangle$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Expansion of a torus overlap]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_overlap_expansion}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.sum_star_weightedSiteProduct_mul,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusBondSiteLabels,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusBondWeight,
        TNLean.PEPS.sum_star_torusSiteProduct_mul,
        TNLean.PEPS.sum_star_torusBondNetwork_mul,
        TNLean.PEPS.permMatrixHom_apply_eq_ite,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusPermutationSiteLabels,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusBondNetwork_perm,
        TNLean.PEPS.sum_star_torusBondNetwork_perm_mul}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_torus_bond_network}
    Let $W(\beta)$ denote the product of the bond matrix entries and
    let $\eta_v(\beta)$ be the four labels read by site $v$.
    The overlap of two networks with site tensors $A_v,B_v$ is
    \begin{align}
        \sum_{\beta,\gamma}\overline{W_A(\beta)}W_B(\gamma)
        \prod_v\left(\sum_s
        \overline{A_{v,s}(\eta_v(\beta))}
        B_{v,s}(\eta_v(\gamma))\right).
    \end{align}
    For permutation bonds, the head label is the permutation of the
    tail label. Hence one free label per bond suffices in each network.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Expand both contractions, interchange the finite sums, and sum each
    physical index independently. A permutation matrix has entry one
    exactly when its row is the image of its column.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Counting compatible regular bonds]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_bond_intertwining_count}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularBond_labels_iff_intertwining,
        TNLean.PEPS.sum_regularBond_labels_eq_card,
        TNLean.PEPS.sum_two_regularBond_labels_eq_card,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusRegularSiteLabels,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusRegularSiteLabels_translation_iff,
        TNLean.PEPS.sum_torusRegularSiteLabels_translation_eq_card,
        TNLean.PEPS.sum_torusClosureSiteLabels_translation_eq_card}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_torus_overlap_expansion}
    On a bond $e$ oriented from $s(e)$ to $t(e)$, let $u_e,w_e$ be
    the group elements in the two networks. Vertex translations $q_v$
    are compatible with this bond precisely when
    \begin{align}
        u_e q_{s(e)}=q_{t(e)}w_e.
    \end{align}
    If the equations hold, each bond has one independent group label;
    otherwise there are no compatible labels. For the two bond families
    on a torus with $N$ sites, the count is $|G|^{2N}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    The tail labels satisfy $\eta_e=q_{s(e)}\theta_e$. Substituting this
    into $u_e\eta_e=q_{t(e)}w_e\theta_e$ and cancelling $\theta_e$
    gives the intertwining equation. Every $\theta_e$ is then free.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Translation compatibility across the torus seams]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_closure_compatibility}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.torusHorizontalClosure_eq_map_element,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusHorizontalClosureElement,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusVerticalClosureElement,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusVerticalClosure_eq_map_element,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsTorusClosureCompatible,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsTorusClosureCompatible.eq_origin,
        TNLean.PEPS.isTorusClosureCompatible_iff_exists_intertwiner,
        TNLean.PEPS.sum_torusClosureCompatible_eq_sum_intertwiner}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_torus_g_closure,
        thm:peps_regular_bond_intertwining_count}
    For closures $(g,h)$ and $(g',h')$, a compatible vertex translation
    is constant, $q_v=x$, and its value satisfies
    \begin{align}
        hx=xh',\qquad gx=xg'.
        \label{eq:peps_closure_seam_intertwining}
    \end{align}
    Summing over compatible vertex translations therefore gives exactly
    the number of simultaneous intertwiners. This holds also when a
    torus circumference is one.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
