    The nonseam horizontal and vertical bonds identify all translations
    in a rectangular fundamental domain. The two seams give the equations
    (\ref{eq:peps_closure_seam_intertwining}). For a circumference of one the corresponding seam is
    already a loop at each site, imposing the same equation.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Overlap of regular isometric torus closures]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_torus_closure_overlap}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.prod_torusSiteGram_eq_sum_translation,
        TNLean.PEPS.sum_star_torusBondNetwork_regularClosure_mul,
        TNLean.PEPS.sum_star_torusGClosure_regular_mul,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.exists_torusGClosure_overlap}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_torus_overlap_expansion, thm:peps_torus_closure_compatibility}
    Let $A_v^\dagger A_v=c_v\Pi_v$ be the local regular isometry identities
    on a torus with $N$ sites, with $c_v>0$. For the actual closure vectors,
    \begin{align}
        \langle\psi_{g,h},\psi_{g',h'}\rangle
        =\left(\prod_v c_v\right)|G|^N
        \#\{x\in G:hx=xh',\ gx=xg'\}.
    \end{align}
    For a uniform tensor the prefactor is $c^N|G|^N$. Neither closure
    pair is required to commute.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_torus_regular_site_gram, thm:peps_finite_indicator_sum}
    Expand the overlap into local Gram kernels. Their product is the
    sum over vertex translations with prefactor
    $(\prod_v c_v)|G|^{-N}$. For a compatible translation, the two bond
    families contribute $|G|^{2N}$, leaving the stated prefactor. By
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_torus_closure_compatibility}, the remaining sum
    counts the simultaneous intertwiners.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Independent regular isometric closure vectors]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_torus_closure_independence}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.torusGClosure_dotProduct_eq_zero_of_pairConjugacyClass_ne,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.exists_torusGClosure_dotProduct_self,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.torusGClosure_dotProduct_self_ne_zero,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.torusGClosure_ne_zero,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.torusGClosureClass_orthogonal,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.linearIndependent_torusGClosureClass,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.linearIndependent_torusGClosureClass_commuting}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_torus_pair_conjugacy}
    For a uniform regular $G$-isometric tensor, the actual vector associated
    to each pair-conjugacy class is nonzero, and distinct class vectors
    are orthogonal. Their family is therefore linearly independent.
    More precisely,
    \begin{align}
        \|\psi_{g,h}\|^2=c^N|G|^N|Z_G(g)\cap Z_G(h)|>0.
    \end{align}
    Restricting to commuting classes proves the independence assertion
    underlying \cite[Theorem~5.9]{Schuch2010PEPS} in the regular
    $G$-isometric case. The all-class assertion is an auxiliary extension:
    no ground-state claim is made for noncommuting closures. Semi-regular
    $G$-injective independence is proved in
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_semiregular_g_injective_closure_independence};
    ground-space identification remains separate
    \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_torus_closure_overlap}
    A simultaneous intertwiner exists exactly when the two closure pairs
    are simultaneously conjugate. Thus the overlap between distinct
    classes vanishes. For a self-overlap the intertwiners form
    $Z_G(g)\cap Z_G(h)$, which contains the identity. Taking the inner
    product of a finite linear relation with each nonzero class vector
    proves independence. Independence is inherited by the commuting
    subfamily.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Independent simultaneous-conjugacy operator sums]%
    \label{thm:peps_pair_conjugacy_operator_independence}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.pairConjugacyOperator_eq_of_pairConjugacyClass_eq,
        TNLean.PEPS.pairConjugacyOperator,
        TNLean.PEPS.pairConjugacyClassOperator,
        TNLean.PEPS.pairConjugacyClassOperator_pairConjugacyClass,
        TNLean.PEPS.pairOperatorDeltaPairing,
        TNLean.PEPS.pairOperatorDeltaPairing_tmul,
        TNLean.PEPS.pairOperatorDeltaPairing_pairConjugacyOperator,
        TNLean.PEPS.pairOperatorDeltaPairing_eq_zero_of_pairConjugacyClass_ne,
        TNLean.PEPS.pairOperatorDeltaPairing_self_eq_card_centralizer,
        TNLean.PEPS.pairOperatorDeltaPairing_self_ne_zero,
        TNLean.PEPS.linearIndependent_pairConjugacyClassOperator,
        TNLean.PEPS.pairConjugacyClassKroneckerOperator,
        TNLean.PEPS.pairConjugacyClassKroneckerOperator_pairConjugacyClass,
        TNLean.PEPS.linearIndependent_pairConjugacyClassKroneckerOperator}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_pair_conjugacy, def:peps_semiregular, def:peps_delta_operator}
    Let $U$ be a semi-regular representation of a finite group $G$ on a
    finite-dimensional complex vector space $E$. Define
    \begin{align}
        \Omega_{g,h}
         & =\sum_{x\in G}U_{xhx^{-1}}\otimes U_{xgx^{-1}},
        \label{eq:peps_pair_operator_sum}                  \\
        \Phi_{g,h}(A\otimes B)
         & =\tr[U_{h^{-1}}A\Delta]\,
        \tr[U_{g^{-1}}B\Delta].
        \label{eq:peps_pair_operator_dual}
    \end{align}
    The operator $\Omega_{g,h}$ depends only on the simultaneous-conjugacy
    class $C$ of $(g,h)$; denote it by $\Omega_C$. The family
    $(\Omega_C)_C$ is linearly independent. The detecting functionals satisfy
    \begin{align}
        \Phi_{g,h}(\Omega_{g',h'})
         & =\bigl|\{x\in G:
        g=xg'x^{-1},\ h=xh'x^{-1}\}\bigr|,
        \label{eq:peps_pair_operator_count} \\
        \Phi_{g,h}(\Omega_{g,h})
         & =|Z_G(g)\cap Z_G(h)|>0.
        \label{eq:peps_pair_operator_diagonal}
    \end{align}
    In a coordinate basis, the corresponding matrices
    $\sum_x U_{xhx^{-1}}\otimes_{\mathrm K}U_{xgx^{-1}}$ are likewise
    well-defined on classes and linearly independent. Here
    $\otimes_{\mathrm K}$ denotes the Kronecker product.
    Restriction to commuting pair classes gives the operator-independence
    argument in \cite[Theorem~5.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{def:peps_pair_conjugacy, thm:peps_semiregular_trace}
    Replacing $(g,h)$ by $(ygy^{-1},yhy^{-1})$ in
    (\ref{eq:peps_pair_operator_sum}) and substituting $x\mapsto xy$
    leaves the sum unchanged. By Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_semiregular_trace},
    each factor of (\ref{eq:peps_pair_operator_dual}) evaluates to a
    Kronecker delta on a representation operator, giving
    (\ref{eq:peps_pair_operator_count}). Its value is zero on distinct
    classes, while (\ref{eq:peps_pair_operator_diagonal}) is nonzero
    because the common centralizer contains the identity. Applying
    $\Phi_{g,h}$ to a finite relation $\sum_C\lambda_C\Omega_C=0$
    therefore gives $\lambda_C|Z_G(g)\cap Z_G(h)|=0$ for the class of
    $(g,h)$, and hence every coefficient vanishes. Finally, the canonical
    isomorphism
    $\operatorname{End}(E)\otimes\operatorname{End}(E)
        \cong\operatorname{End}(E\otimes E)$, followed by passage to the
    tensor-product coordinate basis, takes simple tensors to Kronecker
    matrices and preserves linear independence.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Products of physical linear maps]%
    \label{def:peps_physical_product_map}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.physicalMapSite,
        TNLean.PEPS.physicalProductMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.physicalProductMap,
        TNLean.PEPS.physicalProductMap_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusPhysicalMap,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusPhysicalMap_apply}
    \leanok
    Let $F$ be a rectangular physical matrix. Its product over a finite
    site set $\Lambda$ has coefficients
    \begin{align}
        (F^{\otimes\Lambda})_{\tau,\sigma}
        =\prod_{v\in\Lambda}F_{\tau(v),\sigma(v)}.
    \end{align}
    The transformed site tensor is
    $(FA)^q_{t,r,b,l}=\sum_s F_{q,s}A^s_{t,r,b,l}$.
    Input and output physical dimensions may differ.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Physical maps commute with torus contraction]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_physical_map_contraction}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.siteMap_physicalMapSite,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusPhysicalMap_torusBondNetwork,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusPhysicalMap_torusGClosure}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_physical_product_map, def:peps_torus_g_closure}
    Applying $F$ at every physical site composes the local site map
    with $F$ and preserves every virtual bond operator. In particular,
    \begin{align}
        F^{\otimes\Lambda}\ket{\mathcal M(A\mid g,h)}
        =\ket{\mathcal M(FA\mid g,h)}.
    \end{align}
    The identity holds for arbitrary torus bond operators, with no
    invariance or injectivity hypothesis. It is the physical inverse
    operation used in \cite[Theorem~5.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Expand the physical product map and the actual virtual contraction.
    Interchange the finite sums and factor the physical sum over sites.
    Each site coefficient is replaced by $\sum_s F_{q,s}A^s$ while
    the virtual bond coefficients are unchanged.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Physical product maps and Schmidt rank]%
    \label{thm:peps_physical_product_cut_rank}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.physicalProductMatrix_split,
        TNLean.PEPS.physicalCutMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.physicalCutMatrix_physicalProductMap,
        TNLean.PEPS.rank_physicalCutMatrix_physicalProductMap_le,
        TNLean.PEPS.rank_physicalCutMatrix_eq_of_physicalProductMaps}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_physical_product_map}
    For a partition $\Lambda=R\sqcup R^c$, let $C_R(\psi)$ be the
    coefficient matrix of a physical vector in the two sets of
    configuration coordinates. Then
    \begin{align}
        C_R(F^{\otimes\Lambda}\psi)
        =F^{\otimes R}C_R(\psi)(F^{\otimes R^c})^{\mathsf T}.
    \end{align}
    Consequently its rank cannot increase. If physical product maps
    send $\psi$ to $\phi$ and $\phi$ back to $\psi$, their Schmidt
    ranks agree across every partition. Neither product map need be
    invertible on the full physical space. This is the rank argument
    used in \cite[Corollary~6.10]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Split a physical configuration into its restrictions to $R$ and
    $R^c$. The product coefficient factors over these restrictions,
    and the resulting two physical sums give the two matrix products.
    Matrix multiplication cannot increase rank. Apply this inequality
    to the forward and reverse actions.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Recovering site coefficients from physical composition]%
    \label{thm:peps_site_coefficients_composition}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.siteMap_injective,
        TNLean.PEPS.physicalMapSite_eq_iff_siteMap_comp}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_g_injective, def:peps_physical_product_map}
    The four-leg coefficients are uniquely determined by the site map.
    For a physical matrix $F$ and tensors $A,B$, the coefficient identity
    $FA=B$ is equivalent to $F\mathcal P(A)=\mathcal P(B)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    The entry of $\mathcal P(A)$ in physical row $s$ and virtual column
    $(t,r,b,l)$ is $A^s_{t,r,b,l}$. Composition by $F$ gives exactly
    $\sum_sF_{q,s}A^s_{t,r,b,l}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:peps_torus_nonseam_connectivity}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.IsTorusNonseamCompatible.eq_origin,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsTorusNonseamCompatible}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_torus_regular_coordinates}
    Let $q:\mathbb Z_w\times\mathbb Z_h\to X$ be labels in any set $X$, where $w,h>0$.
    Suppose that $q_{x+1,y}=q_{x,y}$ whenever $x+1\ne0$, and
    $q_{x,y}=q_{x,y+1}$ whenever $y+1\ne0$. Then $q_{x,y}=q_{0,0}$ at every vertex.
    This is the synchronization away from the closure seams used in
    \cite[Theorem~5.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    Induct along the representatives $0,\ldots,w-1$ in the horizontal direction,
    then along $0,\ldots,h-1$ in the vertical direction. Neither induction crosses
    a closure seam. The same argument includes a circumference of one.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:peps_torus_projector_expansion}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.torusBondNetwork_single,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusBondSiteLabelsEquiv,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusBondNetwork_sum,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusBondNetwork_mul,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusDress_single,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusBondNetwork_averagingSite}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_torus_regular_coordinates, def:peps_regular_averaging_site}
    Let $U$ be a finite-dimensional representation of a finite group $G$, and let
    $\Pi$ be the four-leg averaging projector. Write $e(v)$ and $n(v)$ for the
    neighbours east and north of $v$, and let $s_v=(t_v,r_v,b_v,l_v)$ denote the
    physical basis label of the projector site. For arbitrary bond operators
    $O^H_v,O^V_v$, set
    \begin{align}
        H_v(q)=U_{q_{e(v)}}O^H_vU_{q_v^{-1}},\qquad
        V_v(q)=U_{q_v}O^V_vU_{q_{n(v)}^{-1}}.
        \label{eq:peps_torus_projector_bond_operators}
    \end{align}
    The actual torus contraction of the projector sites has coefficient
    \begin{align}
        \Psi_\Pi(s)=|G|^{-N}\sum_{q:\mathbb Z_w\times\mathbb Z_h\to G}
        \prod_v H_v(q)_{l_{e(v)},r_v}\,V_v(q)_{t_v,b_{n(v)}},
        \qquad N=wh.
    \end{align}
    No semi-regularity or unitarity assumption is required for this identity.
    It is the expansion of the projector contraction in
    \cite[Theorem~5.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{lem:peps_torus_bond_network_gauge}
    The bond-end indices are in bijection with the four indices read at the sites.
    For identity sites, the Kronecker deltas leave the product of the bond entries.
    Expand the group average independently at every vertex. Finite distributivity
    gives a sum over vertex labels and the factor $|G|^{-N}$. Absorbing each
    four-leg operator into the neighbouring bonds gives $H_v(q)$ and $V_v(q)$ as in
    (\ref{eq:peps_torus_projector_bond_operators}).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Extracting the torus closure operators]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_projector_extraction}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.matrixLinearFunctional_eq_sum,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusOutputBondPairing,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusOutputBondPairing_bondProduct,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusDeltaPairing,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusSeamPairing,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusDeltaPairing_apply_rep,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusSeamPairing_apply_rep,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusMatrixEntry,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusHorizontalExtractionPairing,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusVerticalExtractionPairing,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusProjectorExtraction,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusProjectorExtraction_bondProduct,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusDeltaPairing_apply_mul_inv,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusHorizontalExtractionPairing_constant,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusVerticalExtractionPairing_constant,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusProjectorExtraction_constant_bondProduct,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusHorizontalExtractionPairing_nonseam,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusVerticalExtractionPairing_nonseam,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusProjectorExtraction_label_bondProduct,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusProjectorExtraction_sum_label_bondProduct,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusProjectorExtraction_torusGClosure,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusProjectorExtraction_normalization_pos}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_semiregular, def:peps_regular_averaging_site,
        def:peps_torus_g_closure, def:peps_delta_operator}
    Let $U$ be a semi-regular representation of a finite group $G$ on
    a virtual space with finite basis $V$. Let $\Lambda$ be a rectangular
    torus with positive width and height, and let $A_0$ be the averaging-
    projector site. There is one explicit linear map
    $E:\C^{\Lambda\to V^4}\to\operatorname{Mat}_{V\times V}(\C)$,
    independent of $g,h$, such that
    \begin{align}
        E\ket{\mathcal M(A_0\mid g,h)}
        =|G|^{-|\Lambda|}\sum_{x\in G}
        U_{xhx^{-1}}\otimes U_{xgx^{-1}}.
        \label{eq:peps_projector_extraction}
    \end{align}
    Here the tensor product is written in the product basis, with the
    horizontal factor first. The coefficient $|G|^{-|\Lambda|}$ is
    strictly positive. This is an explicit contraction realizing the
    independence argument of \cite[Theorem~5.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_semiregular_trace, thm:peps_torus_projector_expansion,
        thm:peps_torus_nonseam_connectivity}
    Put $\delta_p(M)=\tr(U_{p^{-1}}M\Delta)$ and
    $\phi=\sum_p\delta_p$. Trace duality gives
    $\delta_p(U_z)=\delta_{p,z}$ and $\phi(U_z)=1$.
    Retain the horizontal seam bond $(-1,0)$ and the vertical seam bond
    $(0,-1)$. Apply $\delta_1$ to every nonseam output bond and $\phi$
    to every other seam output bond. On each retained bond use a matrix
    entry functional. Explicitly, for fixed rows $(i,k)$ and columns
    $(j,l)$, denote these horizontal and vertical functionals by
    $\kappa^h_v$ and $\kappa^v_v$, respectively. If $E_{a,b}$ is a
    matrix unit and $\beta$ assigns four end labels to each pair of bonds,
    define
    \begin{align}
        (Ef)_{(i,k),(j,l)}
        =\sum_\beta f(\mathcal A(\beta))\prod_{v\in\Lambda}
        \kappa^h_v(E_{\beta_v^{hH},\beta_v^{hT}})
        \kappa^v_v(E_{\beta_v^{vH},\beta_v^{vT}}),
    \end{align}
    where $\mathcal A(\beta)$ is the four-leg configuration read from
    the bond ends, in top, right, bottom, left order. Finite distributivity
    evaluates this contraction separately on each bond.
    For a vertex labelling $q$, a nonseam horizontal bond gives
    $\delta_1(U_{q_{v+e_1}q_v^{-1}})=\delta_{q_{v+e_1},q_v}$;
    a nonseam vertical bond gives
    $\delta_1(U_{q_vq_{v+e_2}^{-1}})=\delta_{q_v,q_{v+e_2}}$.
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_torus_nonseam_connectivity} forces $q_v=x$
    at every vertex. All discarded seam factors then equal one, and
    the retained factors are $U_{xhx^{-1}}$ and $U_{xgx^{-1}}$.
    The normalized projector expansion of
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_torus_projector_expansion} supplies the coefficient
    $|G|^{-|\Lambda|}$, giving~(\ref{eq:peps_projector_extraction}).
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The averaging-projector site]%
    \label{def:peps_regular_averaging_site}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.averagingSite,
        TNLean.PEPS.siteMap_averagingSite,
        TNLean.PEPS.averagingSite_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.averagingSite_invariant,
        TNLean.PEPS.isGInjective_averagingSite}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_g_injective}
    Let $U$ be any representation of a finite group $G$ on a virtual
    space with finite basis $V$, and write $R_q$ for its four-leg
    representation. Take the physical basis to be $V^4$ and define $A_0$ by
    \begin{align}
        (A_0)^s_{t,r,b,l}
                        & =\frac1{|G|}\sum_{q\in G}(R_q)_{s,(t,r,b,l)}, \\
        \mathcal P(A_0) & =\Pi=\frac1{|G|}\sum_{q\in G}R_q.
        \label{eq:peps_averaging_site}
    \end{align}
    Then $\Pi R_q=\Pi$ and $\Pi$ is the identity on invariant vectors,
    so $A_0$ is $G$-injective. Unitarity is not required for these
    statements \cite[Definition~5.1]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Isometry of the regular projector site]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_averaging_site_isometry}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.isGIsometric_regularAveragingSite}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_regular_averaging_site, def:peps_torus_regular_coordinates}
    For four regular virtual legs, $A_0$ is $G$-isometric with
    normalization factor one.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    The unitary group average is self-adjoint and idempotent, so
    $\Pi^\dagger\Pi=\Pi$. Its invariance was established in
    Definition~\ref{def:peps_regular_averaging_site}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Actual closure vectors and the local inverse]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_g_injective_closure_inverse}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.IsGInjective.exists_torusGClosure_averagingSite,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusPhysicalMap_averagingSite}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_regular_averaging_site}
    Let $A$ be $G$-injective for any finite virtual representation $U$
    and write $T=\mathcal P(A)$. There is a local rectangular matrix
    $L$ with $LT=\Pi$ such that, for every closure pair,
    \begin{align}
        L^{\otimes\Lambda}\ket{\mathcal M(A\mid g,h)}
         & =\ket{\mathcal M(A_0\mid g,h)},
        \label{eq:peps_projector_closure_forward} \\
        T^{\otimes\Lambda}\ket{\mathcal M(A_0\mid g,h)}
         & =\ket{\mathcal M(A\mid g,h)}.
        \label{eq:peps_projector_closure_reverse}
    \end{align}
    The reverse identity requires only virtual invariance of $A$.
    These are actual contraction identities for the inverse argument
    of \cite[Theorem~5.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}; no unitarity or local
    isometry hypothesis is needed.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_site_coefficients_composition, thm:peps_g_injective_left_inverse, thm:peps_torus_physical_map_contraction}
    The local inverse and virtual invariance give $LT=\Pi$ and
    $T\Pi=T$. By Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_site_coefficients_composition},
    these are coefficient identities. Apply
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_torus_physical_map_contraction} at every
    physical site in both directions. No inverse on the entire
    physical space is required.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Independent closures of regular $G$-injective tensors]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_g_injective_closure_independence}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.IsGInjective.torusGClosure_ne_zero,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGInjective.linearIndependent_torusGClosureClass,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGInjective.linearIndependent_torusGClosureClass_commuting}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_torus_regular_coordinates,
        thm:peps_semiregular_g_injective_closure_independence}
    For a regular $G$-injective tensor on any nonempty rectangular torus,
    every actual closure vector is nonzero and the vectors indexed by
    distinct pair-conjugacy classes are linearly independent. In
    particular the family indexed by commuting classes is independent.
    The latter is the regular-representation independence component of
    \cite[Theorem~5.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}, with no isometry hypothesis.
    Independence for all pair classes is an auxiliary extension and
    makes no ground-space assertion. The semi-regular representation result appears in
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_semiregular_g_injective_closure_independence};
    ground-space identification remains separate
    \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{def:peps_torus_regular_coordinates,
        thm:peps_semiregular_g_injective_closure_independence}
    The native regular representation is semi-regular by its group-basis
    equivalence with the group-algebra regular representation. Apply
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_semiregular_g_injective_closure_independence}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Schmidt ranks of regular $G$-injective closure superpositions]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_g_injective_closure_rank_transport}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.IsGInjective.rank_physicalCutMatrix_torusGClosure_sum}
    \leanok
    Let $A$ be regular $G$-injective. For every finite family of closure
    pairs and every physical partition $R$, the two vectors
    \begin{align}
        \sum_s\lambda_s\ket{\mathcal M(A\mid g_s,h_s)},\qquad
        \sum_s\lambda_s\ket{\mathcal M(A_0\mid g_s,h_s)}
        \label{eq:peps_closure_superposition_pair}
    \end{align}
    have the same Schmidt rank across $R$. This is the actual local
    deformation step of \cite[Corollary~6.10]{Schuch2010PEPS}; no
    particular boundary-rank formula is asserted here.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_g_injective_closure_inverse,
        thm:peps_physical_product_cut_rank}
    The same forward and reverse physical product maps act on every
    summand. By linearity they recover the superpositions
    (\ref{eq:peps_closure_superposition_pair}) from each other. Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_physical_product_cut_rank}
    gives equality of their coefficient-matrix ranks.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Independent closures of semi-regular $G$-injective tensors]%
    \label{thm:peps_semiregular_g_injective_closure_independence}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.IsGInjective.linearIndependent_torusGClosureClass_of_isSemiRegular,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGInjective.torusGClosure_ne_zero_of_isSemiRegular,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGInjective.linearIndependent_torusGClosureClass_commuting_of_isSemiRegular}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_semiregular}
    Let $U$ be a semi-regular representation of a finite group $G$,
    and let $A$ be $G$-injective for its four-leg virtual representation.
    On every nonempty rectangular torus, the actual closure vectors
    $\ket{\mathcal M(A\mid C)}$, indexed by simultaneous-conjugacy
    classes $C$ of pairs, are linearly independent. Every closure
    vector is therefore nonzero. In particular, the family indexed
    by commuting pair classes is independent.

    The commuting-family assertion proves the independence component
    of \cite[Theorem~5.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}, without a local isometry
    assumption. Independence for noncommuting classes and size-one
    tori are auxiliary extensions. No identification with the
    parent-Hamiltonian ground space is asserted
    \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_g_injective_closure_inverse, thm:peps_torus_projector_extraction, thm:peps_pair_conjugacy_operator_independence}
    Choose the same local inverse $L$ for every closure vector.
    With $N$ the number of vertices, the explicit extraction $E$ gives
    \begin{align}
        E L^{\otimes\Lambda}\ket{\mathcal M(A\mid C)}
         & =|G|^{-N}\Omega_C.
        \label{eq:peps_semiregular_closure_extraction}
    \end{align}
    Thus $|G|^N E L^{\otimes\Lambda}$ sends any proposed linear
    relation among the physical vectors to a relation among the
    independent operators $\Omega_C$. Its coefficients vanish by
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_pair_conjugacy_operator_independence}.
    Each vector is nonzero, and restriction to the commuting classes
    preserves independence.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Regular bonds with inserted group operators]%
    \label{def:peps_regular_twisted_region}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularTwistedLabels,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularTwistedSite,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularTwistedOpenRegionMatrix}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_regular_physical_cut}
    Order the endpoints of each bond $e$, writing them as $s(e)<t(e)$.
    For an assignment $u_e\in G$, the inserted regular matrix makes
    the label at $t(e)$ equal to $u_e$ times the label at $s(e)$.
    Equivalently, replace the coefficient at vertex $v$ by
    \begin{align}
        A^u_{v,s}(\eta)=A_{v,s}(\eta^u),\qquad
        \eta^u_e=
        \begin{cases}u_e\eta_e, & v=t(e), \\ \eta_e,&v=s(e).
        \end{cases}
    \end{align}
    Contracting the internal bonds with a fixed crossing configuration
    gives the actual open map $T_R^u$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[The canonical projector region with bond insertions]%
    \label{def:peps_regular_projector_twisted_region}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularProjectorTwistedRegionMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularProjectorTwistedRegionMatrix_one,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularProjectorTwistedRegionMatrix_apply}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_regular_twisted_region,
        def:peps_regular_projector_open_region, thm:peps_finite_indicator_sum}
    For arbitrary inserted group elements $u_e$, including those on
    crossing bonds, define
    \begin{align}
        B_R^u(\alpha,\theta)
         & =\sum_{\substack{\eta\in G^{E_R}                    \\
                \eta|_{\partial R}=\theta}}
        \prod_{v\in R}P_v(\alpha_v,\eta^u|_{E_v})              \\
         & =\kappa^{-|R|}\sum_{\eta\in G^{E_R}}\sum_{q\in G^R}
        \mathbf1_{\{\eta|_{\partial R}=\theta\}}
        \mathbf1_{\{\alpha_v=q_v\eta^u|_{E_v}\ (v\in R)\}}.
        \label{eq:peps_twisted_projector_region}
    \end{align}
    Here the half-edge modification $\eta^u$ is that of
    Definition~\ref{def:peps_regular_twisted_region}. Identity insertions
    recover $B_R$. This is the literal finite contraction of the local
    averaging-projector sites with the inserted regular operators.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Accessibility with arbitrary regular bond insertions]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_twisted_region_accessibility}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regionPhysicalMap_eq_regularProjectorTwistedRegionMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.regionPhysicalMap_regularProjectorTwistedRegionMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.exists_regularProjectorTwistedRegionMatrix_of_isGInjective,
        TNLean.PEPS.exists_regularProjectorTwistedRegionMatrix}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_regular_projector_twisted_region}
    Let the original regular site maps $A_v$ be $G$-injective, and
    choose local matrices $F_v$ satisfying $F_vA_v=P_v$. The same
    matrices work for every region and every assignment of inserted
    group elements:
    \begin{align}
        \left(\bigotimes_{v\in R}F_v\right)T_R^u(\theta)=B_R^u(\theta),
        \qquad
        \left(\bigotimes_{v\in R}A_v\right)B_R^u(\theta)=T_R^u(\theta).
    \end{align}
    If the sites are $G$-isometric with factors $c_v>0$, one may take
    $F_v=c_v^{-1}A_v^\dagger$. No connectedness or global Gram formula
    is required. These are the accessibility identities of
    \cite[Observation after Lemma~6.3]{Schuch2010PEPS}, extended
    algebraically to arbitrary bond insertions.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_open_region_accessibility,
        thm:peps_region_physical_maps}
    Apply the physical product map inside the actual contraction.
    The local identity $F_vA_v=P_v$ remains valid at the modified
    virtual label $\eta^u|_{E_v}$ and proves the forward equality.
    For the reverse equality, use $A_vP_v=A_v$ and hence
    $A_vF_vA_v=A_v$, again at the modified label. Expanding the local
    averages gives the finite-sum formula
    (\ref{eq:peps_twisted_projector_region}) for $B_R^u$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Expansion of a twisted-region cross Gram matrix]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_twisted_gram_expansion}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularOpenRegionMatrix_apply_group,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularOpenRegionMatrix_crossGram_apply_group,
        TNLean.PEPS.prod_twistedSiteGram_eq_sum_compatible,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularTwistedOpenRegionMatrix_crossGram_eq_sum_compatible}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_regular_twisted_region}
    Suppose the original site maps satisfy $A_v^\dagger A_v=c_v\Pi_v$.
    The entries of $(T_R^u)^\dagger T_R^w$ are obtained by summing over
    two internal-bond configurations and one translation $q_v$ per vertex.
    At each incident half-bond the constraint is
    \begin{align}
        \eta^u_{v,e}=q_v\theta^w_{v,e}.
    \end{align}
    The prescribed crossing configurations are retained, and the scalar
    from the local averages is $(\prod_{v\in R}c_v)|G|^{-|R|}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_site_gram}
    Expand both genuine open contractions and sum the physical indices
    independently. Substitute each original local Gram identity at its
    two transformed virtual configurations.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Synchronization through common identity bonds]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_twisted_synchronization}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regionLabels_eq_of_subgraph_internalEdge_eq,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsTwistedRegionLabelCompatible,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsTwistedRegionLabelCompatible.tail_eq,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsTwistedRegionLabelCompatible.head_eq,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsTwistedRegionLabelCompatible.internalEdge_intertwining,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsTwistedRegionLabelCompatible.exists_common_label,
        TNLean.PEPS.isTwistedRegionLabelCompatible_iff_exists_translation,
        TNLean.PEPS.sum_twistedRegionLabelCompatible_eq_sum_translation}
    \leanok
    Suppose the region has a connected spanning subgraph on whose bonds
    both assignments $u,w$ are the identity. Suppose also that both are
    the identity on crossing bonds. Then compatibility is equivalent to
    one common translation $x$, with
    \begin{align}
        q_v=x,\qquad \eta_e=x\theta_e,\qquad
        u_ex=xw_e\quad\text{for every internal bond }e.
    \end{align}
    Consequently the sum over compatible vertex translations is the
    corresponding restricted sum over $x\in G$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_twisted_gram_expansion}
    The tail constraint gives $\eta_e=q_{s(e)}\theta_e$; cancellation
    in the head constraint gives $u_eq_{s(e)}=q_{t(e)}w_e$.
    Along each common identity bond this equates the endpoint translations.
    Connectedness makes them constant. Boundary identity operators then
    give the common translation of every crossing label.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Counting with a restricted common translation]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_twisted_boundary_count}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regionBoundaryLabel_eq_iff,
        TNLean.PEPS.sum_boundaryRestriction_filtered_translations_eq_card,
        TNLean.PEPS.sum_regionBoundary_filtered_translations_eq_card}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_twisted_synchronization}
    Let $I_R$ count the internal bonds and let $F\subseteq G$ be any
    allowed set of common translations. With crossing configurations
    $\mu,\nu$, the number of translated pairs of bond assignments is
    \begin{align}
        |G|^{I_R}\sum_{x\in F}\mathbf{1}_{\mu=x\nu}.
    \end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    For fixed $x$, the second assignment determines the first. Its crossing
    labels are fixed; every internal label is free. The boundary conditions
    agree exactly when $\mu=x\nu$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Weighted translations on the invariant boundary]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_weighted_boundary_translation}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.toMatrix_regularBoundaryRepresentation_mul_projector,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryWeightedTranslation,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryWeightedTranslation_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryWeightedTranslation_mul_projector,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularBoundaryProjector_mul_weightedTranslation_mul_projector}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_regular_boundary_state}
    For weights $a_x\in\mathbb C$, let
    $W_a=\sum_x a_x L_x^{\otimes b}$. Its matrix entries are
    $(W_a)_{\mu,\nu}=\sum_x a_x\mathbf{1}_{\mu=x\nu}$.
    If $P$ is the invariant-boundary projector, then
    \begin{align}
        W_aP=\left(\sum_xa_x\right)P,
        \qquad PW_aP=\left(\sum_xa_x\right)P.
    \end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Every common translation fixes every vector in the range of $P$.
    Sum this identity with the specified weights and use $P^2=P$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Actual twisted-region cross Gram matrix]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_twisted_region_cross_gram}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.sum_regularBoundary_filtered_translation_eq_weightedTranslation,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularTwistedOpenRegionMatrix_crossGram_of_connected,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularTwistedOpenRegionMatrix_projected_crossGram_of_connected}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_twisted_synchronization, thm:peps_regular_weighted_boundary_translation}
    Under the hypotheses of
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_regular_twisted_synchronization}, put
    \begin{align}
        F_{u,w}=\{x\in G:u_ex=xw_e\text{ for every internal bond }e\},
        \qquad
        \kappa_R=\left(\prod_{v\in R}c_v\right)
        \frac{|G|^{I_R+1}}{|G|^{|R|}}.
    \end{align}
    The actual maps satisfy
    \begin{align}
        (T_R^u)^\dagger T_R^w
        =\frac{\kappa_R}{|G|}\sum_{x\in F_{u,w}}L_x^{\otimes b},
        \qquad
        P(T_R^u)^\dagger T_R^wP
        =\kappa_R\frac{|F_{u,w}|}{|G|}P.
    \end{align}
    These are auxiliary finite-region identities with identity operators
    on crossing bonds. The general torus cut of
    \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS} remains separate
    \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_twisted_boundary_count}
    Apply the expansion and synchronization lemmas. Counting the free
    internal labels contributes $|G|^{I_R}$, leaving the prefactor
    $\kappa_R/|G|$. On the invariant boundary each remaining translation
    acts identically, giving the projected formula.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Superpositions with a common boundary density]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_closure_superposition_density}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularClosureSuperpositionMap_conjTranspose_mul,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularClosureSuperpositionMap,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularClosureSuperpositionFactor,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularClosureSuperpositionMap_mul_projector,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularClosureSuperpositionState,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularClosureSuperpositionState_eq_sum,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularClosureSuperpositionState_eq_sum_of_gram,
        TNLean.PEPS.exists_normalization_regularClosureSuperpositionState}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_weighted_boundary_translation,
        thm:peps_regular_boundary_physical_realization}
    Let $b=n+1$, let $P$ be the regular boundary projector, and suppose
    specified cut maps satisfy
    \begin{align}
        A^\dagger A=c_AP,\quad c_A>0,\qquad
        PN_s^\dagger N_tP=k_{s,t}P.
    \end{align}
    For coefficients $\lambda_s$, put
    $B_\lambda=(\sum_s\lambda_sN_s)P$ and
    $K_\lambda=AB_\lambda^{\mathsf T}$. If $K_\lambda\ne0$, a positive
    scalar normalizes its coefficient vector, whose reduced density is
    \begin{align}
        \rho_A=
        \frac{1}{c_A|G|^n}APA^\dagger.
    \end{align}
    This density is independent of $\lambda$. It has rank $|G|^n$,
    satisfies $\rho_A^2=|G|^{-n}\rho_A$, and has entropy $n\log|G|$.
    This is a conditional matrix statement; identification of its Gram
    hypotheses with a PEPS contraction is separate.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Expanding the mixed Gram matrices gives
    \begin{align}
        B_\lambda^\dagger B_\lambda=\kappa_\lambda P,\qquad
        \kappa_\lambda=\sum_{s,t}\overline{\lambda_s}\lambda_t k_{s,t}.
    \end{align}
    Nonvanishing of $K_\lambda$ implies $B_\lambda\ne0$; positivity of
    its Gram matrix and its nonzero trace imply $\kappa_\lambda>0$.
    Normalize $A$ and $B_\lambda$ by their positive square-root factors
    and apply the physical boundary density calculation.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Common density for actual PEPS with complementary twists]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_twisted_cut_entropy}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.regularTwistedSite_eq_of_trivial_on_incident,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularTwistedOpenRegionMatrix_eq_of_trivial_on_incident,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularClosureSuperpositionState_eq_twistedCut_sum,
        TNLean.PEPS.regularTwistedPhysicalCutSuperposition,
        TNLean.PEPS.exists_normalization_regularTwistedPhysicalCutSuperposition}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_closure_superposition_density}
    Let every site tensor of a finite graph be regular $G$-isometric.
    Let $R$ be nonempty and connected, with $b=n+1$ crossing bonds.
    Consider a finite family of bond assignments $u^{(s)}$, all equal
    to the identity on edges incident to $R$. Suppose their common
    identity bonds contain a connected spanning subgraph of $R^c$.
    Let $\psi_s$ be the actual physical PEPS across the cut with
    assignment $u^{(s)}$.

    There is one $c_R>0$ such that the genuine untwisted region map
    satisfies $T_R^\dagger T_R=c_RP$. Every nonzero superposition
    $\sum_s\lambda_s\psi_s$ has a positive normalization whose reduced
    density on $R$ is the same operator
    \begin{align}
        \rho_R=\frac{1}{c_R|G|^n}T_RPT_R^\dagger.
    \end{align}
    In particular its rank is $|G|^n$, its nonzero spectrum is flat,
    and its entropy is $n\log|G|$.
    This is an auxiliary finite-cut statement with twists confined to
    the complement, rather than the arbitrary torus cut of
    \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}
    \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_twisted_region_cross_gram, thm:peps_open_region_cut}
    Identity operators on edges incident to $R$ leave its actual open
    map unchanged. The connected-region calculation derives its positive
    Gram factor from the local isometries. The twisted-region calculation
    derives each projected complementary mixed Gram matrix as a scalar
    multiple of $P$. Exact contraction across the cut identifies the
    actual superposition with the matrix state of
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_regular_closure_superposition_density}.
    Apply that theorem; no Gram identity for a whole region is assumed.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The rectangular grid obtained by cutting the torus seams]%
    \label{def:peps_torus_rectangular_grid}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.torusNonseamGraph,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusNonseamGraph_le_torusGraph}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_torus_geometry_translation}
    On the torus vertices, retain only consecutive coordinate values
    without wraparound. The result is the ordinary rectangular grid,
    a subgraph of the periodic square lattice.
\end{definition}

\input{chapter/ch24_peps_regular_ginjective_simply_connected_entropy}

\begin{theorem}[Connectivity of rectangles and their complements]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_rectangle_cut_connectivity}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.torusNonseamGraph_compl_rectangle_connected,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusGraph_compl_rectangle_connected,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusNonseamGraph_rectangle_connected,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusGraph_rectangle_connected,
        TNLean.PEPS.nonempty_boundaryEdge_torusRectangle}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_torus_rectangular_grid}
    A bounded coordinate rectangle with positive side lengths is connected
    in the ordinary grid, hence also in the periodic lattice.
    If both side lengths are strictly smaller than the torus dimensions,
    its complement is connected even without the wrap bonds. This last
    assertion also holds for coordinate intervals clipped by the bounds
    of the grid. A nonempty bounded rectangle starting at a positive
    horizontal coordinate has a crossing bond on its left side.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Inside the rectangle, change the two coordinates consecutively.
    For the complement, choose one row and one column missed by the
    rectangle. Every outside vertex can reach their intersection along
    an outside row or column. A vertex at the lower left corner and
    its immediate left neighbour give the crossing bond.
\end{proof}

\input{chapter/ch24_peps_rectangle_realization}

\input{chapter/ch24_peps_stripe_local_equivalence}

\begin{theorem}[Closure seams avoid a strictly interior rectangle]%
    \label{thm:peps_torus_interior_rectangle_seam_exclusion}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.torusNonseamGraph_not_horizontal_wrap,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusNonseamGraph_not_vertical_wrap,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusRightEdge_not_incident_rectangle_of_wrap,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusUpEdge_not_incident_rectangle_of_wrap}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_torus_rectangular_grid}
    Suppose both circumferences are at least three. Wrap bonds are absent
    from the ordinary rectangular grid. If a rectangle lies strictly
    between the first and last coordinate values in both directions,
    no wrap bond is incident to it.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_torus_rectangle_cut_connectivity}
    A wrap bond has coordinate values $0$ and $w-1$, or $0$ and $\ell-1$.
    Their difference cannot be one for a circumference at least three,
    and neither endpoint can lie in the stated interior rectangle.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The actual torus closure as a twisted graph contraction]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_torus_closure_cut_identification}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.torusClosureEdgeAssignment_right,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusIncidentSite,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusClosureEdgeAssignment,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusClosureEdgeAssignment_up,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusRightEdge_head_of_wrap,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusUpEdge_head_of_wrap,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusRegularBondConfigEquiv,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusClosureBondConfigEquiv,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusClosureBondConfigEquiv_right,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusClosureBondConfigEquiv_up,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusClosureTwistedLabels_top,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusClosureTwistedLabels_right,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusClosureTwistedLabels_down,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusClosureTwistedLabels_left,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusGClosure_eq_stateCoeff_twisted,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusGClosure_assembleRegion_eq_regularPhysicalCutMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusGClosure_cut_eq_regularPhysicalCutState}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_torus_g_closure, def:peps_regular_twisted_region}
    On a torus of width and height at least three, the four incident
    graph edges are exactly the top, right, down and left virtual legs.
    The actual closure vector $\psi_{g,h}$ equals the contraction of
    the graph tensor with ordered-head assignment
    \begin{align}
        u_e=
        \begin{cases}
            h^{-1}, & e\text{ is a horizontal wrap bond}, \\
            g,      & e\text{ is a vertical wrap bond},   \\
            1,      & \text{otherwise}.
        \end{cases}
    \end{align}
    Its coefficient vector across any region cut is the actual twisted
    physical cut state. No commutativity or isometry assumption is
    required for these contraction identities. The circumference
    restriction ensures that the simple graph distinguishes all four
    incident virtual legs \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Enumerate the graph bonds by horizontal and vertical torus bonds.
    At a horizontal wrap bond, the ordered head is the native tail;
    replace its summed label by $h$ times the native tail label.
    The inserted $h^{-1}$ then gives the original native tail, while
    the other endpoint gives the native head. At a vertical wrap bond,
    the ordered head is the native head, so the assignment is $g$.
    All four site labels now coincide with those of the native closure
    contraction. The change of labels is bijective. Read the resulting
    identity in the two sets of physical cut coordinates.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Regular isometry in incident-edge coordinates]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_torus_incident_isometry}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.torusIncidentCoordinates_injective,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusIncidentCoordinates,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.exists_torusIncidentSiteGram,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsGIsometric.isGIsometric_torusIncidentSite,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusClosureEdgeAssignment_eq_one_of_nonseam,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusClosureEdgeAssignment_eq_one_on_rectangle_incident}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_regular_torus_closure_cut_identification}
    Suppose both torus dimensions are at least three. A tensor which is
    $G$-isometric for the four-leg regular action remains $G$-isometric
    when its virtual coordinates are indexed by incident graph edges.
    Every closure assignment is the identity on the ordinary grid and
    on every edge incident to a strictly interior rectangle.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_torus_interior_rectangle_seam_exclusion}
    The four incident-edge labels determine the four virtual coordinates
    bijectively and intertwine simultaneous left multiplication. Thus
    their group-average Gram matrices agree. The claims about assignments
    follow from the exclusion of wrap bonds.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Entropy of closure superpositions on interior rectangles]%
    \label{thm:peps_regular_torus_interior_rectangle_entropy}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.torusClosureSuperpositionCut_eq_twistedCutSuperposition,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusClosureSuperpositionCut,
        TNLean.PEPS.exists_normalization_torusClosureSuperpositionCut_rectangle}
    \leanok
    Let $A$ be regular $G$-isometric on a torus of width and height at
    least three. Let $R$ be a coordinate rectangle of positive side
    lengths satisfying
    \begin{align}
        0<x_0,\quad x_0+x_1<w,\qquad
        0<y_0,\quad y_0+y_1<\ell.
    \end{align}
    Write $b$ for its number of crossing bonds and $P$ for the invariant
    boundary projector. There is one $c_R>0$ with
    $T_R^\dagger T_R=c_RP$. For any finite family of closure pairs
    $(g_s,h_s)$ and coefficients $\lambda_s$ such that
    $\sum_s\lambda_s\psi_{g_s,h_s}\ne0$, a positive normalization of this
    actual torus vector has reduced density on $R$
    \begin{align}
        \rho_R=\frac{1}{c_R|G|^{b-1}}T_RPT_R^\dagger.
    \end{align}
    Hence the normalized reduced density is independent of the
    superposition. It has rank $|G|^{b-1}$ and flat nonzero spectrum,
    and its von Neumann entropy is $(b-1)\log|G|$.
    This proves the strictly interior rectangular case of the entropy
    calculation in \cite[Theorem~6.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}. The present case
    also permits noncommuting closure pairs. The source's full statement
    concerns commuting sectors and arbitrary topologically trivial
    regions \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_torus_rectangle_cut_connectivity,
        thm:peps_regular_twisted_cut_entropy,
        thm:peps_regular_torus_incident_isometry}
    The coordinate bounds give connected $R$, a nonempty boundary, and
    connected $R^c$ after removing all wrap bonds. The closure assignments
    are the identity on this complementary grid and on all edges incident
    to $R$. Transport the local regular isometry to incident-edge
    coordinates, identify the native closure superposition with its
    twisted graph contraction, and apply
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_regular_twisted_cut_entropy}. Nonvanishing gives
    the positive normalization; the same region map and constant apply
    to every choice of coefficients.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Pair conjugacy with a fixed first element]%
    \label{thm:peps_pair_conjugacy_centralizer}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.pairConjugacyClass_eq_iff_centralizer}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_pair_conjugacy}
    Fix $g\in G$. The pairs $(g,h)$ and $(g,k)$ have the same class
    exactly when $h=xkx^{-1}$ for some $x$ in the centralizer
    $Z_G(g)=\{x\in G:xg=gx\}$.
    This is the fixed-first-element part of the classification following
    \cite[Theorem~5.9]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    The simultaneous-conjugacy equations are $g=xgx^{-1}$ and
    $h=xkx^{-1}$; the first is equivalent to $xg=gx$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Closure labels for an abelian group]%
    \label{thm:peps_abelian_pair_classes}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.pairConjugacyClass_eq_iff_of_commGroup,
        TNLean.PEPS.pairConjugacyClassEquivOfCommGroup,
        TNLean.PEPS.commutingPairConjugacyClassEquivOfCommGroup,
        TNLean.PEPS.card_commutingPairConjugacyClass_of_commGroup}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_pair_conjugacy}
    If $G$ is abelian, every pair-conjugacy class is a singleton and all
    pairs commute. A finite abelian group therefore has $|G|^2$
    commuting pair-conjugacy classes.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Conjugation is trivial, so the class map identifies the classes with
    $G\times G$; counting pairs gives $|G|^2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Dual quantum-double tensor]%
    \label{def:peps_quantum_double_dual}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.colorDiag, TNLean.PEPS.colorDiag_one,
        TNLean.PEPS.colorDiag_mul, TNLean.PEPS.quantumDoubleDualSpins,
        TNLean.PEPS.quantumDoubleDualTensor, TNLean.PEPS.colorShiftRep,
        TNLean.PEPS.colorShiftRep_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.mem_invariants_colorShiftRep_iff}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_g_injective}
    The virtual legs carry colors $h_1$ (left), $h_2$ (top), $h_3$ (right)
    and $h_4$ (down) in $G$, and the dual tensor is $1$ at the physical
    configuration
    $(h_2h_1^{-1},\,h_3h_2^{-1},\,h_3h_4^{-1},\,h_4h_1^{-1})$ of differences
    of adjacent colors and $0$ elsewhere \cite[Appendix~A, ``The Toric Code
        and quantum double models'']{Cirac2021Matrix}. The group acts on the
    virtual system by shifting all four colors,
    $(U_gx)(h_1,h_2,h_3,h_4)=x(h_1g,h_2g,h_3g,h_4g)$, the right-regular
    representation on each leg, which the relabeling $h\mapsto h^{-1}$
    turns into the left-regular one
    \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[The dual tensor is $G$-isometric]%
    \label{thm:peps_quantum_double_dual_g_isometric}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.quantumDoubleDualSpins_mul_colorDiag,
        TNLean.PEPS.quantumDoubleDualSpins_eq_iff,
        TNLean.PEPS.siteMap_quantumDoubleDualTensor_apply,
        TNLean.PEPS.siteMap_quantumDoubleDualTensor_apply_spins,
        TNLean.PEPS.siteMap_quantumDoubleDualTensor_apply_spins_of_mem_invariants,
        TNLean.PEPS.isGInjective_quantumDoubleDualTensor,
        TNLean.PEPS.isGIsometric_quantumDoubleDualTensor,
        TNLean.PEPS.ToricCodeGroup,
        TNLean.PEPS.isGIsometric_toricCodeDualTensor}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_g_injective, def:peps_quantum_double_dual}
    For every finite group $G$ the dual quantum-double tensor is
    $G$-injective and $G$-isometric, with constant $c=|G|$, for the
    color-shift representation
    \cite[Appendix~A, ``The Toric Code and quantum double
        models'']{Cirac2021Matrix},
    \cite[Section~``Examples'', ``The double models'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.
    In particular the toric code tensor, $G=\mathbb{Z}_2$, is
    $\mathbb{Z}_2$-isometric.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    Two colorings have the same differences exactly when they differ by one
    global shift $h_i\mapsto h_ig$: the shift of the left color determines
    $g$, and the other three differences then fix the remaining colors.
    Hence $(\mathcal{P}x)(\text{differences of }h)=\sum_{g}x(hg)$. Invariance
    follows by reindexing the sum over colorings by a shift. For an
    invariant $x$ the sum equals $|G|\,x(h)$, so $\mathcal{P}x=0$ forces
    $x=0$, and
    $\langle\mathcal{P}x,\mathcal{P}y\rangle
        =\sum_h\overline{(\mathcal{P}x)(\text{differences of }h)}\,y(h)
        =|G|\langle x,y\rangle$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Strings of $R_g$'s on the dual quantum-double network]%
    \label{cor:peps_quantum_double_dual_string_deformation}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.rightRegularMatrix,
        TNLean.PEPS.colorShiftRep_eq_torusLegRep,
        TNLean.PEPS.torusBondNetwork_quantumDoubleDual_westSouthOperatorString_eq}
    \leanok
    \uses{thm:peps_quantum_double_dual_g_isometric,
        thm:peps_torus_operator_string_deformation}
    Let $R_g$ be the permutation matrix of $h\mapsto hg$. The color shift of
    the dual tensor is the action of $R_g$ on the incoming and $R_g^{-1}$ on
    the outgoing legs. Hence, in the torus network of the dual tensor with
    colors in $G$ on the bonds, a string of $R_g$'s along the left and bottom
    sides of a rectangle of $m<w$ columns and $n<h$ rows of sites gives the
    same network as the string along its top and right sides
    \cite[Section~``Two dimensions: $G$-injective PEPS'',
        eq.~(2d:move-strings)]{Schuch2010PEPS}.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    On the incoming legs $R_g$ sends $x$ to $h\mapsto x(hg)$, and on the
    outgoing legs $R_g^{-1}$ acting along the edge does the same, so the
    action on the four legs is the color shift. The dual tensor is invariant
    under it by Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_quantum_double_dual_g_isometric}, and
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_torus_operator_string_deformation} applies.
\end{proof}

Placed at every site of the torus, the dual tensor gives a network whose
bonds carry colors in $G$ and whose physical spins are the differences of the
colors of adjacent bonds; its state is the superposition over all color
patterns \cite[Section~``Examples'', ``The double models'']{Schuch2010PEPS}.
The review identifies it with the equal-weight superposition of the
configurations obeying Gauss' law \cite[Appendix~A, ``The Toric Code and
    quantum double models'']{Cirac2021Matrix}. On the torus not every such
configuration is a difference of colorings: the transports of the spins
around the two non-contractible cycles must be trivial.

\begin{lemma}[Parallel sections of transports on the torus]%
    \label{lem:peps_torus_parallel_section}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.zmodTransport, TNLean.PEPS.zmodTransport_zero,
        TNLean.PEPS.zmodTransport_succ,
        TNLean.PEPS.apply_natCast_eq_zmodTransport_mul,
        TNLean.PEPS.zmodTransport_self_eq_one_of_apply_add_one,
        TNLean.PEPS.zmodTransport_val_add_one,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsTorusParallelSection, TNLean.PEPS.IsTorusFlat,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsTorusParallelSection.mul_right,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsTorusParallelSection.isTorusFlat,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsTorusParallelSection.zmodTransport_row,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsTorusParallelSection.zmodTransport_column,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsTorusFlat.zmodTransport_column_mul,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsTorusParallelSection.eq_mul_right,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsTorusFlat.zmodTransport_column_eq_one,
        TNLean.PEPS.exists_isTorusParallelSection_iff,
        TNLean.PEPS.card_isTorusParallelSection}
    \leanok
    \uses{lem:peps_torus_shift_const}
    Let $G$ be a finite group and assign to every site $v$ of the torus two
    elements $a_v,b_v\in G$. Call $f$ from the sites to $G$ a parallel section
    if $f_{v+(1,0)}=a_vf_v$ and $f_{v+(0,1)}=b_vf_v$ for every $v$. Parallel
    sections exist if and only if
    $b_{v+(1,0)}a_v=a_{v+(0,1)}b_v$ for every $v$ and the ordered products
    $a_{(w-1,0)}\cdots a_{(0,0)}$ and $b_{(0,h-1)}\cdots b_{(0,0)}$ are the
    identity. If they exist, there are exactly $|G|$ of them, and any two
    differ by right multiplication with one group element.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    If $f$ and $f'$ are parallel sections, $f_v^{-1}f'_v$ is invariant under
    both unit shifts and hence constant, so $f'=fg$; conversely $fg$ is a
    parallel section for every $g$. The two paths from $v$ to $v+(1,1)$ give
    the flatness condition, and going once around a row or a column gives
    the trivial products. Conversely, for flat $a,b$ with trivial products
    set $f_{(x,y)}=b_{(x,y-1)}\cdots b_{(x,0)}\,a_{(x-1,0)}\cdots a_{(0,0)}$
    for the representatives $0\le x<w$, $0\le y<h$. Flatness moves the
    product up a column one column to the right, conjugated by the
    horizontal transports at its ends, so the product around every column is
    trivial; this and the trivial product around the bottom row give the
    two transport rules also where the representatives wrap around.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The dual quantum-double network is the Gauss-law superposition]%
    \label{thm:peps_quantum_double_dual_gauss_law}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.quantumDoubleDualSiteTensor,
        TNLean.PEPS.quantumDoubleDualPEPS,
        TNLean.PEPS.quantumDoubleDualRowTransport,
        TNLean.PEPS.quantumDoubleDualColumnTransport,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsQuantumDoubleGaussLaw,
        TNLean.PEPS.IsQuantumDoubleTrivialHolonomy,
        TNLean.PEPS.isTorusFlat_quantumDoubleDualTransport_iff,
        TNLean.PEPS.forall_eq_quantumDoubleDualSpins_iff,
        TNLean.PEPS.stateCoeff_quantumDoubleDualPEPS}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_quantum_double_dual, lem:peps_torus_site_tensor_contraction,
        lem:peps_torus_parallel_section}
    Let $w,h\ge3$ and place the dual quantum-double tensor at every site of
    the torus. Write $(s_1,s_2,s_3,s_4)$ for the top left, top right, bottom
    right and bottom left spins of a site. A spin configuration obeys Gauss'
    law if at every site $s_2s_1=s_3s_4$, and around the corner shared by
    the sites $v$, $v+(1,0)$, $v+(1,1)$, $v+(0,1)$ the spins satisfy
    $s_1(v+(1,0))\,s_2(v)=s_4(v+(1,1))\,s_3(v+(0,1))$, the local and the
    plaquette terms of \cite[Section~``Examples'', ``The double
        models'']{Schuch2010PEPS}. It has trivial holonomy if the ordered
    products of $a_v=s_2(v)s_1(v)$ along the row $y=0$ and of
    $b_v=s_4(v+(0,1))^{-1}s_1(v)$ along the column $x=0$ are the identity.
    Then the coefficient of the contracted network is $|G|$ at every
    configuration obeying Gauss' law with trivial holonomy, and $0$ at every
    other configuration. So the state is the equal-weight superposition of
    the Gauss-law configurations of trivial holonomy; those of nontrivial
    holonomy do not occur on the torus
    \cite{gap:rmp_peps_quantum_double_g_isometry}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    By Lemma~\ref{lem:peps_torus_site_tensor_contraction} the coefficient
    counts the colorings of the horizontal and vertical bonds whose
    differences are the given spins. Write such a coloring through the left
    color $f_v$ and the top color $u_v$ of each site. The top left spin gives
    $u_v=s_1(v)f_v$, the top right spin gives $f_{v+(1,0)}=a_vf_v$, the
    bottom left spin of the site above gives $f_{v+(0,1)}=b_vf_v$, and the
    bottom right spin is then equivalent to $s_2s_1=s_3s_4$ at $v$. So the
    colorings are counted by the parallel sections of $a,b$ when the local
    rule holds, and there are none otherwise. Using the local rule,
    flatness of $a,b$ is the plaquette rule, and
    Lemma~\ref{lem:peps_torus_parallel_section} counts the sections.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Primal quantum-double tensor]%
    \label{def:peps_quantum_double_primal}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.quantumDoublePrimalLabels,
        TNLean.PEPS.quantumDoublePrimalTensor}
    \leanok
    A tensor carries the four edge spins $g_1,g_2,g_3,g_4$ of a plaquette (top
    left, top right, bottom right, bottom left), and is $1$ when its virtual
    labels are $g_1g_2$ (top), $g_2^{-1}g_3$ (right), $g_4g_3$ (down) and
    $g_1g_4^{-1}$ (left), and $0$ otherwise \cite[Appendix~A, ``The Toric Code
        and quantum double models'']{Cirac2021Matrix}.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[The primal virtual labels fuse to the identity]%
    \label{thm:peps_quantum_double_primal_fusion}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.quantumDoublePrimalTensor_ne_zero_mul_eq_one,
        TNLean.PEPS.quantumDoublePrimalTensor_ne_zero_map_mul_eq_one,
        TNLean.PEPS.quantumDoublePrimalTensor_smul_map_mul,
        TNLean.PEPS.character_mul_quantumDoublePrimalTensor}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_quantum_double_primal}
    On the support of the primal tensor the virtual labels satisfy
    $t\,r\,b^{-1}l^{-1}=1$. Hence $\pi(t)\pi(r)\pi(b)^{-1}\pi(l)^{-1}=1$ for
    every representation $\pi$ of $G$, in particular every irreducible one,
    so that each entry of the tensor times the operator
    $\pi(t)\pi(r)\pi(b)^{-1}\pi(l)^{-1}$, acting by $\pi$ on the top and
    right legs and by $\pi^{-1}$ on the down and left legs, equals the same
    entry times the identity. For a one-dimensional representation $\chi$
    the tensor is invariant under this virtual action
    \cite[Appendix~A, ``The Toric Code and quantum double
        models'']{Cirac2021Matrix}. This is a statement about one tensor; the
    resulting symmetry of the contracted network is
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_quantum_double_primal_network}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    $(g_1g_2)(g_2^{-1}g_3)(g_4g_3)^{-1}(g_1g_4^{-1})^{-1}
        =g_1g_3g_3^{-1}g_4^{-1}g_4g_1^{-1}=1$.
    Since $\pi$ is multiplicative,
    \begin{align}
        \pi(t)\pi(r)\pi(b)^{-1}\pi(l)^{-1}=\pi(t\,r\,b^{-1}l^{-1})=\pi(1)=1
    \end{align}
    on the support, which gives the operator identity entrywise. A
    one-dimensional representation is the case of a character $\chi$, where
    the identity reads $\chi(t)\chi(r)\chi(b)^{-1}\chi(l)^{-1}=1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Virtual symmetry of the primal quantum-double network]%
    \label{thm:peps_quantum_double_primal_network}
    \lean{TNLean.PEPS.quantumDoublePrimalTensor_ne_zero_mul_eq_mul,
        TNLean.PEPS.quantumDoublePrimalSiteTensor,
        TNLean.PEPS.quantumDoublePrimalPEPS,
        TNLean.PEPS.sum_smul_torusWestSouthString_quantumDoublePrimalPEPS_eq}
    \leanok
    \uses{def:peps_quantum_double_primal, lem:peps_torus_string_pull_through}
    Place the primal quantum-double tensor at every site of the torus, and
    let $\pi$ be a representation of $G$, in particular an irreducible one.
    The virtual string that multiplies $\pi(g)$ for each bond with label $g$
    it crosses can be pulled through every rectangle of sites of the
    contracted network: inserted along the left and bottom sides of the
    rectangle it gives the same network as inserted along its top and right
    sides \cite[Appendix~A, ``The Toric Code and quantum double
        models'']{Cirac2021Matrix}. Around one site the two paths give
    $\pi(l)\pi(b)$ and $\pi(t)\pi(r)$, so the closed string around a site is
    the operator $\pi(t)\pi(r)\pi(b)^{-1}\pi(l)^{-1}$ of
    Theorem~\ref{thm:peps_quantum_double_primal_fusion}. The statement holds
    on every torus of width and height at least two; the source's network,
    with four distinct bonds at each site, is the case of width and height
    at least three \cite{gap:rmp_peps_examples_small_torus}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}\leanok
    On the support of the primal tensor $t\,r\,b^{-1}l^{-1}=1$, so
    $tr=lb$ and $\pi(t)\pi(r)=\pi(l)\pi(b)$. This is the hypothesis of
    Lemma~\ref{lem:peps_torus_string_pull_through} for $X_g=\pi(g)$.
\end{proof}
